TITLE: psybersecurity

===== FILE 2/19: xhtml%2fhalftitle.xhtml =====

Psybersecurity

Psybersecurity: Human Factors of Cyber Defence is a clarion call to action in the face of a stark reality: over 90% of cyberattacks exploit human vulnerabilities, as highlighted by the 2022 Global Risks Report from the World Economic Forum. This gap between the rapid advancement of cybersecurity technologies and the slower pace of development in human-centric defences poses a formidable challenge to national security and personal safety. Amidst the dazzling progress of AI technologies like ChatGPT and Microsoft Security Co-Pilot, the human element of cybersecurity remains critically underdeveloped.

Set against the backdrop of the Australian government’s ambitious goal to become the world’s most cybersecure nation by 2030, this book embarks on a mission to address the overlooked human factors in cyber defence. It advocates for a balanced approach that not only relies on technological advancements but also significantly enhances the human aspects of cybersecurity.

Through an interdisciplinary exploration, Psybersecurity delves into how cyberthreats exploit human vulnerabilities and offers innovative solutions for building resilience against these vulnerabilities. It examines the necessity for cybersecurity strategies that encompass psychological insights, systemic resilience, and the mitigation of human errors, particularly within critical infrastructures and cyber-physical systems (CPS).

Furthermore, this work critiques existing cybersecurity education frameworks, proposing a comprehensive curriculum that equips individuals with technical skills and the behavioural competencies needed to navigate the cyber landscape ethically and effectively. It also addresses AI’s ethical dilemmas and psychological impacts, offering a forward-looking perspective on combating AI-driven harassment and endorsing a new field of study: ‘Psybersecurity’.

Psybersecurity: Human Factors of Cyber Defence aims to bridge the gap between cybersecurity and human sciences, ignite a transformation in understanding, and fortify our digital world. It is an essential read for academics, professionals, and anyone committed to building a safer, more resilient cyber future in alignment with Australia’s 2030 vision (Australian Government, 2023).

===== FILE 7/19: xhtml%2fprefacepage.xhtml =====

Preface

The World Economic Forum’s (2022) Global Risks Report starkly reveals that over 90% of cyberattacks prey on human vulnerabilities, highlighting a critical imbalance between the technological advancements in cybersecurity and the underdeveloped human factors integral to its defence. This imbalance in cybersecurity development is evident in the overabundance of human attack vectors. There has been an emphasis on developing hardware and software, while human factors in cybersecurity have not been given as much attention. As we marvel at the near-science-fiction capabilities of artificial intelligence technologies like ChatGPT and Microsoft Security Co-Pilot, it is evident that efforts to fortify human aspects of cybersecurity have not kept pace with hardware and software capabilities.

The Australian government has set an ambitious goal to establish Australia as the world’s most cybersecure nation by 2030. However, achieving this goal relies heavily on addressing the lack of emphasis on human factors in cybersecurity, which is exploited in most cyberattacks. This vulnerability poses a significant national security challenge and highlights the need to bridge the gap between cybersecurity and the human sciences. Psybersecurity: Human Factors of Cyber Defence endeavours to bridge the human factors gap in cybersecurity, thereby taking a pivotal step towards actualising the government’s vision for 2030.

This book takes a decisive step towards bridging the significant gap in cybersecurity’s human element, echoing the Australian government’s 2030 vision for unparalleled national cyber resilience. It presents a nuanced exploration into the myriad ways human vulnerabilities intersect with cyberthreats, advocating for a balanced focus on technological and human-centric defences.

By navigating the complexities of cybersecurity from an interdisciplinary lens, the book highlights the pressing need for robust strategies that address both the human psyche and systemic flaws. It introduces innovative solutions for enhancing systemic resilience and mitigating human errors within critical infrastructures and cyber-physical systems (CPS), emphasising the importance of psychological insights in securing digital landscapes.

Moreover, it scrutinizes current cybersecurity educational frameworks, arguing for a curriculum fostering technical proficiency and behavioural change. It ventures into the ethical and psychological implications of AI, proposing forward-thinking measures to safeguard against AI-driven harassment and to promote ‘Psybersecurity’ as a scholarly bridge between cybersecurity and the human sciences for academics and professionals alike.

===== FILE 8/19: xhtml%2fabouttheeditorspage.xhtml =====

About the editors

Oliver Guidetti is a postdoctoral researcher specialising in the human factors of cybersecurity. He has an extensive background working on applied socio-technical projects that examine the interplay between the human brain and technology. His PhD research focused on measuring and mitigating vigilance decrement in cyber network defence tasks. Guidetti has published numerous research papers on topics such as cyber vigilance tasks, the neuropsychology of network defence operators, human resource policy in relation to neurotechnology, and the effects of cognitive load on the performance of cyber first responders. Through his work, he hopes to contribute to our increasingly digital world’s socio-technical security and resilience.

Mohiuddin Ahmed is Senior Lecturer of Computing and Security Discipline at the School of Science, Edith Cowan University, Australia. He earned his PhD in computer science from the University of New South Wales in 2016. He is a member of the Association for Computing Machinery (ACM), the Australian Information Security Association (AISA), a senior member of the Institute of Electrical and Electronics Engineers (IEEE), a Certified Professional of the Australian Computer Society (ACS), and a Fellow of the Higher Education Academy, UK. He is also an ACM Distinguished Speaker and delivered several keynotes and invited talks on cybersecurity. He has been educating the next generation of cyber leaders and researching to disrupt the cybercrime ecosystem. His research is focused on ensuring national security and safeguarding critical infrastructures from cyber terrorists. He secured several external and internal grants worth more than AU$1.7 million and has been collaborating with academia and industry.

Craig Speelman has been Professor at the School of Psychology and Social Science, Edith Cowan University, since 2008. He was Head of the School during 2002–2011. Speelman conducts research in the general area of cognitive psychology, but much of his research has focused on the topic of skill acquisition and transfer. He is the author of Beyond the Learning Curve: The Construction of Mind and several book chapters. He has edited three research volumes and also authored papers published in highly ranked journals, including Journal of Experimental Psychology: LMC, Nature Scientific Reports, Frontiers in Psychology, The American Journal of Geriatric Psychiatry, and PLOS ONE. He has received funding from the Australian Research Council, Health Workforce Australia, and the Association of Independent Schools (AISWA). He has served on editorial boards of journals and was the editor-in-chief of the GSTF Journal of Psychology. He has served on the organising committees for national and international psychology conferences. Speelman has applied this expertise to developing a training programme for acquiring skin cancer detection skills, the development of basic arithmetic skills, where he produced the commercial app Numbeat, and psychological aspects of cybersecurity.

===== FILE 10/19: xhtml%2fch01.xhtml =====

Chapter 1 Integrating human factors and systemic resilience An interdisciplinary approach to cybersecurity in critical infrastructures and utilities

Avisen Moonsammy, Mohiuddin Ahmed, Oliver Guidetti, and Bazlur Rashid

DOI: 10.1201/9781032664859-1

1.1 Introduction: The Convergence of Disciplines in Cybersecurity

In the ever-evolving digital landscape, critical infrastructures and utilities face a myriad of cybersecurity threats. As Clark and Hakim (2017) identified, the challenge is no longer confined to the virtual realm; it extends to the physical world as cyber-physical systems become increasingly integral to critical operations. Despite the growing recognition of these cyber- physical threats (Cohen, 2010; Gunduz & Das, 2020), one area remains underexplored: the human element in cybersecurity. As Bates (2017) and Boyce et al. (2011) have pointed out, human performance significantly influences cybersecurity. Nevertheless, current cybersecurity strategies often fail to incorporate human factors fully, leaving utilities and other critical infrastructures vulnerable to human-induced risks (Hellström, 2007). The growing complexity of cyberthreats necessitates an interdisciplinary approach to cybersecurity that bridges the gap between psychology, systems theory, and cybersecurity (Boyce et al., 2011). This approach recognises that the key to enhancing cyber defence lies in technological advancement and understanding and managing human behaviour (Bates, 2017). Furthermore, as Von Solms and Van Niekerk (2013) and Panguluri et al. (2017) have shown, cybersecurity culture in organisations plays a crucial role in shaping this behaviour. The significance of this study is underscored by the urgency of addressing systemic vulnerabilities (Hellström, 2007) and enhancing cyber resilience (Kott & Linkov, 2019), particularly in an era where AI is poised to play a significant role in cybersecurity (Takahashi et al., 2010).

1.1.1 Statement of the problem

The digitalisation of our society, especially in critical infrastructures like utilities and health, has presented numerous benefits, such as increased efficiency, improved reliability, and unprecedented convenience. However, this reliance on digital systems also exposes these infrastructures to cybersecurity threats, which, if not correctly managed, could result in severe consequences, including service disruptions, loss of lives, financial losses, and potential threats to national security. Cybersecurity in critical infrastructures involves complex and interconnected issues, such as the evolving nature of cyberthreats, the intertwined roles of technology and humans in vulnerability and defence, the importance of systemic resilience, and the potential of emerging technologies like AI in enhancing cybersecurity measures. The human factor represents a significant yet often underestimated element of cybersecurity. Employees’ behaviours, organisational culture, and leadership commitment are crucial in shaping an organisation’s cybersecurity posture.

Furthermore, traditional approaches to cybersecurity that focus on individual aspects, such as information security or technical defences, have been found insufficient in the face of multifaceted and sophisticated cyberthreats. Integrated cybersecurity frameworks, which encompass multiple layers of defence and consider human and technical factors, are increasingly recognised as crucial. Nevertheless, understanding and implementing such integrated approaches remain challenging for many organisations. Moreover, while systemic resilience – the ability of a system to withstand and adapt to disturbances – is critical in cybersecurity, it is a concept that is not yet fully understood or operationalised in many critical infrastructures. However, AI holds significant potential for enhancing cybersecurity, and harnessing this potential effectively requires further exploration.

In light of these complexities and challenges, this chapter seeks to comprehensively examine cybersecurity in critical infrastructures, focusing on the human factor, integrated cybersecurity frameworks, systemic resilience, and the role of AI. It aims to contribute to the understanding and practice of cybersecurity, ultimately helping safeguard our critical infrastructures against current and future cyberthreats.

1.1.2 Significance of this study

This study is relevant to cybersecurity, especially critical infrastructures, and is essential from multiple perspectives (Figure 1.1).

Comprehensive Understanding of Cybersecurity: By adopting an integrated view of cybersecurity, this study enhances the understanding of the multifaceted and interdependent nature of cybersecurity issues. It goes beyond technical defence and includes human and cultural elements, systemic resilience, and emerging technologies. This contributes to a more comprehensive cybersecurity.
Insights Into Human-Related Risks: The study makes a significant contribution to discussions on human factors in cybersecurity, a topic that has often been overshadowed by a focus on technological aspects. By shedding light on human-related risks, this research underscores the importance of shaping organisational culture and training programmes to mitigate these risks.
Promotion of Integrated Cybersecurity Frameworks: The study offers practical insights into how these frameworks can be developed and implemented by advocating for integrated cybersecurity frameworks. It can guide organisations, especially those operating critical infrastructures, in designing cybersecurity strategies to address various threats and vulnerabilities.
Exploration of Systemic Resilience: The research brings systemic resilience to the fore in cybersecurity discussion. This is a relatively underexplored yet vital aspect of cybersecurity, and the study’s emphasis on systemic resilience can help organisations strengthen their capacity to withstand and recover from cyberattacks.
Harnessing the Potential of AI: The study also paves the way for further discussions and investigations into the use of AI in cybersecurity. The insights it provides can assist in driving forward the practical and responsible application of AI technologies in enhancing cybersecurity defences.
Figure 1.1 Study Significance.

This study can potentially inform and influence the cybersecurity strategies and practices of organisations operating critical infrastructures by focusing on these aspects. The insights generated from this research can play a vital role in bolstering cybersecurity measures, thereby safeguarding critical services, economies, and societies.

1.1.3 Research objectives

The primary objective of this chapter is to examine the intersection of human factors and systemic vulnerabilities in cybersecurity, focusing on utilities and other critical infrastructures. In doing so, this chapter aims at the following:

Explore the role of human behaviour in cybersecurity, including its influence on systemic vulnerabilities and potential as a focus for cyber defence (Boyce et al., 2011; Kovács & Nyikes, 2020)
Investigate the impact of cybersecurity culture on human behaviour and system resilience (Panguluri et al., 2017; Von Solms & Van Niekerk, 2013)
Develop an integrated framework for cybersecurity that effectively addresses both human factors and systemic vulnerabilities (Cormier & Ng, 2020)
Evaluate the potential of AI in mitigating human-related risks (Takahashi et al., 2010)
Offer practical recommendations for enhancing cyber defence through a comprehensive understanding of the human element (Bates, 2017; Gunduz & Das, 2020)
1.1.4 Chapter overview

This chapter is organised into nine sections, each exploring a different aspect of the human element in cybersecurity. Following this introduction, Section 1.2 reviews the concept of cyber-physical security and its importance in critical infrastructures. Section 1.3 examines systemic vulnerabilities in these infrastructures and how they can be exploited in cyberattacks. Section 1.4 delves into the human factor in cybersecurity, discussing human errors, social engineering, and insider threats. Section 1.5 discusses the role of culture in cybersecurity, while Section 1.6 proposes an integrated approach to cybersecurity. Section 1.7 explores the concept of systemic resilience in cybersecurity. Finally, Section 1.8 looks at the future of cybersecurity, particularly the role of AI, before the chapter concludes with a summary of insights and practical recommendations in Section 1.9. As such, this chapter seeks to contribute to the existing literature by offering an in-depth exploration of the human element in cybersecurity, emphasising utilities and other critical infrastructures.

1.2 Cyber-Physical Security and Critical Infrastructure: The Indivisible Duo
1.2.1 Definition of key terms

Cyber-Physical Systems (CPS): Cyber-physical systems represent the convergence of computational resources and physical processes. CPS leverages computing, communication, and control technologies to interact with the physical world in real time, generating tangible outcomes based on digital instructions (Gunduz & Das, 2020). As shown in Figure 1.2, CPS encompasses everything from Operational Technology (OT) to the Industrial Internet of Things (IIoT), Smart Cities, Smart Buildings, Industry 4.0, etc. In the context of this chapter, CPS is central to understanding how digital security measures directly influence the safe operation of utilities and other critical infrastructures.

Figure 1.2 Cyber-Physical System (CPS) Components.

Critical infrastructures are systems, networks, and physical and cyber–based assets that society heavily relies upon. They encompass vital sectors such as water and wastewater utilities, power grids, and transportation systems. The incapacitation or destruction of such infrastructures would significantly impact security, national economic security, public health, or safety. As highlighted by Cohen (2010), safeguarding these assets from cyberthreats is paramount to maintaining societal order and well-being.

Cyber-physical security pertains to measures implemented to shield cyber-physical systems from threats that could cause physical or virtual damage. It involves preserving the integrity of a system’s computational and physical elements. Clark and Hakim (2017) advocate for a multifaceted, integrated approach to ensuring the security of these interconnected systems.

These definitions provide a foundational understanding for this chapter’s exploration of the human factors impacting cybersecurity in critical infrastructures. The discussion on systemic vulnerabilities and their contribution to cybersecurity threats becomes more relevant by appreciating the interconnectedness and vulnerability of CPS and critical infrastructures. Examining the impact of systemic vulnerabilities on cybersecurity threats highlights the significance of a company’s cybersecurity culture in mitigating risks. People play a critical role in operating and protecting these systems. Finally, the definitions also serve as a stepping stone to exploring the future of cybersecurity. As technology advances, AI’s role in mitigating threats, streamlining responses, and enhancing the security of CPS and critical infrastructures becomes increasingly significant, signifying an exciting frontier in cybersecurity.

1.2.2 Evolution and importance of cyber-physical systems

The rise of CPS has mirrored advancements in computing and communication technologies. These developments have led to increasing digitisation and interconnection of systems, blurring the boundaries between the physical and cyber worlds. This evolution has given rise to CPS, capable of sophisticated interactions with the physical world, driving efficiencies and opportunities in numerous sectors (Gunduz & Das, 2020). The importance of CPS in today’s society cannot be overstated. Their integration into critical sectors such as utilities, healthcare, transportation, and manufacturing has made them essential components of our everyday lives. CPS streamline processes, increase efficiency, and offer unparalleled adaptability in managing complex, real-time operations. However, the prevalence of these systems has also drawn the attention of cybercriminals who seek to exploit their vulnerabilities for malicious intent (Clark & Hakim, 2017). This highlights the first objective of this chapter, which is to provide an in-depth understanding of the human factors affecting cybersecurity in critical infrastructures. As CPS continues to permeate essential sectors, understanding how human operators interact with these systems and where potential vulnerabilities exist becomes essential for ensuring their secure operation.

Additionally, the evolution and importance of CPS underscore the need to discuss systemic vulnerabilities and their contribution to cybersecurity threats. CPS are characterised by their interconnectivity, which, while providing numerous benefits, also presents a series of complex vulnerabilities that can be exploited in cyberattacks (Hellström, 2007). Furthermore, the pervasive nature of CPS across various sectors highlights the crucial role of an organisation’s cybersecurity culture in risk mitigation. Given the complexity and criticality of these systems, fostering a solid cybersecurity culture can help minimise human errors, encourage proactive protection measures, and enhance the overall security posture of organisations operating these systems (Panguluri et al., 2017; Von Solms & Van Niekerk, 2013). Given the complexity of these systems, this chapter proposes an integrated cybersecurity framework. This will incorporate risk analyses and hazard management strategies adapted to the unique characteristics of CPS, fostering systemic resilience against potential cyber-physical attacks (Cormier & Ng, 2020; Kott & Linkov, 2019). Finally, considering the fast-paced evolution of CPS and the challenges they present, the role of AI has become increasingly crucial in cybersecurity. AI’s potential to automate detection, improve response times, and predict threats can significantly enhance the security of CPS, pointing to an exciting direction for the future of cybersecurity (Takahashi et al., 2010).

1.2.3 Specific challenges in cyber-physical security

Cyber-physical security presents unique challenges due to CPS’s convergence of cyber and physical domains. One such challenge is the real-world ramifications of cyberthreats. In CPS, a cyberattack can manifest physical consequences, thus extending the threat landscape beyond data breaches or system disruptions (Gunduz & Das, 2020). For instance, a cyberattack on a water treatment plant could manipulate control systems, leading to water contamination or service disruption. Such attacks can have far-reaching consequences on public health and safety, highlighting the tangible risks of cyberthreats in CPS (Gunduz & Das, 2020).

Furthermore, the systemic interconnectedness of CPS introduces another layer of complexity to cybersecurity. Systemic vulnerabilities – weaknesses in one part of the system that could be exploited to compromise the entire system – make defending CPS an intricate task (Hellström, 2007). An attacker can exploit a single vulnerability point to cause severe damage to an entire CPS system, showing the multiplying effect of systemic vulnerabilities. The human element further complicates the cybersecurity landscape. Human operators, whether they interact directly with CPS or indirectly through decision-making roles, can significantly influence the security of these systems (Bates, 2017; Boyce et al., 2011). Their actions, both intentional (as in the case of insider threats) and accidental (such as errors due to lack of training or awareness), can introduce vulnerabilities or exacerbate existing ones (Kovács & Nyikes, 2020). It is crucial to comprehend and handle the human elements involved in cybersecurity. This highlights the significance of understanding and managing human factors in cybersecurity. Addressing these challenges necessitates an integrated, multidisciplinary approach to cyber-physical security. Strategies must incorporate technical defences, human factor considerations, and organisational strategies like risk management and resilience-building (Cormier & Ng, 2020).

1.3 Systemic Vulnerabilities and cybersecurity Threats
1.3.1 Systemic vulnerabilities: an overview

Systemic vulnerabilities pertain to weaknesses inherent within a system or network that threat actors could capitalise upon to undermine its integrity (Hellström, 2007). They are not isolated incidents; instead, they form part of the interconnected web of a system’s architecture. In the context of critical infrastructures, these vulnerabilities often emerge from the intricate complexity and interconnectivity intrinsic to CPS (Gunduz & Das, 2020). These vulnerabilities can take various forms. Architectural vulnerabilities, for instance, arise from designing and implementing a system or network (Cormier & Ng, 2020). They can also be linked to outdated or unsupported systems that no longer receive security patches, exposing them to known and emerging threats (Clark & Hakim, 2017). Weak authentication mechanisms, which can enable unauthorised access, form another significant category of systemic vulnerabilities (Von Solms & Van Niekerk, 2013).

Additionally, human errors contribute significantly to systemic vulnerabilities (Boyce et al., 2011). This can be misconfigurations, weak passwords, clicking on malicious links, or not adhering to cybersecurity best practices. These human-induced vulnerabilities underscore the need for cybersecurity education and awareness as part of an organisation’s defence strategy (Kovács & Nyikes, 2020).

Understanding and addressing these systemic vulnerabilities is paramount to enhancing the security of CPS. This aligns with the second objective of this chapter, which seeks to discuss systemic vulnerabilities and their contribution to cybersecurity threats (Hellström, 2007). By doing so, the aim is to inform the development of robust, holistic cybersecurity strategies that account for the multifaceted nature of systemic risks in CPS. However, addressing systemic vulnerabilities alone is not enough. This chapter also explores the human factors affecting cybersecurity in critical infrastructures and highlights the importance of an organisation’s cybersecurity culture in mitigating these vulnerabilities (Bates, 2017; Panguluri et al., 2017).

1.3.2 The interface between systemic vulnerabilities and cybersecurity

Systemic vulnerabilities are intimately linked with the concept of cybersecurity. Owing to the interconnected nature of CPS, a vulnerability in one section of a system can propagate, enabling an attacker to compromise other parts of the system, sometimes with catastrophic results (Clark & Hakim, 2017; Hellström, 2007). This domino effect indicates how systemic vulnerabilities can escalate from isolated incidents to pervasive threats. For instance, consider a utility infrastructure employing an Industrial Control System (ICS). In such a scenario, a security weakness within the ICS could be exploited to manipulate physical processes, resulting in service disruptions or even physical damage (Gunduz & Das, 2020). This example underlines how cybersecurity threats in CPS can transcend the digital realm and induce physical consequences.

Moreover, human elements introduce systemic vulnerabilities in cybersecurity. Human errors, such as mishandling sensitive data, misconfigurations, or susceptibility to phishing attacks, can contribute to systemic vulnerabilities (Bates, 2017). Lack of adequate training can result in staff not adhering to cybersecurity best practices. At the same time, malicious insiders pose threats that are particularly difficult to defend against due to their legitimate access to systems (Boyce et al., 201; Kovács & Nyikes, 2020). Addressing these vulnerabilities and comprehending their interplay with cybersecurity is crucial.

1.3.3 Cyberthreats leveraging systemic weaknesses

The landscape of cyberthreats is a vast expanse populated by diverse actors. These actors extend from individual hackers with potentially limited, albeit still dangerous, resources to organised crime groups who orchestrate sophisticated attacks. At the most severe end of the spectrum are nation-state actors with advanced capabilities and vast resources, enabling them to execute highly damaging attacks (Cohen, 2010). Moreover, the types of threats these actors can deploy are numerous and varied. They encompass malware, which can disrupt system operations or exfiltrate data, and ransomware, which lock users out of their systems until a ransom is paid. Another significant threat is the Advanced Persistent Threat (APT), a stealthy and continuous computer hacking process often orchestrated by nation-states to steal information or disrupt operations. These threats present unique risks and require specific mitigation strategies. Recognising and understanding these threats is critical in countering systemic vulnerabilities in cybersecurity.

1.4 The Human Element in Cybersecurity: The Weakest Link
1.4.1 Human factors in cybersecurity: an overview

The role of the human element in cybersecurity is pivotal and cannot be understated. The security of cyber-physical systems depends not only on robust technological and architectural systems but also on how humans interact with and manage these systems (Bates, 2017). Humans can sometimes be the weakest link, as their actions can introduce vulnerabilities into the system (Boyce et al., 2011). Critical infrastructure systems are susceptible to human error due to their complexity and interconnected nature. A seemingly minor mistake can trigger a chain of events, leading to significant damage or disruptions. These systems, essential for the functioning of a society, are made more vulnerable by their dependence on human operators, who are susceptible to making errors or being targeted by threat actors (Hellström, 2007). This understanding of human factors in cybersecurity aligns closely with the purposes of this chapter, which aims to provide an in-depth analysis of how human actions and behaviours impact the security of critical infrastructures. By acknowledging humans’ role in creating or exacerbating systemic vulnerabilities, we can develop more effective strategies to address these vulnerabilities (Bates, 2017; Boyce et al., 2011).

The chapter also emphasises the importance of fostering a solid cybersecurity culture within organisations to mitigate risks. By training employees in cybersecurity best practices and encouraging a culture that values security, organisations can minimise human errors that may otherwise lead to breaches or other security incidents (Panguluri et al., 2017). Additionally, the proposed cybersecurity framework considers not just technical measures but also the management of human factors. By incorporating elements such as risk analysis, hazard management, and systemic resilience, the framework aims to provide a holistic solution to cybersecurity challenges in critical infrastructure (Cormier & Ng, 2020; Kott & Linkov, 2019).

1.4.2 Human errors: a breach in cyber defence

Human errors are a significant cause of security incidents, accounting for many cybersecurity breaches (Bates, 2017). These errors can take numerous forms, from simple mistakes such as misconfiguring security settings to more complex issues like failing to follow established security protocols (Boyce et al., 2011). Human errors, unintentional or deliberate, can potentially provide an open door for threat actors to gain unauthorised access to systems and carry out malicious activities (Kovács & Nyikes, 2020). Various factors contribute to these human errors. Stress, cognitive overload, lack of proper training, and a poor understanding of cybersecurity protocols all can increase the likelihood of mistakes. These factors are not only a concern for the individuals making the errors but also pose a systemic risk to the entire organisation, mainly when the errors occur in critical infrastructure systems (Bates, 2017; Boyce et al., 2011). We aim to understand better the human factors affecting cybersecurity in critical infrastructures. Understanding the root causes of human error can help formulate strategies to prevent such errors, mitigate their impacts, and reveal the need for a robust cybersecurity culture within organisations (Bates, 2017; Boyce et al., 2011). Training and education can help reduce human errors by increasing employees’ awareness of the potential consequences of their actions and equipping them with the knowledge and skills to follow security protocols effectively (Panguluri et al., 2017).

1.4.3 The psychology behind social engineering

Social engineering represents a unique and highly effective class of cyberthreats that exploit the human element rather than technical vulnerabilities (Boyce et al., 2011). A deep understanding of human psychology and behavioural tendencies is central to social engineering attacks. By manipulating these tendencies, cybercriminals deceive individuals into performing actions compromising security, such as disclosing sensitive information or performing unauthorised transactions (Boyce et al., 2011). The human element is often considered the weakest link in the cybersecurity chain, making it the target of social engineering. It plays on natural human instincts and emotions, including trust, curiosity, fear, and a desire to be helpful. It also exploits social norms and relationships, such as authority and reciprocation, which can lead individuals to act against their better judgment (Boyce et al., 2011). For example, an attacker may pose as a superior or trusted colleague, leveraging the authority relationship to convince the victim to disclose sensitive data or execute a malicious action. Alternatively, an attacker may try to get the victim to perform a specific task, such as providing login credentials, by offering a favour or assistance and expecting the victim to reciprocate (Boyce et al., 2011). Understanding the psychology behind social engineering is critical in designing effective countermeasures. By studying the psychological principles of these attacks, organisations can better equip their employees with the knowledge and awareness to detect and avoid such threats (Boyce et al., 2011; Panguluri et al., 2017).

Moreover, comprehending human factors contributes to an effective cybersecurity culture. When employees understand how they might be manipulated, they are less likely to fall victim to such tactics. Consequently, regular training that focuses on the psychological aspects of social engineering can be crucial in building a resilient cybersecurity culture (Panguluri et al., 2017; Von Solms & Van Niekerk, 2013). Furthermore, integrating this understanding of human psychology into an organisation’s cybersecurity framework can contribute to systemic resilience. By considering the technical and human elements of cybersecurity, organisations can build robust defences that address the full scope of potential threats (Cormier & Ng, 2020; Kott & Linkov, 2019). Finally, emerging technologies like AI may also significantly combat social engineering. By analysing behaviour and communication patterns, AI systems could identify and flag suspicious activities that may indicate a social engineering attempt, thereby providing another layer of defence against these psychologically driven cyberthreats (Takahashi et al., 2010).

1.4.4 Insider threats: a hidden menace

Insider threats are a significant and often under-recognised cybersecurity concern. Such threats are typically posed by individuals with authorised access to an organisation’s systems, such as employees, contractors, or business partners (Kovács & Nyikes, 2020). These individuals, whether acting out of malicious intent, negligence, or manipulation by external parties, can inflict considerable damage due to their privileged access and knowledge of the organisation’s operations (Bates, 2017; Kovács & Nyikes, 2020). Insider threats are uniquely challenging due to their diverse origins. They can stem from disgruntled employees seeking retaliation, careless staff making unintentional mistakes or even spies who have intentionally infiltrated the organisation (Kovács & Nyikes, 2020). Such threats may also involve complex psychological and interpersonal dynamics, making them difficult to predict and detect (Bates, 2017). A daunting aspect of insider threats is their potential to bypass many traditional security controls. Insiders, especially IT-related ones, often deeply understand the organisation’s network and security practices. This knowledge allows them to evade detection tools, manipulate systems, and even erase evidence of their activities (Kovács & Nyikes, 2020).

Addressing insider threats requires delicate handling due to potential legal and ethical implications. Strategies must balance security requirements with respect for individual privacy and legal boundaries. If handled improperly, reactive measures can harm morale and exacerbate mistrust within the organisation (Kovács & Nyikes, 2020). The growing recognition of the importance of insider threats has led to the call for a comprehensive approach to their management. Such an approach should be multifaceted, combining technical measures, policy frameworks, and human resource strategies (Panguluri et al., 2017). From a technical perspective, this may involve employing advanced user behaviour analytics to detect abnormal patterns and implementing stricter access controls (Kott & Linkov, 2019; Takahashi et al., 2010). Organisational policies should clearly define acceptable and unacceptable behaviours and guide the handling of sensitive information (Panguluri et al., 2017). The aspect of human resources is critical in minimising insider threats. Regular training programmes, comprehensive background checks, and a positive work environment can contribute to this goal (Panguluri et al., 2017). Additionally, proactive communication strategies can help employees understand and better adhere to security practices that pragmatically reduce the risk of insider threats (Von Solms & Van Niekerk, 2013).

1.5 Role of Culture in Cybersecurity: Shaping the Human Factor
1.5.1 The significance of cybersecurity culture

Organisational culture powerfully influences individuals’ actions and attitudes within a workplace setting, including how they engage with cybersecurity matters (Panguluri et al., 2017). A cybersecurity culture, therefore, pertains to an organisation’s shared norms, values, attitudes, and behaviours aimed at promoting and ensuring cybersecurity (Panguluri et al., 2017). This culture forms an integral part of an organisation’s overall security posture, often playing a decisive role in determining the effectiveness of cybersecurity strategies (Panguluri et al., 2017). The role of cybersecurity culture is multifaceted. Firstly, it shapes how employees perceive and adhere to security policies (Panguluri et al., 2017). In an organisation with a robust cybersecurity culture, employees are likely to view security policies as critical tools for risk mitigation, leading to higher compliance rates.

Conversely, in organisations where such a culture is weak or absent, employees may view these policies as unnecessary burdens, leading to potential lapses in adherence (Boyce et al., 2011; Panguluri et al., 2017). Secondly, cybersecurity culture can significantly influence how seriously threats are taken within an organisation (Panguluri et al., 2017). When cybersecurity is embedded in an organisation’s culture, all levels, from top management to operational staff, are likely to recognise the importance of cybersecurity and the potential ramifications of threats. Such recognition often translates into proactive actions to manage and mitigate these threats (Clark & Hakim, 2017; Panguluri et al., 2017). Finally, an organisation’s cybersecurity culture can also shape how effectively incidents are handled when they occur (Panguluri et al., 2017). Organisations with a robust cybersecurity culture are more likely to have effective incident response plans and respond quickly and efficiently to security incidents. Employees in such organisations are also likely to be better prepared to identify and respond to potential security threats (Cormier & Ng, 2020; Panguluri et al., 2017).

Therefore, promoting a positive cybersecurity culture is not just about preventing threats; it also involves building resilience within the organisation and enhancing its ability to respond and recover from incidents. This includes fostering a learning environment where incidents are seen as opportunities for improvement rather than blame (Panguluri et al., 2017). Understanding and fostering a solid cybersecurity culture is integral to mitigating human factors affecting cybersecurity in critical infrastructures (Bates, 2017). Moreover, a robust cybersecurity culture forms a cornerstone for the proposed integrated cybersecurity framework, contributing to its goal of achieving systemic resilience (Cohen, 2010; Cormier & Ng, 2020). As we look to the future, organisations that successfully instil a proactive and informed cybersecurity culture will be better equipped to navigate the evolving cybersecurity landscape and effectively leverage emerging technologies such as AI.

1.5.2 Creating a cybersecurity culture in utilities

The vital role of utilities in society, combined with their increasing dependence on cyber-physical systems, makes them a high-value target for cybercriminals (Gunduz & Das, 2020). Creating a robust cybersecurity culture within utilities is paramount (Panguluri et al., 2017). This involves fostering an environment where every employee is aware of the cyber risks associated with their role, understands the importance of cybersecurity, and is committed to implementing sound cybersecurity practices (Panguluri et al., 2017). Continuous cybersecurity training and awareness programmes are vital to achieving this (Panguluri et al., 2017). Employees must be updated on the latest threats, the potential consequences of security breaches, and the protective measures they can adopt. This could include training on identifying phishing attempts, securing personal devices, and adhering to access management policies. Importantly, such training should be tailored to the unique context of utilities, considering the specific cybersecurity risks associated with their operations and technologies (Boyce et al., 2011).

Leadership commitment is another vital element in fostering a cybersecurity culture (Panguluri et al., 2017). Effective cybersecurity requires leaders to express commitment, promote integration into all processes, and hold all members accountable (Panguluri et al., 2017). Such commitment, when visible to all levels of the organisation, encourages employees to take cybersecurity seriously and strive for adherence to established policies and procedures (Cohen, 2010). Moreover, embedding cybersecurity considerations into all aspects of the organisation’s operations can further enhance the cybersecurity culture (Panguluri et al., 2017). This means incorporating cybersecurity into the design and operation of cyber-physical systems, decision-making processes, and hiring and performance evaluation procedures (Cormier & Ng, 2020). It also ensures that cybersecurity policies are feasible, clearly communicated, and consistently enforced (Von Solms & Van Niekerk, 2013).

The effective creation of a cybersecurity culture in utilities helps protect these critical infrastructures from threats. It aligns with the chapter’s aim to explore how human factors and organisational culture can contribute to cybersecurity (Bates, 2017). Furthermore, it provides an essential foundation for the proposed integrated cybersecurity framework by enhancing hazard management and contributing to systemic resilience (Cohen, 2010; Cormier & Ng, 2020). Utilities can build a robust defence line that complements technological security measures by considering the human factor. Ultimately, this integrated, human-centric approach represents the future of cybersecurity in utilities, one where human factors and technological advancements, like AI, work in synergy to secure critical infrastructures.

1.5.3 Shift from information security to cybersecurity

The migration from a primary focus on information security to a more comprehensive emphasis on cybersecurity signifies a strategic and conceptual evolution in response to the complexities of today’s technology ecosystems (Von Solms & Van Niekerk, 2013). While the terms are often used interchangeably, a careful delineation reveals the nuanced differences and the reasons behind this paradigm shift. Traditionally, information security has been associated with protecting data in both physical and electronic forms. It emphasises the principles of confidentiality (preventing unauthorised access), integrity (ensuring accuracy and consistency over time), and availability (guaranteeing reliable access by authorised individuals) of data (Von Solms & Van Niekerk, 2013). It primarily concerns safeguarding data from threats such as data breaches, identity theft, and service disruptions (Cohen, 2010).

Cybersecurity, on the other hand, has a broader scope. It involves protecting systems, networks, and data in cyberspace (Von Solms & Van Niekerk, 2013). This includes information and the systems and processes that use, store, and transmit that information. Cybersecurity encompasses securing all interconnected digital components, such as hardware, software, and data, from cyberthreats (Cohen, 2010). This includes threats that can lead to physical damage or significant real-world consequences, especially within cyber-physical systems (Clark & Hakim, 2017; Gunduz & Das, 2020). Therefore, the shift from information security to cybersecurity reflects an understanding of the complexities and interdependencies inherent in modern cyber-physical systems (Von Solms & Van Niekerk, 2013). With this shift, organisations acknowledge that securing data alone is not enough. There is a need to protect the entire ecosystem, including the systems that process and transmit data, the networks that connect them, and the interfaces with physical systems (Cohen, 2010; Von Solms & Van Niekerk, 2013).

Given the interconnectivity of modern systems, a vulnerability in one part of the system can potentially compromise the whole, highlighting the systemic vulnerabilities inherent in these systems (Hellström, 2007). The human factors influencing cybersecurity further complicate this landscape, necessitating comprehensive strategies that address security’s technical and human aspects (Bates, 2017; Boyce et al., 2011; Kovács & Nyikes, 2020). This shift also underscores the importance of developing an integrated cybersecurity framework that incorporates risk analysis, hazard management, and systemic resilience, all of which are crucial in the evolution of cybersecurity (Cormier & Ng, 2020). Furthermore, as we delve deeper into the future of cybersecurity, emerging technologies such as AI become increasingly prominent in navigating the complex landscape of cybersecurity threats.

1.5.4 Impact of culture on cybersecurity performance

The cybersecurity culture within an organisation significantly influences its ability to safeguard against, detect, and respond to cyberthreats (Panguluri et al., 2017). This culture, defined by shared values, beliefs, norms, and attitudes towards cybersecurity, plays a vital role in shaping behaviour and attitudes, consequently impacting cybersecurity performance (Panguluri et al., 2017). A robust cybersecurity culture, where the importance of cybersecurity is deeply ingrained in daily activities, has several notable benefits (Panguluri et al., 2017). Firstly, it fosters a proactive security posture where employees follow the cybersecurity policies and actively participate in enhancing them. Employees are more likely to be vigilant, comply with security protocols, identify potential threats, and report security incidents promptly (Panguluri et al., 2017). This active engagement and vigilance can help prevent potential security breaches and reduce the impact of those that occur (Boyce et al., 2011; Panguluri et al., 2017). Secondly, a robust cybersecurity culture reduces the risk associated with insider threats. With comprehensive education and awareness programmes, employees can better understand the value of the data and systems they are protecting, making them less likely to engage in harmful activities, whether intentional or unintentional (Kovács & Nyikes, 2020; Panguluri et al., 2017). Thirdly, such a culture provides resilience against social engineering attacks. With an understanding of the tactics used by attackers and the vulnerabilities they exploit, employees are better equipped to resist manipulation attempts and avoid falling for scams that could compromise the organisation’s security (Boyce et al., 2011; Panguluri et al., 2017).

Conversely, a weak cybersecurity culture can significantly hamper an organisation’s security performance. When employees lack understanding or awareness of cybersecurity practices and the implications of their actions, they are more likely to commit errors, ignore policies, and become targets for social engineering attacks (Bates, 2017; Panguluri et al., 2017). This can lead to a higher rate of security incidents, increased vulnerability to insider threats, and more significant potential for substantial security breaches (Bates, 2017; Panguluri et al., 2017). This cultural dimension of cybersecurity is critical to an organisation’s overall security strategy. By embedding a robust cybersecurity culture, organisations can transform their employees from potential security risks into valuable assets in their cybersecurity efforts, thereby improving their cybersecurity performance (Panguluri et al., 2017).

1.6 Integrated Cybersecurity Frameworks and Strategies: A Holistic Approach
1.6.1 The need for integration in cybersecurity

The escalating sophistication and diversity of cyberthreats underscore the need for a more comprehensive, integrated approach to cybersecurity (Cormier & Ng, 2020). Isolated cybersecurity measures, although necessary, are no longer sufficient in today’s dynamic threat landscape. This is particularly true for utilities and other critical infrastructures, which are prime targets for cyberthreat actors due to their interconnectedness and vital role in society (Cohen, 2010). An integrated cybersecurity approach represents a paradigm shift from traditional, fragmented security measures towards a more holistic and inclusive strategy (Cormier & Ng, 2020). It acknowledges that cybersecurity is not solely a technical issue but a complex, multifaceted problem involving technological, human, and organisational elements (Cohen, 2010; Cormier & Ng, 2020). It also recognises the interdependencies and feedback loops among these elements and the need to address them in a coordinated manner (Kott & Linkov, 2019).

An integrated cybersecurity strategy ensures that defences are robust and resilient across multiple dimensions (Cormier & Ng, 2020). This includes the technical dimension, which involves system architecture, software, and hardware, and the human dimension, which encompasses the attitudes, behaviours, skills, and knowledge of individuals within the organisation (Boyce et al., 2011; Cohen, 2010). It also includes the organisational dimension, covering governance structures, policy frameworks, and the overall cybersecurity culture (Panguluri et al., 2017). Acknowledging and addressing the interconnected nature of these dimensions enables an integrated cybersecurity strategy to provide a more thorough and robust defence against cyberthreats. This is crucial for protecting data and systems integrity, confidentiality, and availability and ensuring the continued delivery of essential services by utilities and other critical infrastructures (Cohen, 2010; Cormier & Ng, 2020; Gunduz & Das, 2020).

Integrating risk analysis, hazard management, and systemic resilience into cybersecurity strategies is particularly important in this context. This approach enables organisations to identify better, prioritise, and manage vulnerabilities and threats and develop resilience to withstand and recover from cyberattacks (Bates, 2017; Cormier & Ng, 2020; Kott & Linkov, 2019).

1.6.2 Principles of integrated cybersecurity frameworks

Integrated cybersecurity frameworks are based on six fundamental principles that shape their design and implementation. These principles, outlined below, encapsulate cybersecurity’s holistic, adaptable, and interconnected nature (Cormier & Ng, 2020).

Interdependency Recognition: Integrated cybersecurity frameworks acknowledge that all aspects of cybersecurity, from hardware and software to human behaviours and organisational policies, are interconnected (Cormier & Ng, 2020). A failure or vulnerability in one area can potentially impact others. This principle underlines the importance of a comprehensive approach to cybersecurity that addresses individual elements and their interrelationships (Cohen, 2010).
Systemic Perspective: These frameworks adopt a systems-based perspective, which appreciates the complexity and interconnectedness of modern cyber-physical systems (Cormier & Ng, 2020; Gunduz & Das, 2020). They focus not only on the identification and mitigation of individual vulnerabilities but also on understanding how these vulnerabilities interact within the broader system. This includes potential cascade effects, where a compromise in one area can lead to failures or breaches in others (Hellström, 2007).
Continuous Monitoring and Adaptation: Integrated cybersecurity frameworks recognise the dynamic cyberthreat landscape, with new vulnerabilities and vectors emerging (Cormier & Ng, 2020). This principle emphasises that identifying and responding to threats must be performed continuously, highlighting the need for adaptive defence strategies to accommodate changes in the threat, technological and regulatory landscapes (Kott & Linkov, 2019).
Balanced Focus on Prevention, Detection, and Response: A well-designed framework gives equal weight to preventive measures, threat detection, and incident response (Cormier & Ng, 2020). This principle aligns with the understanding that it is impossible to eliminate all risks, hence the need for robust detection and response capabilities to manage incidents effectively when they do occur (Clark & Hakim, 2017).
Resilience Building: An essential element of cybersecurity frameworks is their inbuilt resilience (Kott & Linkov, 2019). This principle stresses the importance of defending against threats and building capabilities to withstand, recover from, and adapt to cyber incidents. This could involve backup systems, redundancy, and incident response plans (Kott & Linkov, 2019).
Stakeholder Engagement: These frameworks require the involvement of all relevant stakeholders. This principle asserts that cybersecurity is a shared responsibility that requires active participation from all organisation members, from top-level management to frontline employees (Panguluri et al., 2017).

These principles underscore the alignment of integrated cybersecurity frameworks, particularly regarding systemic resilience and the role of human factors in cybersecurity (Bates, 2017; Boyce et al., 2011; Panguluri et al., 2017).

1.6.3 Risk analyses and hazard management in cybersecurity

Cyberthreats’ increasing complexity and frequency underline the importance of cybersecurity risk analyses and hazard management. These two concepts are interlinked components of an integrated cybersecurity strategy and involve a cyclical process of identifying, assessing, and mitigating potential cyberthreats (Cormier & Ng, 2020).

Risk Analyses: This is the first step in the risk management process, and it entails identifying, classifying, and evaluating potential risks (Cormier & Ng, 2020). The process begins with a thorough inventory of the organisation’s information assets, including hardware, software, data, and human resources. Potential threats and vulnerabilities that could result in unauthorised access, data loss, or service disruption are first identified in this step (Gunduz & Das, 2020). The potential impact and likelihood of those identified threats are then assessed to prioritise risk mitigation (Cormier & Ng, 2020).

Hazard Management: Following risk analyses, hazard management involves formulating strategies to mitigate the identified risks, considering their severity and likelihood (Cormier & Ng, 2020). Organisations often adopt strategies that involve both technical and human-centred measures to ensure the safety and security of data. Technical measures may include encryption of sensitive information, usage of firewalls and intrusion detection systems to prevent unauthorised access, timely patching and updates to address known vulnerabilities, and implementation of backup and disaster recovery plans to ensure business continuity in the event of a breach (Cormier & Ng, 2020; Kott & Linkov, 2019). Since human factors play a significant role in cybersecurity, hazard management also includes human-centred measures (Bates, 2017). This can involve creating cybersecurity awareness programmes to educate employees about common threats such as phishing and social engineering, offering training on good cybersecurity practices, fostering a cybersecurity-conscious culture, and implementing policies to manage insider threats (Bates, 2017; Boyce et al., 2011; Panguluri et al., 2017).

Integrating risk analyses and hazard management provides a proactive approach to cybersecurity, enabling organisations to anticipate and prepare for potential threats rather than simply reacting. It aligns closely with the aim of this chapter to propose an integrated cybersecurity framework that incorporates risk analysis, hazard management, and systemic resilience, and it acknowledges the significant role of human factors in cybersecurity (Bates, 2017; Boyce et al., 2011; Panguluri et al., 2017).

1.6.4 Implementing an integrated cybersecurity strategy

In the face of escalating cyberthreats, implementing an integrated cybersecurity strategy is no longer optional but necessary for organisations, especially those responsible for critical infrastructure. This multifaceted process requires active participation and commitment across all organisational levels to successfully establish a comprehensive security posture (Panguluri et al., 2017).

Leadership Commitment: Leadership is crucial in implementing an effective cybersecurity strategy. They are responsible for prioritising cybersecurity, allocating appropriate resources, and establishing a security-conscious culture within the organisation (Panguluri et al., 2017). Leaders can demonstrate their commitment by integrating cybersecurity into business strategies, promoting transparency in security practices, and endorsing continual investments in cybersecurity initiatives (Panguluri et al., 2017).

Engaging All Employees: Cybersecurity is not just the responsibility of IT departments; it is a shared obligation across the organisation (Bates, 2017; Boyce et al., 2011). Employees can strengthen or weaken the organisation’s cybersecurity posture through actions. Hence, a vital aspect of an integrated cybersecurity strategy is fostering employee awareness, understanding, and adherence to cybersecurity policies and best practices. This includes ongoing education and training programmes tailored to the roles and responsibilities of different employee groups (Bates, 2017; Panguluri et al., 2017).

Ongoing Monitoring and Adaptation: In an environment of constantly evolving cyberthreats, static cybersecurity measures are inadequate. An integrated cybersecurity strategy must continuously monitor systems, networks, and user behaviours and periodic reviews and updates of cybersecurity policies and controls (Cormier & Ng, 2020). This dynamic approach enables organisations to promptly identify and respond to emerging threats, adapt their strategies as needed, and minimise the impact of security incidents (Cormier & Ng, 2020).

In addition to the above, the strategy should incorporate risk analysis and hazard management principles, as discussed earlier, emphasising technical and human-centred measures to create a resilient defence against diverse cyberthreats (Cormier & Ng, 2020; Panguluri et al., 2017). When utilities and other critical infrastructures implement such integrated cybersecurity strategies, they not only enhance their resilience against cyberthreats but also safeguard their systems, data, and the delivery of essential services and emphasise the necessity of integrated cybersecurity frameworks incorporating risk analysis, hazard management, and systemic resilience, recognising the pivotal role of human factors (Bates, 2017; Boyce et al., 2011; Panguluri et al., 2017).

Because of the multifaceted nature of cybersecurity, this chapter proposes an Integrated Cybersecurity Framework (ICF) that incorporates all of these concepts, such as risk analysis, hazard management, and systemic resilience. The ICF is structured around six key pillars, informed by the interconnected nature of cyber-physical systems and the myriad threats they face. These pillars include risk analysis, hazard management, systemic resilience, cybersecurity culture, human factors, and future-proofing (Figure 1.3).

Figure 1.3 Proposed Integrated Cybersecurity Framework (ICF).

The first pillar of the ICF is risk analysis, which involves a meticulous process of identifying and evaluating potential cyberthreats to an organisation (Cormier & Ng, 2020). This analysis considers the specific nature of each threat, its probability of occurrence, its potential impact, and the vulnerabilities it may exploit. Given the fast-paced evolution of cyberthreats, this process is not static but is continuously updated to remain relevant.

Hazard management is the second pillar of the ICF. Once potential risks are identified and analysed, appropriate strategies are developed and implemented to mitigate these risks (Cormier & Ng, 2020). Cormier and Ng (2020) and Panguluri et al. (2017) emphasised developing and implementing targeted strategies to mitigate these identified risks. Such strategies encompass a range of measures, including technical solutions like intrusion detection systems and encryption, as well as human-centred approaches, such as comprehensive training and awareness programmes.

Systemic resilience is the third pillar of the ICF, which Kott and Linkov (2019) described as the capacity of a system to continue functioning even when faced with disruptions. That is, the resilience of a system is determined by its ability to absorb and adapt to changes, shocks, and incidents, thus enhancing its overall resilience. The objective is to ensure that cyber-physical systems can recover from cyberattacks effectively while maintaining operational integrity.

The ICF’s fourth pillar is a robust cybersecurity culture built on shared norms, values, and behaviours influencing employees’ cybersecurity actions (Panguluri et al., 2017). This cultural framework is pivotal in mitigating risks and enhancing an organisation’s security posture.

The sixth pillar of the ICF recognises the crucial role of human operators in cybersecurity and the need to manage risks associated with human origin. As noted by Bates (2017), Boyce et al. (2011), and Panguluri et al. (2017), this includes continuous training to address insider threats and social engineering, highlighting the complex interplay between technology and human behaviour in cybersecurity.

The sixth and final pillar of ICF pertains to future-proofing. A forward-facing perspective of the virtual threat landscape is necessary to keep pace with new and emerging security challenges. Cormier and Ng (2020) and Takahashi et al. (2010) advocate for continuously adapting cybersecurity strategies and integrating emerging technologies like artificial intelligence. This proactive stance ensures that organisations can effectively detect and respond to advanced threats, safeguarding their assets against the unpredictable landscape of cyberthreats.

The ICF provides a holistic approach to cybersecurity, integrating human and technical security aspects and emphasising the system’s interdependencies. By implementing this framework, organisations, especially those in charge of critical infrastructures, can enhance their resilience against cyberthreats, protect their systems and data, and ensure the continued delivery of essential services (Cohen, 2010; Cormier & Ng, 2020). The proposed Integrated Cybersecurity Framework (ICF) shares several similarities with other established frameworks, such as the NIST Cybersecurity Framework (CSF) and the ISA/IEC 62443.

Alignment with NIST CSF: The NIST CSF revolves around five functions: identify, protect, detect, respond, and recover. These functions resonate with the pillars of the ICF. For instance, ‘identity’ aligns with our emphasis on risk analysis, ‘protect’ and ‘detect’ coincide with our focus on hazard management, and ‘respond’ and ‘recover’ map directly onto our systemic resilience pillar. Like the NIST CSF, the ICF also underscores the importance of a continuous and dynamic approach to cybersecurity, recognising that the threat landscape evolves rapidly (Cormier & Ng, 2020). However, the ICF further emphasises the role of cybersecurity culture and human factors in cybersecurity, which are not explicitly covered in the NIST CSF. This makes the ICF particularly relevant for organisations that operate in complex, high-stakes environments where human error can have significant consequences (Bates, 2017; Boyce et al., 2011; Panguluri et al., 2017).

Alignment with ISA/IEC 62443: ISA/IEC 62443 is a series of standards developed explicitly for Industrial Automation and Control Systems (IACS). It provides detailed guidance on technical and system-level security requirements, which align with our ICF’s hazard management and systemic resilience pillars (Cormier & Ng, 2020; Kott & Linkov, 2019). The ISA/IEC 62443 also strongly emphasises risk assessment, which matches our focus on risk analysis. Additionally, its emphasis on security by design and continuous improvement resonates with our recommendation for continuous adaptation and future-proofing (Cormier & Ng, 2020; Takahashi et al., 2010). Like the NIST CSF, the ICF extends beyond ISA/IEC 62443 by underlining the importance of cybersecurity culture and human factors in cybersecurity, reinforcing the significance of these elements in maintaining a robust cybersecurity posture (Bates, 2017; Boyce et al., 2011; Panguluri et al., 2017).

In closing, the ICF aligns well with existing industry frameworks while adding a more explicit focus on human factors and organisational culture. It provides a comprehensive and holistic approach to cybersecurity that reflects the complexity and interdependencies of modern cyber-physical systems.

1.7 Systemic Resilience in Cybersecurity: Bouncing Back from Attacks
1.7.1 Systemic resilience: an overview

Systemic resilience represents the robustness and adaptability of a system in the face of potential disruptions, allowing it to sustain its core operations under adverse circumstances (Kott & Linkov, 2019). Rooted in systems theory, it accentuates the interconnectedness and interdependence of a system’s components. The theory advocates that a system’s resilience cannot be simply deduced from the individual resilience of its components but necessitates an understanding of their collective interactions and dynamics (Kott & Linkov, 2019). In cybersecurity, systemic resilience goes beyond traditional measures of defence and protection. Instead, it emphasises the ability to endure cyberthreats, recover from security incidents, and evolve in response to changes in the threat landscape (Kott & Linkov, 2019). Due to their inherent complexity and interconnectedness, cyber-physical systems are particularly susceptible to cascading failures stemming from cyberthreats. These threats could compromise one system component and propagate across the system due to their interlinked nature, thereby magnifying the impact of the initial threat (Kott & Linkov, 2019).

Underlining the significance of systemic resilience in cybersecurity, it becomes critical for organisations, particularly those in charge of critical infrastructures, to devise strategies that enable their systems to withstand, adapt to, and recover from cyberthreats. These strategies may encompass various aspects, including strengthening security measures, establishing robust incident response plans, and promoting a security-conscious culture within the organisation. They should also involve building capacities to absorb shocks, learn from incidents, and reconfigure system components while maintaining essential functions (Kott & Linkov, 2019). By incorporating systemic resilience into their cybersecurity frameworks, organisations can ensure their capabilities to ‘bounce back’ from cyberattacks, mitigating the risk of catastrophic failures and preserving the continuity of their operations.

1.7.2 Role of systemic resilience in cybersecurity

In the context of cybersecurity, systemic resilience encompasses the capacity of an organisation or infrastructure to anticipate, withstand, recover from, and adapt to cyberthreats (Kott & Linkov, 2019). It emphasises preventing attacks and ensuring rapid recovery and learning during a breach (Kott & Linkov, 2019). This is critical given that no cybersecurity measures can guarantee absolute protection against all potential threats (Cohen, 2010; Kott & Linkov, 2019). Systemic resilience in cybersecurity is predicated on the understanding that no single defence mechanism can be completely foolproof. As threat actors evolve their techniques and exploit novel vulnerabilities, some attacks will inevitably succeed (Cohen, 2010; Kott & Linkov, 2019). In this scenario, resilience becomes the critical distinguishing factor between organisations that can weather a cyber storm and those that cannot.

Anticipation in this context refers to the ability to foresee and prepare for potential cyberthreats based on a comprehensive understanding of the threat landscape and an organisation’s unique vulnerabilities (Gunduz & Das, 2020). Anticipation strategies could involve ongoing threat intelligence and predictive analytics that help identify emerging threats and vulnerabilities, thus allowing proactive steps to be taken to secure the system (Gunduz & Das, 2020). Withstanding attacks, the second component of resilience, involves deploying robust defence mechanisms to prevent, deter, and disrupt attacks (Cohen, 2010). This could include the use of firewalls, intrusion detection systems, robust authentication protocols, and other technical controls (Cormier & Ng, 2020). Equally important are human-oriented strategies, such as practical training programmes that help employees recognise and respond to threats (Bates, 2017; Boyce et al., 2011; Panguluri et al., 2017). Recovery after a cyberattack involves quickly restoring normal operations and minimising the breach’s impact (Kott & Linkov, 2019). This can be facilitated by incident response plans that outline precise procedures for identifying, containing, and rectifying a breach. Data backups and disaster recovery plans also play a crucial role in this stage, enabling rapid restoration of lost or compromised data (Cormier & Ng, 2020; Kott & Linkov, 2019).

Finally, adaptation involves learning from past attacks to improve future resilience. After a breach, it is essential to conduct a post-mortem analysis to understand what went wrong and how to prevent similar incidents in the future (Kott & Linkov, 2019). This may involve updating technical controls, revising policies and procedures, or enhancing training programmes (Bates, 2017; Boyce et al., 2011; Panguluri et al., 2017). The role of systemic resilience in cybersecurity thus encompasses a wide range of strategies, all aimed at enhancing the ability of an organisation to survive and thrive in the face of cyberthreats (Cohen, 2010; Kott & Linkov, 2019). By embedding resilience into their cybersecurity strategy, organisations can not only mitigate the risks of cyberthreats but also turn these challenges into opportunities for improvement and growth (Kott & Linkov, 2019; Von Solms & Van Niekerk, 2013).

1.7.3 Building systemic resilience in critical infrastructures

Building systemic resilience in critical infrastructures is not an easy task. Still, it is vital, given the critical services these infrastructures provide and the potentially devastating consequences of cyber breaches (Clark & Hakim, 2017; Kott & Linkov, 2019). This process typically involves several key steps, each of which necessitates meticulous planning, execution, and ongoing management.

Understanding the System: This is the first step in building systemic resilience. A comprehensive understanding of the system’s structure, functions, and interdependencies is required to assess its vulnerability to cyberthreats (Clark & Hakim, 2017; Kott & Linkov, 2019). This involves mapping out the various components of the system, identifying the interconnections between them, and understanding how a disruption in one part could affect the whole (Clark & Hakim, 2017). This systems-thinking approach provides a comprehensive view of potential vulnerabilities and risks, enabling more effective mitigation strategies (Clark & Hakim, 2017; Kott & Linkov, 2019).

Identifying Threats and Vulnerabilities: Once the system’s structure and functions are understood, the next step is identifying potential threats and vulnerabilities (Cormier & Ng, 2020). This involves analysing the cyberthreats that could target the infrastructure and examining the system’s weak points (Cormier & Ng, 2020; Kott & Linkov, 2019). Risk assessment techniques, such as threat modelling and vulnerability scanning, can be used (Cormier & Ng, 2020).

Implementing Resilience Measures: Having identified potential threats and vulnerabilities, the next step is implementing measures to enhance resilience. This could involve technical defences like firewalls, intrusion detection systems, or encrypted communications (Cormier & Ng, 2020; Kott & Linkov, 2019). Equally important are human-centred measures, such as cybersecurity awareness training, transparent and enforceable policies, and a positive cybersecurity culture (Bates, 2017; Boyce et al., 2011; Kott & Linkov, 2019). The goal is to create an integrated defence that protects the system from multiple angles, recognising that no single measure is sufficient (Cormier & Ng, 2020; Kott & Linkov, 2019).

Continuous Monitoring and Adaptation: Finally, given the rapidly changing nature of the cyberthreat landscape, continuous monitoring and adaptation are crucial. This involves regularly reviewing and updating the system’s defences in response to new threats or changes in the system itself (Kott & Linkov, 2019). Incident response plans and disaster recovery plans should be tested and refined regularly to ensure they remain effective (Cormier & Ng, 2020; Kott & Linkov, 2019). Emerging technologies like AI and machine learning (ML) can also enhance threat detection and response capabilities (Takahashi et al., 2010).

In sum, building systemic resilience in critical infrastructures requires a holistic approach that addresses both human and technical factors and evolves in response to changes in the threat landscape and the system itself (Kott & Linkov, 2019). By doing so, these infrastructures can better protect the essential services they provide against the increasing threat of cyberattacks (Cohen, 2010; Boyce et al., 2011; Cohen, 2010; Cormier & Ng, 2020).

1.8 The Future of Cybersecurity and AI’s Role: The Next Frontier
1.8.1 Emerging trends in cybersecurity

As we look towards the future, cybersecurity is set to evolve in several vital ways. Novel technologies, methodologies, and threats continually emerge, prompting shifts in cybersecurity practices and strategies (Von Solms & Van Niekerk, 2013). This section will highlight some of the most salient emerging trends in cybersecurity, such as the growing reliance on AI and ML, the increasing emphasis on proactivity and resilience, and the expanding recognition of the human factor in cybersecurity (Boyce et al., 2011; Boyce et al., 2011; Von Solms & Van Niekerk, 2013, pp. 6, 12).

AI and ML are increasingly instrumental in cybersecurity (Von Solms & Van Niekerk, 2013). These technologies can enhance threat detection, automate incident response, and predict potential vulnerabilities (Von Solms & Van Niekerk, 2013). For instance, ML algorithms can analyse large volumes of data to detect anomalies or unusual patterns that might indicate a cyberattack (Von Solms & Van Niekerk, 2013). AI can also simulate cyberattacks and defences, enabling organisations to proactively test and improve security measures (Von Solms & Van Niekerk, 2013). However, as these technologies are adopted, they also introduce new vulnerabilities and attack vectors that must be managed (Von Solms & Van Niekerk, 2013).

In addition to defending against attacks, there is a growing emphasis on anticipating and preparing for them (Kott & Linkov, 2019). This involves implementing proactive measures, such as threat hunting and penetration testing, and building systemic resilience to bounce back from attacks (Kott & Linkov, 2019). Cybersecurity is increasingly seen as an ongoing process of managing risk rather than a static goal of preventing all attacks (Kott & Linkov, 2019).

The role of human factors in cybersecurity will increasingly need to be recognised (Boyce et al., 2011). As systems become more complex and attackers become more sophisticated, the human element is often the weakest link in cybersecurity (Bates, 2017; Boyce et al., 2011). Recognising this, there is an expanding focus on cybersecurity culture, user education, and human-centred design in cybersecurity practices (Boyce et al., 2011; Panguluri et al., 2017).

These trends underline the need for an integrated approach to cybersecurity, which recognises the interconnectedness of various aspects of cybersecurity and balances technical measures with human-centred strategies (Boyce et al., 2011; Cormier & Ng, 2020; Kott & Linkov, 2019). This will be crucial for navigating the future’s complex and evolving cybersecurity landscape (Von Solms & Van Niekerk, 2013).

1.8.2 AI and cybersecurity: a new paradigm

AI and ML technologies have dramatically reshaped the cybersecurity landscape, opening new opportunities for threat detection, prevention, and response. As Takahashi et al. (2010) articulated, these technologies herald a new era in cybersecurity, marked by increased automation, adaptability, and sophistication.

One of the primary applications of AI in cybersecurity is threat detection and prediction. Traditional cybersecurity tools rely on predefined rules and signatures to identify threats.
