In contrast, AI can learn from patterns and anomalies in data to detect new and unknown threats (Takahashi et al., 2010). ML algorithms can analyse large volumes of network traffic or system activity data to identify suspicious patterns that might signify an attack (Takahashi et al., 2010). Furthermore, these algorithms can be trained on historical data to predict potential attacks or identify likely targets, enabling proactive threat mitigation (Takahashi et al., 2010).

AI can also automate repetitive and time-consuming cybersecurity tasks like log analysis, alert triage, or incident response. This improves efficiency and frees human resources for more complex tasks (Takahashi et al., 2010). AI can also help orchestrate responses to detected threats, automating actions such as isolating affected systems or blocking malicious IPs (Takahashi et al., 2010).

AI’s learning capabilities allow cybersecurity defences to adapt and evolve. This is crucial given the rapidly changing nature of cyberthreats. By learning from each incident, AI can continually improve its detection capabilities and response strategies (Takahashi et al., 2010). However, while AI holds tremendous promise for cybersecurity, it also introduces new challenges (Takahashi et al., 2010). For instance, attackers can use it to launch more sophisticated attacks or evade detection.

Moreover, as AI models become more complex, they can become ‘black boxes’, making it difficult to understand why they make certain decisions (Takahashi et al., 2010). Lastly, AI systems can be targets of attacks, such as adversarial attacks that seek to deceive AI models (Takahashi et al., 2010). Therefore, as we embrace the potential of AI in cybersecurity, it is also essential to address these challenges and ensure that AI is used responsibly and effectively (Takahashi et al., 2010). This includes securing AI systems, making AI decisions transparent and understandable, and considering the ethical implications of AI use (Takahashi et al., 2010). As we move forward into the AI-driven future of cybersecurity, these considerations will be crucial to harnessing the full potential of AI while mitigating its risks (Takahashi et al., 2010).

1.8.3 The future of AI in mitigating human-related risks

AI stands poised to revolutionise how we manage human-related cybersecurity risks. Its ability to learn from vast volumes of data, predict patterns, and automate tasks can significantly alleviate the vulnerabilities inherent in human behaviours and decisions (Bates, 2017; Boyce et al., 2011; Takahashi et al., 2010). This potential, while yet to be fully realised, points towards a future where AI plays an integral role in bolstering human defences in the cybersecurity domain.

Mitigating Human Errors with AI: Human errors significantly contribute to security breaches. These errors often stem from cognitive overload, inadequate training, or the misconfiguration of security settings (Bates, 2017). AI can help mitigate these risks by automating specific tasks and reducing the chances of human error (Takahashi et al., 2010). For instance, AI could manage complex configurations or carry out routine checks to ensure that security settings have not been inadvertently altered (Takahashi et al., 2010).
Modelling and Predicting Human Behaviour: AI and ML algorithms are proficient at analysing large datasets to detect patterns and make predictions. By applying these capabilities to human behaviour, AI can help organisations understand their employees’ behaviour patterns and predict risky behaviours (Boyce et al., 2011; Takahashi, 2010). These insights can be used to develop more effective security protocols, prevent social engineering attacks, and anticipate potential insider threats (Boyce et al., 2011; Kovács & Nyikes, 2020; Takahashi et al., 2010).
Fostering Cybersecurity Culture: AI can also be crucial in fostering a solid cybersecurity culture within organisations. ML algorithms can be used to personalise cybersecurity training, tailoring it to the needs and behaviours of individual employees (Panguluri et al., 2017; Takahashi et al., 2010). This could increase the effectiveness of training programmes and improve employees’ understanding and adherence to cybersecurity protocols (Panguluri et al., 2017; Takahashi et al., 2010). Furthermore, AI systems could provide real-time feedback on employees’ cybersecurity behaviours, helping them to recognise and correct risky behaviours (Panguluri et al., 2017; Takahashi et al., 2010).
Envisioning the Future of AI in Cybersecurity: It is clear that while AI will augment technical defences, its most transformative potential may lie in its ability to enhance the human elements of cybersecurity. By mitigating human-related risks, modelling human behaviour, and fostering a cybersecurity culture, AI could significantly enhance the overall security posture of organisations (Panguluri et al., 2017; Takahashi et al., 2010). However, realising this potential will require careful consideration of ethical and transparency issues, ongoing research and innovation, and the commitment of organisations to leverage AI in their cybersecurity strategies (Takahashi et al., 2010).
1.9 Conclusions and Recommendations: Charting the Path Forward
1.9.1 Summary of key findings

As we take stock of the multifaceted world of cybersecurity in critical infrastructures, several vital insights stand out. Cybersecurity is a complex domain that protects systems, networks, data, and humans. Physical and digital security are intertwined, especially in safeguarding critical infrastructures. Human factors are pivotal in cybersecurity frameworks. Systemic resilience is crucial to anticipate, withstand, recover from, and adapt to cyberthreats. Integrated cybersecurity frameworks that offer holistic defence strategies and align with industry standards are necessary. A comprehensive approach that addresses technical and human aspects, promotes systemic resilience, and incorporates advanced technologies is needed to enhance cybersecurity in critical infrastructures.

Understanding Cybersecurity’s Multifaceted Nature: Cybersecurity is not monolithic. It is a complex web that includes the security of systems, networks, data, and humans (Cohen, 2010; Von Solms & Van Niekerk, 2013). The shift from focusing on information security to cybersecurity highlights the importance of understanding this intertwined nature of physical and digital security (Von Solms & Van Niekerk, 2013). Protecting critical infrastructures, like utilities, requires a comprehensive approach that addresses cybersecurity’s human and technical aspects (Cohen, 2010; Panguluri et al., 2017).
The Human Factor in Cybersecurity: The role of human behaviours, emotions, and decisions in cybersecurity has been underscored throughout the discussions (Bates, 2017; Boyce et al., 2011; Clark & Hakim, 2017). Humans can be the weakest link in cybersecurity due to a lack of awareness, susceptibility to social engineering, and insider threats (Bates, 2017; Boyce et al., 2011; Clark & Hakim, 2017; Kovács & Nyikes, 2020). However, humans can also be the strongest defenders when equipped with the proper knowledge, tools, and motivation (Bates, 2017; Boyce et al., 2011; Panguluri et al., 2017). This highlights the importance of fostering a solid cybersecurity culture within organisations (Panguluri et al., 2017).
Systemic Resilience in Cybersecurity: Systemic resilience, the capacity to anticipate, withstand, recover from, and adapt to cyberthreats, has emerged as a crucial aspect of cybersecurity (Kott & Linkov, 2019). Hence, it is essential to recognise the significance of systemic resilience in reducing the likelihood of service disruptions and damages in case of a cyberattack. To build systemic resilience in critical infrastructures, one must comprehend the structure and functions of the system, identify threats and vulnerabilities, put in place measures to enhance resilience, and constantly monitor and adapt to changes (Clark & Hakim, 2017; Cormier & Ng, 2020; Kott & Linkov, 2019).
Integrated Cybersecurity Frameworks: The discussions have also emphasised the need for an integrated approach to cybersecurity. Integrated cybersecurity frameworks, like the one proposed, allow for a holistic defence strategy that recognises the interdependencies between various aspects of cybersecurity, takes a systemic perspective, and adapts to the evolving cybersecurity landscape (Cormier & Ng, 2020). These frameworks align with industry standards like NIST CSF and ISA/IEC 62443, reinforcing their relevance and applicability (Cormier & Ng, 2020; ISA, 2024).

The insights gleaned from these discussions underline the complexity of cybersecurity in critical infrastructures and the importance of adopting a multifaceted and proactive approach. They highlight the need to address both human and technical aspects of cybersecurity, foster systemic resilience, and leverage emerging technologies and methodologies like AI and ontology. Moving forward, these insights can guide the development of more robust, resilient, and adaptable cybersecurity strategies in the face of an ever-evolving threat landscape.

1.9.2 Recommendations for practice

Drawing from the insights gained from the chapter, this section provides actionable recommendations for enhancing cybersecurity in critical infrastructures. It proposes strategies to mitigate human-related risks, cultivate a cybersecurity culture, implement integrated cybersecurity frameworks, and build systemic resilience (Bates, 2017; Boyce et al., 2011; Clark & Hakim, 2017; Cohen, 2010; Cormier & Ng, 2020; Kott & Linkov, 2019; Panguluri et al., 2017).

Mitigating Human-Related Risks: Staff training and awareness programmes should be a core component of cybersecurity strategies (Bates, 2017; Boyce et al., 2011). These programmes should cover technical skills and foster awareness of cybersecurity, potential threats, and individuals’ roles in maintaining security (Boyce et al., 2011; Panguluri et al., 2017). Using simulated cyberattacks or ‘red teaming’ exercises can help staff understand the nature of threats and how to respond (Bates, 2017; Boyce et al., 2011). Moreover, stringent access controls and monitoring systems can help to prevent and detect malicious insider activities (Boyce et al., 2011).
Cultivating a Cybersecurity Culture: A robust cybersecurity culture can significantly enhance security performance (Panguluri et al., 2017). This requires strong leadership commitment and ongoing efforts to embed security considerations into the organisation’s operations (Panguluri et al., 2017). One practical approach could be integrating cybersecurity objectives into performance metrics and rewards systems (Bates, 2017; Panguluri et al., 2017). Regular communication and feedback can reinforce the importance of security and keep it at the forefront of employees’ minds (Panguluri et al., 2017).
Implementing Integrated Cybersecurity Frameworks: An integrated approach to cybersecurity can provide robust protection against critical infrastructures’ multifaceted threats (Cormier & Ng, 2020). Frameworks such as the NIST Cybersecurity Framework or the IEC 62443 standard provide a comprehensive guide for implementing such an approach (ISA, 2024). These frameworks emphasise a continuous process of identifying, protecting, detecting, responding to, and recovering from cyberthreats (ISA, 2024). They also underscore the importance of considering technical and human factors in cybersecurity (Cormier & Ng, 2020; ISA, 2024).
Building Systemic Resilience: Systemic resilience involves understanding the system’s structure, identifying threats and vulnerabilities, implementing resilience measures, and adapting to changes and new threats (Clark & Hakim, 2017; Cormier & Ng, 2020; Kott & Linkov, 2019). Incident response plans should be implemented to ensure rapid and effective attack recovery (Kott & Linkov, 2019). Continuous monitoring and regular ‘health checks’ can help identify and address potential vulnerabilities before they are exploited (Cormier & Ng, 2020; Kott & Linkov, 2019).
Leveraging AI and Ontology: AI and ontology hold significant potential for enhancing cybersecurity (Gunduz & Das, 2020; Takahashi et al., 2010). AI can automate threat detection, conduct predictive analysis, and respond to threats more quickly and effectively (Takahashi et al., 2010). On the other hand, ontology can help to improve understanding and communication in cybersecurity, making it easier to identify and address vulnerabilities (Gunduz & Das, 2020). Organisations must invest in the necessary skills and infrastructure to leverage these technologies (Takahashi et al., 2010). Collaborating with technology vendors, academic institutions, or specialised cybersecurity firms may be beneficial (Takahashi et al., 2010).

In conclusion, securing critical infrastructures is complex and challenging but also essential. The recommendations in this section offer practical strategies for enhancing cybersecurity, which can be tailored to individual organisations’ specific needs and contexts. It is crucial to remember that cybersecurity is a continuous effort, requiring ongoing monitoring, learning, and adaptation in the face of a constantly evolving threat landscape.

1.9.3 Avenues for future research

Future research in cybersecurity has great potential to improve our understanding and development of more effective strategies and frameworks. There is a particular need to explore the human factor in cybersecurity, including how human behaviour affects cybersecurity. This could involve examining the impact of demographic factors and organisational cultures on security practices and developing innovative methods for altering behaviours and promoting a culture of cybersecurity awareness (Bates, 2017; Boyce et al., 2011; Panguluri et al., 2017).

Another critical area of focus is creating and validating integrated cybersecurity frameworks. This might include developing or refining new frameworks and conducting comparative studies or empirical research to assess their effectiveness in various settings. It is crucial to explore how these frameworks can be implemented practically across different organisational contexts, considering various constraints (Cormier & Ng, 2020; ISA, 2024).

The concept of systemic resilience in cybersecurity also warrants further investigation. Understanding how to achieve systemic resilience across different types of organisations and infrastructures is essential. This includes studying the factors contributing to resilience and developing methods to measure and assess it, which is vital for tracking progress and identifying areas that require improvement (Kott & Linkov, 2019).

As the use of artificial intelligence (AI) in cybersecurity continues to increase, future research should aim to advance AI technologies and explore their potential applications in this domain. This could involve creating new AI algorithms or techniques specifically for cybersecurity purposes and considering the ethical and practical challenges of using AI in this field. Additionally, the role of ontology in supporting various cybersecurity aspects, such as risk assessment and incident response, presents another area for research (Gunduz & Das, 2020; Takahashi, 2010).

Lastly, conducting in-depth case studies and empirical research on cybersecurity within critical infrastructures is crucial. These studies can provide insight into real-world challenges and strategies employed in cybersecurity, offering invaluable insights that can inform both practice and policy. Furthermore, empirical research can validate and refine theoretical concepts and frameworks, thereby enhancing the academic rigour of the cybersecurity field (Hellström, 2007).

In conclusion, while much has been learned about cybersecurity, much remains to be explored. The suggested avenues offer exciting opportunities for advancing knowledge and practice in this vital field. Future research in these areas can significantly enhance the security and resilience of critical infrastructures worldwide.

Cybersecurity’s growing importance and complexity in critical infrastructures cannot be understated. This chapter has illuminated various facets of cybersecurity, drawing particular attention to the human factor, the evolution from information security to cybersecurity, the role of culture in shaping cybersecurity practices, the need for integrated cybersecurity frameworks, the concept of systemic resilience, and the promising role of AI in the cybersecurity landscape. The human factor, often overlooked, is a critical component of cybersecurity. Fostering a robust cybersecurity culture within organisations is not just about implementing strict policies or employing the latest technological defences. Still, it also involves educating employees, nurturing an environment of shared responsibility, and embedding cybersecurity considerations into all aspects of the organisation’s operations.

Moving from information security to cybersecurity represents a broader view of the intertwined nature of physical and digital security, highlighting the need to address human and technical security aspects. The shift reflects the complex interdependencies within cyber-physical systems and recognises the reality of the contemporary digital environment. Building systemic resilience in critical infrastructures and implementing integrated cybersecurity strategies are essential for protecting these critical systems. They require a deep understanding of the systems, their potential threats and vulnerabilities, and the continuous adaptation to evolving circumstances.

AI promises a new paradigm in cybersecurity, with its ability to automate tasks, predict attacks, and learn over time. It holds particular potential for mitigating human-related risks and contributing to developing a robust cybersecurity culture. However, despite the advancements in understanding and addressing cybersecurity challenges, the field is evolving, and new threats, methodologies, and technologies continue to emerge. As such, continual research and adaptation are vital to staying ahead of these threats. Future research is encouraged to delve deeper into the mentioned topics, aiming for a safer and more secure digital future. This chapter highlights that cybersecurity in critical infrastructures is a multifaceted and evolving challenge that requires a comprehensive, integrated, and forward-looking approach. It underscores that everyone within an organisation has a role in cybersecurity and the importance of resilience, adaptability, and continuous learning in navigating the cybersecurity landscape. As we look towards the future, the insights and discussions presented in this chapter can provide valuable guidance for practitioners and researchers in enhancing the security and resilience of our critical infrastructures.

Chapter 2 Analysing cyber-physical attacks The human operator challenge in mining

Osmar Couto and Ahmed Ibrahim

DOI: 10.1201/9781032664859-2

2.1 Introduction

Supervisory Control and Data Acquisition (SCADA) systems are embedded in most process plants in mining sites worldwide. Although mining activities do not represent a mission-critical system for human survival, they provide a base for a high economic proportion for specific countries where this sector is the leading tax income generation. Therefore, these mining sites represent significant targets for cyber-physical attacks (CPA) to destabilise and cause financial loss or even be classified as terrorist actions when they threaten a country’s stability.

Real-world incidents have flagged several industries and started decades ago (Nicholson et al., 2012; Pizarro & Fuenzalida, 2021). Nicholson et al. (2012) explained that SCADA attacks were 30% external and 70% internal in a survey between 1982 and 2000, which had turned to the opposite during 2001 and 2003, where 30% were internal and 70% were external attacks; the authors of the survey attributed the increase in external attacks to the standardisation of the network standards. Coates et al. (2010) presented details of SCADA (Supervisory Control and Data Acquisition) system attacks that were not widely disseminated. These attacks included instances such as the use of a trojan firmware by the United States to trigger an explosion in a pipeline located in the Soviet Union during the Cold War. In another example, Coates et al. (2010) identified cyberattackers that could infiltrate the primary power grid in California, in 2001, and the intrusion went unnoticed for a staggering 17 days. These incidents serve as stark reminders of the potential vulnerabilities of critical infrastructure systems to cyberthreats and the potentially devastating consequences of successful attacks.

Further, in 2003, Slammer Worm turned off the safety monitoring of the Davis-Besser nuclear plant in Ohio. Worldwide known attacks were used to warn about SCADA system vulnerabilities, employing malware such as Stuxnet (2010), Duqu (2011), and Havex (2014) (Nazir et al., 2017). Moreover, the Ukrainian power industry and railway were attacked by BlackEnergy crimeware (Adepu et al., 2020).

A complex SCADA system is usually supervised by human operators who follow up on alarms and anomalies through a human–machine interface (HMI) and other supervisory interfaces that communicate the health of the system and monitor endpoint devices, such as actuators, limit switches, pressure valves, and other sensors.

The human operator reacts to abnormal behaviour of the mining process plant (MPP) based on theoretical training and practical knowledge of the process plant or how it should work as business as usual (BAU) behaviour. The decision to stop production is not taken lightly, as the implications are costly. Therefore, the human operator always performs the best to keep the plant running at maximum capacity.

Cyber-physical systems (CPS), defined by the interaction between humans, cyberspace, and machines, are employed in smart grids, traffic controls, e-health, and smart infrastructures (Kriebel et al., 2018). The Industrial Control System (ICS) is also part of the CPS realm, presenting different features and concerns than the Information Technology (IT) realm.

2.2 Mining Process Plants (MPP)

MPPs vary depending on several characteristics, such as the mining type, mineral and final product required, and SCADA system. Therefore, a basic overview of these characteristics is presented to establish an environment for the MPP model proposed for the analysis in this chapter.

2.2.1 Mining

MPPs are usually designed based on the mining type where the mineral can be found, which dictates the type of processing plant: open-pit, water table, surface mining, and underground mining (Parreno, 2017; Yihdego & Drury, 2016). There are several types of minerals, as shown in Figure 2.1. Moreover, each type of mineral requires a specific process frame, such as drilling, crushing and screening, grinding, slurry, pyro, material handling, and compaction (Metso Corporation, 2018).

MPP can be complicated and designed in several stages. Figure 2.2 shows the generic concept of a process plant as a guideline to understand the basics of a process plant environment at a mining site. Table 2.1 presents the basic process concepts for an iron ore mining site. These processes are not mandatory and can be arranged in different scenarios, as required for the outcome of the production of the desired product.

Completing all these processes requires a complex process control interaction with the process plant infrastructure as temperature, chemicals, volume, high torque machines, loading and unloading machines, and handling tonnes of minerals must be precisely controlled (Table 2.1). The safety instrumented systems (SIS) run with the primary process control system and provide a fail-safe system to take over the primary system in the case of main system failure. Moreover, the fail-safe system must be up to date and tested to ensure that if the main process controls fail, the SIS will take over and guarantee that all systems fall back to a safe position and put no lives at risk.

Figure 2.1 Several Mineral Types Explored in the Mining Industry (Metso Corporation, 2018).
Figure 2.2 The Generic Concept of a Process Plant.
Table 2.1 Processes and Brief Descriptions

Process

	

Description




ROM pad

	

The crude mineral enters the process plant through the ROM Pad




Primary crushing

	

The mineral is crushed into the desired size for processing, and the coarse ore is collected




Screening

	

The required size is achieved during the secondary crushing stage, and any remaining fines can be collected




Material handling

	

Material handling is responsible for moving minerals across all processes, including overland and feeding conveyors, bins, train loadout, and all other equipment specific to each process or mining site




Train loadout

	

The train loadout system loads materials onto wagons for despatch to clients

The main components of a mine processing plant are illustrated in Figure 2.2, beginning with crude ore dropped into a crushing bin and ending with the final product delivered to a train loadout. The MPP begins by crushing and screening the ore, moving it to the scalp and screening it before the final products are shipped to clients or buyers by train. Not depicted in the diagram are additional utility services that support the processing plants operations and include power, water, and communications. Operators can control the processes and take action if critical plant systems, such as the SCADA network, fire alarm monitoring system, or, in some locations, a tornado system raise the alarm.

2.2.2 Overview of SCADA systems in MPP

SCADA systems generally show the process plant stages broken down into pages. The SCADA function informs the operator of how the plant is working and its status. Generally, one or more SCADA system pages capture each MPP process or stage. This page scheme is employed to facilitate the operator to follow the stage’s functionality or to track better the information presented on the page, which could come from sensors, instruments, manual inputs, and data recorded for comparison with previous stages or the operator’s shifts.

The generic plant diagram from Figure 2.2 would be shown on five or more pages, grouping the main instruments and systems. SCADA pages can vary from a simple follow-up on crushing and transporting ores in conveyors to a complex process of treating a mineral with mills, valves, instruments, pumps, pressure controls, and other devices that can add variables to the processes and vulnerable points to CPA. An alarm on the SCADA system page can require several levels of action from the operator. Table 2.2 presents the action levels the human operator is required to follow according to the alarm level. Figure 2.3 shows the concept of a SCADA alarm page and its alarm levels.

Table 2.2 The Action Level Required by the Human Operator According to the Alarm Level

Alarm Level

	

Human Operator Action




Minimal (green)

	

The operator acknowledges the alarm and leaves the plant running




Medium (orange)

	

The operator acknowledges the alarm and stops part of the plant affected by the anomaly that triggered the alarm, trying to isolate the attack and avoid letting the attacker reach the entire plant




Maximum (red)

	

The operator shut down the entire plant until the issue that triggered the alarm was verified and fixed

Figure 2.3 SCADA Alarm Page Visualisation.Note: This figure shows a SCADA alarm page concept. The shade of the alarm row can identify different threat levels to the MPP operation: no shade, less concerning, already treated, going up to heavily shaded as a more threatening issue, or a lightly shaded alarm that was not acknowledged and escalated to a deep shade.
2.3 Cyber-Physical Systems and Attacks
2.3.1 Cyber-physical attacks (CPA)

Cyber-physical systems (CPS) are vulnerable to both cyberattacks and physical attacks. When cyberattacks result in physical damage, they are classified as CPA (Yampolskiy et al., 2012). CPA can harm the CPS infrastructure in a process plant and lead to economic losses and fatalities in rare cases (Li et al., 2016). Understanding CPS vulnerabilities requires identification and measurement of situational awareness of virtual cyberthreats and local physical attacks.

Focusing on SCADA systems, Nicholson et al. (2012) analysed the role of SCADA systems in the cyber realm and its vulnerabilities. They broke down all components of possible cyber warfare manoeuvres that could target private companies, government, or the military. They explained that adversaries in OT are the same as in the IT environment, and the energy sector was more affected than others. Moreover, disgruntled employees were among the top adversaries responsible for cyberattacks. The consequences of cyberattacks on SCADA can be severe, causing loss of lives, financial loss of revenue, share price reduction, or even brand damage (Nicholson et al., 2012).

Nazir et al. (2017) warned that several vulnerabilities exist in the SCADA systems, such as the ones described in Table 2.3. Further, the poor administration of SCADA systems by their owners and the vendor’s responsibility to fix their products’ system vulnerabilities could be added to the list of vulnerabilities, even after they retire or stop the equipment sale (Nicholson et al., 2012). Do et al. (2017) and Nicholson et al. (2012) have identified various types of SCADA system threats that can be classified based either on the attack techniques used or specific system targets (Table 2.3).

Table 2.3 CPS Vulnerabilities and Threats (Do et al., 2017; Nazir et al., 2017)

Vulnerabilities

	

Threats by Attacking a Specific Target

	

Threats by Attack Technique



Generic operating systems
Legacy systems with long operational life
Multiple points of entry and failure
Communications protocols
Real-time and complex interactions
Conflicting priorities (patching)
Social engineering and insider attacks
Backdoors
Integral protection
	
Cyberattacks on the control centre where the SCADA servers and back-end infrastructure are hosted
Cyberattacks on a communication network – all interconnections between the different layers of the CPS
Physical attacks on the technical process – physically damaging the infrastructure or its components
	
Malware
Denial-of-service (DoS)
Network attacks
Main-in-the-middle attacks
False sequential logic (FSL) attacks
False data injection (FDI)
Integrity attacks

The literature presents several examples of these attacks, such as the FSL proposed by Li et al. (2016), where they explained that CPS work is based on a feedback response built on the sequence of commands and sensor readings. Therefore, an incorrect sequence of legitimate commands would disturb the sequence in which processes occur, which would cause damage to or interruption of the CPS.

2.4 Humans and Human Operators in Mining
2.4.1 Human operations in mining

Mining is a high-risk industry with high accident rates. A systematic review of research trends in mining accidents identified 57 studies related to mining accidents from 2015 to 2019 (Noraishah Ismail et al., 2021). Four main themes revealed were as follows:

The leading causes of mining accidents (46%).
Prevention of mining accidents (20%).
Challenges (17%).
Impacts (17%) of post-mining accidents.

The primary cause of mining accidents, accounting for more than half of the studies reviewed by Noraishah Ismail et al. (2021), was mechanical failure, followed by poor safety management and leadership issues. To prevent such accidents, recommended strategies include using software applications and models, training programmes, improved regulations, and supervision. Mine owners face challenges in ensuring safe work environments and dealing with the consequences of accidents. Most of the reviewed articles, totalling over 60%, focused on coal mining accidents in China. Other countries represented in the studies included Brazil, South Africa, Spain, Iran, the United States, India, and Turkey. Mining accidents harm human health, the environment, societies, governments, and economies, with significant environmental disasters such as Brazil’s 2015 Fundão tailings dam collapse (Noraishah Ismail et al., 2021).

The psychological pressure on Operators is anticipated as they oversee various systems while people work around and with them. This pressure arises from the potential threat to human lives from cyberattacks that could disrupt one or more of these systems, leading to mechanical failures in numerous equipment used in mining processes.

2.4.2 Human operator

The plant operator or control room operator, further referred to as the Operator, supervises the entire plant process or partial processes via the SCADA system. To become an operator, they must undergo technical training regarding the equipment and machinery environment in which they work. However, there is no training in cybersecurity or how to cope with the pressure of an imminent CPA. In addition, there is a mining social environment to be considered as isolation, as many mining sites are in remote areas, which can bring mental health issues.

The Operator is trained in the processes designed and implemented in the mining site plant and supports activities to keep the workplace environment safe. Some examples of training available in Australia are as follows:

Control Room Operator Course by Coal Services (Services, 2018)
Certificate II in Process Plant Operations (PMA20116) by TAFE (Australia, 2019)
Certificate III in Underground Metalliferous Mining by TAFE (Training, 2018)

Table 2.4 lists the units in PMA20116 related to IT/OT, which do not include any cybersecurity knowledge or skills required by the MPP. Most training programmes are designed to train the Operator to be competent in operating SCADA systems in a real-world environment. RMIT’s (2019) SCADA systems course and Nexus’ (2019) workshop are good examples.

The Operator monitors the SCADA HMI page in the control room. If an alarm is triggered, the HMI alarm page of the SCADA system alerts the operator that the process plant is in an abnormal operating state. The primary action of the operator is to identify the cause of the alarm. The operator’s decision on what to do next depends on two sources of information: their Subject Matter Expertise (SME) in the process plant’s operations and the information displayed on the HMI screen from the SCADA system. Operators typically trust their expertise, which is a combination of their job experience and training. However, operators may not always trust the information a SCADA system provides. As an SME in the process plant’s operation, the operator is not an expert in CBPS and, therefore, lacks the necessary knowledge to assess CPA, which is beyond the scope of their job description.

Table 2.4 Extract of the PMA20116 Process Plant Operations Certificate II Syllabus Related to IT/OT (Australia, 2019)

2.5 Human Psychological Challenges in Mining
2.5.1 Impact of cyberattacks on mental health

Bada and Nurse (2020) identified in their research that cyberattacks that increase threats can have significant social and psychological impacts. The critical issues examined included risk perception, protection motivation, culture of fear, and attacker/target characteristics. Real-world examples such as the WannaCry ransomware and Lloyds Bank cyberattacks were analysed to demonstrate the concepts.

Their principal findings, presented in Table 2.5, were associated with victims developing negative emotions like anxiety, trauma, and helplessness based on perceived control, as their response efficacy and self-efficacy shape threat perceptions and severity. Susceptibility to attacks was less than desirable or acceptable in their minds. Moreover, post-attack government communication and the attacker’s identity can influence public response and further damage the victim’s confidence.

When practitioners in different professions and industries are responsible for the confidentiality of their clients or patients, they are hit by an uncomfortable feeling of responsibility for the data and the risk of losing them. For example, the impact of cyberattacks on mental health in the digital mental health industry was investigated by Inkster et al. (2023).

Inkster et al. (2023) ponder the damage caused by the weight of such responsibility, as their research suggests that practitioners need to be aware of the potential risks of cyberattacks and take appropriate measures to protect client confidentiality, not only by storing them but also during virtual assessments. Furthermore, they transpose the same assessment to cybersecurity professionals, who also need to be aware of the potential risks of cyberattacks and take appropriate measures to protect their mental health.

Table 2.5 Findings from Gross et al. (2016) and Bada and Nurse (2020)

Impact

	

Title

	

Reference




Social

	

Disruption to daily lives, widespread anxiety, loss of confidence in technology

	

Bada and Nurse (2020)


	

Isolation or deprsession after financial losses

	
	

Erosion of trust in technology after attacks cause service disruptions

	
	

Demands for greater protection and regulation from government post-attack

	

Gross et al. (2016)




Psychological

	

Negative emotions like frustration, anger, anxiety, and helplessness

	

Bada and Nurse (2020)


	

Trauma, depression, grief, shame, and sense of violation after victimisation

	
	

Worry about potential life endangerment from critical infrastructure attacks

	
	

Loss of perceived control and learned helplessness

	
	

Paranoia, avoidance behaviours, and lack of confidence after cyberattacks

	

The psychological impact of a cyberattack can be tremendous, and the effects can be felt for weeks and months. Cyberattack victims may feel embarrassed, ashamed, and worried about what others think if they discover the details. Workplace security mistakes can lead to employment loss, which can devastate one’s mental and physical well-being. Avoidance is also common; if a person feels uncomfortable thinking about a cyberattack, they might use discomfort as an excuse to avoid improving their cybersecurity. Ignoring their feelings can prevent them from processing what happened and doing what they must to ensure that it does not happen again (Steel, 2022).

2.5.2 Human operator mental health

Pizarro and Fuenzalida (2021) presented findings that mining operations can significantly impact their workforce’s mental health and safety. After reviewing the relevant literature, it appears that managing these issues requires addressing four key themes: psychological and personal factors, psychosocial and health-related factors, physical well-being, and organisational factors. The research found that mine workers are at risk of various mental health issues, including depression, anxiety, stress, and PTSD.

The survey addressed literature from 2008 to 2019 in several countries such as Australia, Chile, China, and others that are not less critical while varying the mining activity from coal, copper, gold, diamond, and others that are not specified. Other variables were the research method applied, instruments, and sample qualification by gender and age (Pizarro & Fuenzalida, 2021).

This research suggests that mine workers are at risk of various mental health issues, and it is essential to identify and manage these risks to create a more mentally healthy workplace. To ensure their well-being, enterprises must address various factors, such as hazardous environments, work organisation, interpersonal relations, psychosocial risks, well-being, substance abuse, personality traits, and physical health.

2.6 Autonomous Cyber-Physical Security (CBPS)

Researchers have proposed different approaches to autonomous CBPS to replace the cybersecurity specialist in detecting CPA. The following research articles have presented the essential characteristics contributing to this chapter’s discussion.

Layal et al. (2016) proposed an autonomous SCADA system using discrete components and software using a wireless network to connect the sensors. The Autonomous SCADA System research is a straightforward example of how setting up and controlling thresholds can be used for process control supervision and automation of a process plant, as monitored abnormal behaviour can be used to alarm or trigger a response. The Recommender and the Anomaly Behaviour Analysis (ABA) showed one way to set up the interrelation between the assistant and the SCADA system.

Sayan et al. (2017) proposed a cybersecurity assistant based on machine learning. The assistant was trained to identify normal and abnormal behaviours using cyber situation analysis and recognition features by predicting attacks and employing cyberattack trend and impact analysis modules. This solution employed IDS detection based on network traffic to analyse abnormal behaviour, which could not identify FSL and FDI attacks.

The Behavioural Observation for Critical Infrastructure Security Support (BOCISS) proposed by Hurst et al. (2013) is an independent agent from the SCADA system that collects data from the SCADA system through network access to the plant equipment. When abnormal behaviour is identified, a data manager collects and distributes data to BOCISS, classifying, storing, preparing, and presenting the alarm to the operator.

A self-protecting SCADA system model introduced by Chen et al. (2016), which uses function code scanning and malicious parameter injection attacks to validate self-protecting features, was proposed. A dataset created in real time from the system behaviour is sent to the intrusion estimation module (IEM), which predicts the expected behaviour and then compares it to the system’s actual behaviour. When deviations from the predicted value are found, warnings are sent to the multi-criteria controller (MAC), which triggers a response to a possible attack.

A novel intrusion tolerance scheme for a networked control system was proposed by Huang et al. (2015) as a case study in the Ocarina Architecture Analysis and Design Language (AADL) environment. Three types of injected attacks were claimed to be defended by the proposed scheme.

A System-Aware Cybersecurity Architecture proposed by Jones and Horowitz (2012) combines techniques, such as multiple vendors, to create diversity, configuration hopping, data consistency checking, and tactical forensics. The system works through a real-time collection of information to assess the integrity of the plant and then analyses the information collected and acts with controls to protect the system, if required. A case study was proposed using a nuclear plant, where an examination was presented considering the pre-attack, trans-attack, and post-attack phases.

The literature discussed above explores the evolution of cybersecurity solutions, from simple microcontrollers to fully developed machine learning operators. The gaps in these six types of research highlight the need for a more comprehensive solution, as the operator is a crucial component of the CPS. They are responsible for detecting attacks on the CPS and taking the necessary action to mitigate any potential attacks and protect the CPS. Thus, investigating and mitigating attacks are procedures a human operator trained in troubleshooting procedures for SCADA systems would perform based on their training in response to a SCADA alarm.

2.7 Autonomous Operator for Cyber-Physical Systems

The role of the human operator is a crucial but often neglected aspect of the intricate field of safeguarding mining process plants from cyber-physical attacks. Although the existing literature has mainly concentrated on automated methods that employ sensor data, simulated situations, and network analysis, there is a lack of comprehension and integration of the human element.

This section aims to explore the diverse obstacles faced by human operators in the context of mining operations and to recognise their indispensable role in the face of a continuously evolving threat environment. It delves into the relationship between psychological stress, operational risks, and cyberthreats and proposes incorporating autonomous agents to bridge the gap between cybersecurity knowledge and the complex workings of mining process plants.

The focus of protection measures against cyber-physical attacks for mining process plants has mainly been on automated methods that depend on data from various sensors, simulations, and network traffic analysis. Although these strategies promise to strengthen system security, there is a noticeable gap in the field: the limited incorporation of the human operator. As technology progresses, it is vital to address the human element, which includes cognitive complexities, vulnerabilities, and crucial roles that have been relatively disregarded in current research.

Human Operators in an MPP must contend with the tangible dangers associated with their job and deal with the dual pressures of psychological stress and the constant threat of cyberattacks. The convergence of these pressures creates a complex environment where the human operator is not only expected to navigate the intricate complexities of mining operations but also to bear the responsibility of protecting against potential cyber breaches, which adds to the existing stressors.

Improving the relationship between cybersecurity and the practical requirements of mining operations calls for a change in how we think about the situation. One emerging solution is using autonomous agents, in this case, an Autonomous Operator (AO), that can bridge the gap between cybersecurity and the real-time demands of mining process plants. The AO has the potential to enhance the abilities of the human operator by serving as an intermediary that combines extensive cybersecurity knowledge with the intricacies of mining operations, thereby protecting against physical cyberattacks.

The Autonomous Operator (AO) would react to the alarms presented by the SCADA system, reproducing the procedure that would be followed by a human operator trained in troubleshooting procedures in SCADA systems to decipher the cause of the alarm. If the cause of the alarm is deemed a malicious attack, the AO will collect information from all layers of the MPP, normalising the information in a way that the AO can process. Once the AO can ingest the information, the classification would determine if CPA caused the abnormal behaviour. When CPA is identified, the AO communicates to the human operator via the SCADA HMI that the cause of the abnormal behaviour is CPA.

The AO may lift the burden from the Operator to identify or be concerned with CPA. With that purpose and its effects, the mental health issues caused by the continued concern of the operation’s disruption and blame if successful CPA occurs can be reduced and, with time, eliminated, giving back to the Operator their confidence. Furthermore, the AO can work as a machine companion to learn from and double-check actions over time. The AO does not mean to be a cybersecurity specialist; it aims to augment the Operator’s skills. However, its assessment is second to none because it is a machine learning algorithm prioritising access to specific information.

The AO needs to trust the information sent to it to create independent connections between the AO and critical information points in the proposed process plant model. Even with separate connections, information gathered by the AO can be compromised. If an attacker identifies these connections, an attempt to interfere with or alter the data collected by the AO could occur, which could mislead the AO’s classification.

2.7.1 A Theoretical CPS model for an autonomous operator

Figure 2.4 presents a theoretical CPS model high-level block diagram of a typical MPP block diagram to represent the CPS on the MPP for implementing AO in an MPP. The AO will be independently connected, through a firewall, to the critical points of the MPP to collect data without relying on the SCADA network, which could be compromised during CPA. The direct access to critical points, such as PLCs, HMI stations, legacy and SCADA servers, allows the AO to compare the data in different stages of the processes. Hence, the AO can identify where the data or the process was altered or attacked. The data flow typically goes from the valve/actuator to the PLC, is visualised or handled by the SCADA system, and is recorded to the historian server.

Figure 2.4 The Conceptual Design of the CPS Model Proposed.

Two significant advantages of an AO in an MPP are as follows:

Troubleshooting and conducting investigations
Truly autonomous

The SCADA system operator knows how to troubleshoot the machines and devices in the specific process plant where they work, which allows them to investigate and fix issues that may arise. Operators do not need to be engineers to verify the abnormal behaviour of processes and operations. They can physically go to the field and check the machines and devices in person per their training procedures or use several tools to troubleshoot systems. Physical malicious acts or equipment failures can be identified by visual inspection.

The AO will be trained to understand how the presented process plant model usually operates and learn the operator troubleshooting procedures. Once a SCADA alarm is triggered, the AO will understand that abnormal behaviour is occurring. The AO will collect data from the CPS to determine what causes the abnormal behaviour, which could be classified as CPA or ordinary faults such as system or device failure.

If the cause of the alarm is deemed a malicious attack, the AO will collect information from all layers of the MPP. When CPA is identified, the AO communicates to the Operator via the SCADA HMI.

2.8 Conclusions

The effects of cybersecurity concerns and the fear of being hacked on people’s mental health have been widely investigated through research. Depression, anxiety, and panic attacks were found to be some of the illnesses and symptoms associated with the pressure of dealing with personal and professional life environments and responsibilities. Additionally, the isolation at the mining site and continuous worries about safety and financial losses may exacerbate the mental health issues experienced by the Operator.

Proposed solutions for autonomous or aware CPS have varied in their approach to the Operator’s participation. Some have suggested eliminating the Operator’s involvement, while others have sought to minimise it. However, most of these solutions focus on transforming the Operator into a cybersecurity expert, which would be costly and shift their primary responsibility from maintaining the process plant’s availability and functionality to monitoring cybersecurity. Conversely, the AO aims to expand the Operator’s capabilities without requiring additional knowledge or attention towards cybersecurity.

The research review revealed gaps in the operator’s role and mental health state. This study suggests an AO to enhance the operator’s capabilities rather than create a cybersecurity expert. The AO will be trained using abnormal operation behaviour, which refers to the logical sequence of expected processes and events in the plant’s operations. Research focusing on network traffic monitoring may not effectively capture the Operator’s domain knowledge. Therefore, an AO that combines cybersecurity knowledge with the Operator’s skillset could be a more suitable solution.

It is important to note that alarms in the SCADA system may result from attacks or fake data injection, leading to false information. Future work should include evaluating the impact of the AO on the Operator’s mental health and alleviating the burden of cybersecurity intricacies. Additionally, improvements to the AO’s machine learning engine should be made to increase the accuracy of correct CPA identification over false positives. Once the AO is familiar with the MPP’s operations and processes, standard metrics will be established for a comprehensive survey and comparison of results.
