Chapter 3 Building cognitive resilience for enhanced cyber governance

Marthie Grobler and Tooba Aamir

DOI: 10.1201/9781032664859-3

3.1 Introduction

In an online world, cybersecurity is often pictured as a computer or a keyboard with a locked padlock and potentially some asterisks to give the impression of data privacy. In phishing-specific imagery, you often see a laptop with a physical fishing rod reaching either in or out of the laptop to hook a credit card or a personal identification card. Cyberthreats are often pictured as a dark hoodie-clad figure hunching behind a laptop or strings of bits and bytes in white, yellow, or green text on a dark background, with the word ‘password’ in red text. These imageries are all very typical of cybersecurity representation. These imageries are also far removed from how people experience and perceive cybersecurity and cyberthreats.

People generally associate cybersecurity with a password or lock combination, an antivirus programme, and some form of multi-factor authentication. In a simplified form, people believe these measures are sufficient to reach that locked padlock state of cybersecurity. On an organisational level, an added firewall and intrusion detection system may be considered sufficient cybersecurity protection. There is also some kind of disconnect, with people often disassociating cybersecurity with other smart devices such as mobile phones, tablets, smart-home speakers and smart TVs, and believing that cybersecurity is only something that impacts their laptop or desktop. That is one of the primary reasons why people can very easily fall for social engineering attacks because this attack type relies on human interaction and does not fit within the typical hoodie-clad figure imagery.

It is very difficult for humans to comprehend these intangible concepts. In many cases, humans take on the attitude that if they cannot see it, it is not real, so they ignore it. That makes it very easy to exploit human nature and human psychology. The fact that cyberthreats and attacks are ever-changing makes it even more difficult for humans to keep up and easier for attackers to catch somebody off-guard. That is why there is a need to focus on cyber resilience. Humans must be cognitively resilient to govern their cybersecurity successfully – the human brain is built on knowing and understanding, learning and thinking, and adapting. It is within human nature to be cognitively resilient, and by channelling this correctly, humans should be able to govern their cybersecurity efficiently. Therefore, a shift is needed from the adage that humans are the weakest link. Humans become increasingly vulnerable as they merge with technology and cyberspace, exposing themselves to various intangible threats and risks (Grobler et al., 2021). As such, a deeper understanding of human behaviour and behavioural economics will provide an alternative lens on cybersecurity governance. Where cybersecurity solutions often focus solely on technical or operational mechanisms or human-centred solutions, converging technical and operational, strategic and human-centred solutions that overlay user competencies on various skill sets and abilities is much needed to build cognitive resilience for enhanced cybersecurity governance.

Humans need to know what they are doing to manage their cybersecurity. Humans need to understand what can go wrong, be willing to learn from their (and others’) experiences, and think under pressure to navigate the digital world successfully. With the ability to effectively adjust to challenging experiences, humans can easily adapt to the fast-moving digitisation of the world and thrive in a world that requires them to have their cognitive wits about them. This chapter explores how cognitive resilience can be built to ensure enhanced cybersecurity governance. We will examine how cybersecurity attacks and events aim to exploit human psychology in the context of governance and how humans’ innate resilience and cognitive abilities can help them navigate these events. We will look at triggers that adversaries can manipulate to breach security and at humans’ understanding of complex connections that can help them to think under pressure. We will discuss how these tactics compromise decision-making processes, data sharing, and citizen engagement and could contribute to the potential erosion of public trust, compromise sensitive data, and disruptions in governance operations. We will investigate the role and effectiveness of awareness campaigns that harness psychological insights to empower citizens or executives with the knowledge and skills to recognise, resist, and report social engineering attempts. Simply put, we will explore how humans think and act under pressure and how this impacts their cybersecurity standing, either positively or negatively.

3.2 Human Psychology and Cognitive Abilities

Psychology is the scientific study of the human mind and behaviour. It explores how we perceive, remember, and communicate and investigates complex psychological disorders and cultural influences on cognition. The study of human psychology and cognitive abilities is a fundamental branch of psychology that deals with the intricate workings of the human mind and behaviour. It focuses on understanding the underlying mental processes and mechanisms that govern human cognition. Cognitive psychologists employ experimental methods to unravel these facets of human cognition, and integrating cognitive neuroscience facilitates a deeper understanding of the neural basis of cognitive functions.

The significance of cognitive psychology extends to advancing our understanding of human behaviour. It provides invaluable insights into the cognitive processes that steer our actions, decisions, and interactions with the world. Beyond its foundational importance, cognitive psychology has far-reaching implications, contributing to various branches of psychology, including clinical psychology, developmental psychology, and social psychology (Núñez et al., 2019). This multidisciplinary relevance underscores the imperative nature of comprehending the intricacies of human cognition as it finds applications in diverse fields, ranging from educational methods and the treatment of cognitive disorders to enhancing user experience in technology design.

Originating in the mid-20th century, cognitive psychology has profoundly impacted our understanding of how individuals perceive, think, learn, and ultimately make decisions (Fleming, 2019). Cognitive psychology embodies several vital principles and concepts that serve as the cornerstones of this discipline. Specifically, it considers how humans process information. Cognitive psychologists often adopt the information processing model, which draws a parallel between the human mind and a computer. This model speculates that the mind receives information, processes it, and generates responses based on this processing. It considers the ability to retain and divide attention. It considers how individuals allocate their mental resources to various stimuli in their environment and the role of attention in the perception of the world, short-term and long-term memory and subsequent forgetting. Cognitive psychology plays a sizeable role in language and communication, including language structure and comprehension. Finally, it considers problem-solving, decision-making, and how people navigate challenges (O’Reilly & Munakata, 2000).

The principles and concepts within this field form the basis for our understanding of human cognition, with far-reaching applications in various domains of science and practical endeavours. In essence, cognitive psychology represents an exploration of the cognitive processes that define our interactions with the world, shaping our thoughts, actions, and decisions.

3.3 Digital World and Cybersecurity Governance

Digitisation is complicating our understanding of human behaviour. People cannot keep up with technological changes, and their main focus is often not on cybersecurity per se but instead on their primary goal. For example, somebody using a search engine focuses on finding more information on what they are looking for, not the potential cyber dangers. Similarly, somebody using a mobile phone to navigate the train timetable focuses on getting from point A to point B and not looking for potential malicious website redirection. The rapid digital transformation of the current economy and society is causing significant cybersecurity gaps that increase the risk associated with cyber incidents across different economic areas. It is, therefore, necessary to upskill and upscale cybersecurity governance in this digital world.

Cybersecurity governance is a comprehensive strategy that integrates critical functions and supporting enablers to prevent and manage cyberthreats and cyberattacks. Building on the NIST (2023) cybersecurity framework, functions may include identification, protection, detection, response, and recovery-related actions. Examples of these functions include identifying digital assets, their risk profiles and current cybersecurity posture, and the protection of these assets by implementing appropriate risk mitigation mechanisms and system hardening, either through technical or procedural changes (NIST, 2023). Functions may include detection-specific activities, such as log analysis and network scanning, to identify any anomalous device or network behaviour; response functions may include immediate remediation activities to contain any threats and limit the immediate consequences, whilst recovery-related activities focus on more long-term consequences and impact management. A cybersecurity governance framework is needed to ensure adequate consideration is given to covering the entire spectrum of the organisation’s cyber needs through a single integrated framework that separates cyber governance from cyber management while meeting stakeholder needs and enabling data sharing (ISACA, 2012).

These functions are supported by enablers that provide the structure and support for the organisation to implement the governance functions. The enablers include policies, frameworks, legislation, processes, organisational structures, culture, ethics, behaviour, information, services, infrastructure, applications, people, skills, and competencies (ISACA, 2012). Policies, frameworks, and legislation may include organisational information security policies, national-level legislation such as the Australian Privacy Act 1988, and international frameworks and best practices such as the ISO/IEEE 27000 series. Processes may be very organisation-specific, such as detailing the steps for user authentication or how to report a stolen or misplaced laptop. Organisational structures may include the constitution of a board committee or an information security steering committee, appointing a Chief Information Security Officer, or using an external security consulting service. Culture, ethics, and behaviour relate to top-down cybersecurity enforcement, bottom-up staff involvement, the visibility of an active cybersecurity champion, and staff’s attitude to cybersecurity-related reporting. Information is the basis of communication and critical to process flows and operationalisation. Services, infrastructure, and applications are the hardware, firmware, software, and middleware needed to process and commoditise the information. Finally, people refer to the users and beneficiaries of the information and services with the skills and competencies needed to run the business and cybersecurity functions.

Cybersecurity governance is needed to create structure and provide tangible elements in an otherwise chaotic, intangible cyber domain. A cybersecurity governance framework is a mechanism used to help humans manage cybersecurity in a way that aims to bridge some of the gaps created by digitisation and upscale cybersecurity management in an otherwise non-cyber focused organisational environment. A cybersecurity governance framework can be customised to characterise and evaluate the maturity level of organisations across critical domains. Although cybersecurity functions are traditionally only used in large organisational settings, the basic principles are the same regardless of organisational context, size, or sector. They can be adapted to any maturity level. Such a framework can be used to evaluate the organisation’s current and aspirational target cybersecurity profiles according to the framework. It can be applied to increase the cybersecurity culture, set the priorities of investment in information security, and measure the implementation progress. It can also identify and evaluate latent cybersecurity risks, including understanding the probability of occurrence and possible impact on the organisation’s operational continuity.

A cybersecurity governance framework is a risk-based approach to managing cybersecurity risks in an increasingly digitised world. The framework provides a common language for understanding, managing, and expressing cybersecurity risk to internal and external stakeholders. It can be used to help identify and prioritise actions for reducing cybersecurity risk, and it is a tool for aligning policy, business, and technological approaches to managing that risk. Such a framework intends to understand cybersecurity through a methodical business lens (particularly through the governance functions that categorise security aspects into logical blocks of actions). Similarly, the governance framework facilitates a better understanding of the business functions and operations. This is done through the system mapping and interconnectivity processes uncovered during the specific cybersecurity functions’ activities and the framework enablers’ structures and processes. Implementing a cybersecurity governance framework can support the development of cognitive resilience. Appreciating human behaviour affects governance and can lead to enhanced cybersecurity.

3.4 Social Engineering and Human Psychology

Social engineering, a sophisticated criminal technique, manipulates individuals to reveal confidential information or perform actions contrary to their usual behaviour (Hadnagy, 2010). This section delves into the psychological underpinnings of social engineering, crucial for recognising and defending against its tactics, thereby enhancing resilience to manipulation by malicious actors. The discussion encompasses a range of deceptive techniques exploiting human psychology and trust, emphasising the exploitation of vulnerabilities rather than relying solely on technical exploits.

The effectiveness of social engineering hinges on its exploitation of human psychology, with malicious actors understanding and manipulating cognitive and emotional responses to achieve their objectives (Uebelacker & Quiel, 2014). Fundamental principles influencing human behaviour and trust, such as influence, persuasion, authority, and cognitive biases, play a pivotal role. Psychological principles like reciprocity, commitment, social proof, authority, and scarcity are utilised to manipulate targets through influence and persuasion, creating situations where compliance with requests or trust in authority is expected. This section also explores social engineers’ specific exploitation of trust and authority, including impersonating trusted figures or entities and manipulating cognitive biases to align information with targets’ existing beliefs.

The evolving landscape of social engineering, adapting to changing technologies and circumstances, makes it a pervasive cybersecurity threat (Wang et al., 2021). Unlike traditional computer attacks, social engineering focuses on exploiting human vulnerabilities, allowing attackers to bypass security measures without directly engaging with technical defences. The expansion of the cyber environment, including avenues like email, mobile phones, and the Internet of Things (IoT), further intensifies the social engineering threat. Open office environments, BYOD policies, and remote work have expanded the attack surface for social engineers (Wang et al., 2020).

The availability of open-source intelligence simplifies information gathering and facilitates crafting more credible and targeted social engineering attacks (Abraham & Chengalur-Smith, 2010). Prevalent techniques, including pretexting, impersonation of authority figures, and fabricating emergencies, capitalise on human vulnerabilities, creating avenues for attacks. Integrating technologies like machine learning and artificial intelligence are expected to enhance the efficiency and aggressiveness of social engineering attacks, making targeted, large-scale, and automated attacks a genuine possibility. Thus, social engineering has evolved into a persistent and universal security threat.

In an era marked by technological progress, the human element in securing digital landscapes gains significance. Cognitive psychology, dedicated to exploring mental processes, emerges as a pivotal force in reshaping cybersecurity strategies (Pfleeger & Caputo, 2013). This transformative role extends beyond technological alignment, forming a foundational framework for anticipating and countering cyberthreats. Integrating cognitive psychology into cybersecurity emphasises human factors, delving into human vulnerabilities, trust dynamics, and cognitive biases.

This section further examines how cybersecurity attacks exploit human vulnerabilities through psychological approaches, impacting trust dynamics and cognitive biases. For instance, cognitive psychology provides insights into how individuals may succumb to phishing attacks due to innate tendencies, like the inclination to trust familiar sources. Recognising these vulnerabilities is crucial in designing cybersecurity measures that shield against external threats while accounting for inherent weaknesses in human cognition. A human-centric approach places skilled individuals at the core of security concepts, with technology supporting human capabilities throughout the entire technology life cycle.

The decisions made by individuals, whether end users or cybersecurity professionals, profoundly impact the security posture of digital environments. Cognitive biases, information processing models, and external stimuli’s influence on decision-making become critical analysis points in crafting effective cybersecurity strategies. The human-centric approach advocated by cognitive psychology addresses the multifaceted challenges presented by human vulnerabilities, trust dynamics, and cognitive biases. Embracing a multidisciplinary perspective that integrates insights from cognitive psychology enhances the technical robustness of security measures and strengthens cybersecurity’s adaptive and resilient nature. This approach acknowledges the constant interplay between human factors and technological advancements within the evolving governance context, providing a cornerstone in crafting strategies that navigate the intricate interplay of human cognition in the ever-evolving digital landscape. To illustrate the relationship between human vulnerabilities, trust dynamics and cognitive biases, consider the following examples.

3.4.1 Example 1: malicious actors use whaling attack to defraud bank of us$75.8 Million

In 2016, malicious actors targeted a high-ranking executive of the Belgian Bank Crelan with a spear-phishing email. The attack focused on exploiting weaknesses in the institution’s internal communication channels and employee training programmes. The malicious actors created convincing scenarios that prompted employees to disclose sensitive information, including login credentials and financial transaction details. The malicious actors accessed sensitive financial information and transaction records by exploiting a vulnerability in the institution’s email system. This breach exposed weaknesses in the organisation’s cybersecurity infrastructure, revealing gaps in employee training, communication protocols, and system vulnerabilities. None of the bank’s clients were affected, and the bank’s reserve covered the loss (Cimpanu, 2016).

Human Vulnerabilities: The malicious actors targeted the organisation’s internal communication platforms and financial databases, using a combination of pretexting and impersonation to deceive employees. They relied on information processing by the targeted individual and influenced their perception of their immediate surroundings and decision-making by leveraging urgency and perceived authority to create a stressed environment that required immediate action.
Trust Dynamics: By impersonating high-ranking executives and employing tactics such as authority and urgency, the malicious actors gained access to critical financial systems. The email used legitimate graphics and a lookalike domain name to fool the targeted individual and have them transfer the money without double-checking with somebody from inside the organisation first.
Cognitive Biases: The malicious actors leveraged cognitive biases, such as trust in authority figures, to convincingly pose as legitimate personnel, making it challenging for employees to discern the malicious intent. The malicious actors strategically manipulated these biases to navigate the organisation’s security measures, emphasising the critical role of understanding human behaviour in cybersecurity.
3.4.2 Example 2: malicious actors use AI voice impersonation to defraud organisation of US$243,000

In 2019, malicious actors used AI deepfake technology to generate a voice impersonation of a chief executive to social engineer the CEO of a UK-based energy organisation. The malicious actor, pretending to be the CEO’s boss, demanded an immediate transfer of US$243,000 to a supplier. Since this was outside the standard operational guidelines, the impersonator falsely assured the CEO that the amount would be reimbursed. After the money was transferred to the specified Hungarian-based supplier, the money was transferred again to a Mexico-based account and then to further locations, making identifying the malicious actors and tracking the funds more difficult. The malicious actors made second and third attempts to engage the original victim, but these attempts were unsuccessful (Trend Micro, 2019).

Human Vulnerabilities: The CEO was easily influenced and persuaded by the voice impersonation of their boss. It was reported that the CEO ‘recognised the subtle German accent in his boss’s voice – and that it carried the man’s “melody” ’ (Damiani, 2019). People are inclined to readily accept direct orders from their line managers as risk acceptance is perceived on a higher authority level. The malicious actors employed several social engineering techniques, including pretexting, impersonating an authority figure, and fabricating an emergency or critical situation.
Trust Dynamics: The CEO trusted the chief executive’s authority as a senior figure within the organisation. Initially, there was no distrust; therefore, the CEO felt no need to verify the caller’s identity. This trust was broken after the transferred funds were not reimbursed, leading to unsuccessful second and third attempts to social engineer the same individual.
Cognitive Biases: The CEO believed that the voice impersonation represented their boss, influencing their response to the out-of-procedure funds transfer request, intending to support their boss and appear to be a good employee. The malicious actors leveraged the perceived risk compensation bias to build trust with the targeted individual.

These examples demonstrate different social engineering techniques using different technology platforms but intending to leverage a perceived authoritative trust to persuade unsuspecting individuals to forego the proper procedure. Although not specified in the examples, it can be assumed that both organisations had some procedure stipulating the authorities needed to transfer funds. However, since the human element was targeted, none of these procedures were presumably followed since various cognitive factors unduly influenced the humans’ decision-making. For example, the employee may have wanted to impress the boss by actioning any direct tasks immediately and effectively. The employee may have assumed that the boss would not suggest anything illegal or violate the organisational policy and assumed that any direct tasks would be acceptable. If the employee had any reservations about the process, they may have assumed risk transfer in that the order came from a more senior individual, and therefore the more senior individual would be responsible for any adverse outcomes. Social engineers can easily identify possible cognitive biases through reconnaissance of a targeted organisation. In addition, social engineers often develop multiple pretext scenarios that will enable them to manipulate individuals regardless of their engagement, decision-making, or trust levels. In these cases, the impact of these social engineering attacks was profound, resulting in significant financial losses and reputational damage, both for the organisation and the involved individual. The impact of these attacks was not limited to immediate financial losses but also extended to the erosion of trust among customers and stakeholders. Regarding the targeted individual, various factors would contribute to how their experience would shape their future cyber resilience. In the ideal environment, this individual would become a cybersecurity champion who shares their story and advocates for better cybersecurity governance that considers human behaviour. However, in some cases, the individual may be ostracised and shunned from an organisational perspective, affecting their cybersecurity standing to such an extent that they become entirely disengaged.

3.4.3 Example 3: malicious actors exploit human nature to defraud australians of more than AU$7.2 Million

In 2022, Australians have lost more than AU$7.2 million in family impersonation text scams. The malicious actors generally send a message, usually via WhatsApp or SMS, posing as a friend or family member who has lost their phone or got a new number. There are different variations, but the malicious actors generally fabricate a story related to them being locked out of the bank account and asking the victim to send money to them. According to the Australian Competition and Consumer Commission, more than 11,000 incidents were reported in Australia in 2022. Although any mobile number can receive these fraudulent texts, the scam targets women and older individuals (Bahr, 2023).

Human Vulnerabilities: This attack is grounded purely in human nature and banking on the fact that a mum would do whatever she could to help her child. It also leverages the failures of human–technology interaction, as many people would have had to borrow somebody else’s mobile phone in a pinch to send a message or make a call in an emergency.
Trust Dynamics: The victim trusts that their family or friends will reach out to them when in need. Given the demographics of the targeted victims, these individuals are not necessarily considered tech-savvy and will automatically assume that the message is legitimate and would not consider verifying the texter’s identity.
Cognitive Biases: Over two-thirds of family impersonation scams have been reported by women over 55 years of age, so scammers are combining the fight or flight adrenaline rush with familial behavioural patterns and exploiting the fact that people will step up to protect their families.

This example highlights that human vulnerabilities can be exploited in any circumstance and are not restricted solely to work environments. Cognitive biases are present in all levels of mental reasoning, regardless of whether it is a work or personal environment. Trust dynamics are equally prevalent in both environments, specifically personal ones, since human relations are often more private and individualised. Although the financial impact is often insignificant in the bigger scheme of things, falling victim to such a scam could be devastating for an individual due to the loss of money and the perceived breach of trust relationship with the impersonated individual. In addition, victims of these scams often develop new negative cognitive biases against the benefits of technology and often never regain complete trust in the digitised world again.

3.5 Public Trust and Citizen Engagement

Exploring the implications for public trust and citizen engagement is essential in social engineering. The tactics utilised in social engineering attacks can potentially undermine decision-making processes, compromise the sharing of critical data, and disrupt the level of citizen engagement. This, in turn, directly threatens the functioning of governance operations and the credibility of public institutions. Successful social engineering attacks can erode public trust, leading to increased scepticism and reduced engagement. Additionally, compromising sensitive data jeopardises individuals and organisations and threatens the confidentiality and security that citizens expect from the relevant organisations. These issues underscore the need for robust security measures and public awareness to protect public trust and citizen engagement in an increasingly prevalent era of digital threats. This section will discuss how these tactics compromise decision-making processes, data sharing, and citizen engagement.

Cybersecurity incidents, especially those that occur on a large scale, significantly impact the reputational perception of the involved organisation. Not only will the users directly affected by the incident be negatively associating the organisation with their financial losses and changed social standing, but through cognitive biases, these negative associations are often transferred to other individuals whom the specific cybersecurity incidents may not have directly impacted. As described in the Situational Crisis Communication Theory, the attributions of crisis responsibility significantly affect how people perceive an organisation’s reputation in crisis and their affective and behavioural responses to that organisation following the crisis (Kuipers & Schonheit, 2021). Through social connectedness, people often empathise with the negative experiences shared by others and are accordingly influenced by these negative impacts. Furthermore, the power of social media sentiment is a powerful tool that can influence people towards a specific state of trust or mistrust in an organisation. It, therefore, remains an evolving challenge to actively engage citizens and build their trust in cybersecurity, especially if previous cyber incidents have broken their trust. To illustrate the relationship between public trust and citizen engagement, consider the following examples.

3.5.1 Example 4: authentication gaps: losing user trust to malicious exploits

In 2022, malicious actors breached Optus’s security, affecting up to 10 million current and former customers and over a third of the Australian population. The breach was effected through an application programming interface (API) available online with no authorisation or authentication required – anybody with the URL could access the data (Taylor, 2022). This breach resulted in a lawsuit that claims Optus breached consumer and telecommunications law and failed in its duty of care to protect users from harm.

Public Trust: Optus claimed in their media statements that a sophisticated attack caused the data breach. However, it has been shown that the lack of authentication controls on the API was a very trivial cybersecurity omission. Optus’ lack of care in basic cybersecurity measures significantly damaged public trust. They acknowledged that the public’s trust in the organisation had been broken and confirmed that an independent external review was crucial in rebuilding customer trust (ABC, 2022a).
Citizen Engagement: The incident and Optus’ handling of the event caused a lot of mistrust and disengagement amongst customers, as evidenced by negative sentiments on social media accounts and other public forums. One month after the cyber incident, 10% of affected users left the network for competitor networks, with an additional 56% considering a change (Sharples, 2022). Optus has been criticised for handling the incident, and users have been very outspoken about their future engagement with the organisation.
3.5.2 Example 5: credential stuffing, blaming users for data theft

In 2023, malicious actors reused old usernames and passwords from other websites (known as credential stuffing) to break into DNA testing organisation 23andMe’s database, stealing the personal information of nearly 7 million customers. The malicious actors used this information to find the impacted customers’ genetic relatives and family tree information through an additional opt-in feature (Lyngaas, 2023). Following the breach’s discovery, 23andMe instructed affected users to change their passwords and later rolled out two-factor authentication for all its customers (MacDonald, 2023). 23andMe is blaming wholly on the customers who negligently used recycled username and password combinations as login credentials for their accounts on the organisation’s website. Their stock dropped more than 8% after this announcement (Revell, 2024).

Public Trust: Although some of the user accounts were breached due to users’ poor password hygiene, many additional user accounts were accessed due to the additional opt-in feature selected by some users – these accounts were not breached due to poor password hygiene. Given 23andMe’s response to the current lawsuit by plaintiffs affected by this breach, the public trust in the organisation has been broken. The organisation also only instituted two-factor authentication after the incident; considering the sensitive nature of the data they access, this should have been an essential security measure. The possibility exists that personal trust relationships are impacted due to familial connections identified inadvertently by the breached data.
Citizen Engagement: Given the personal nature of the data loss (including genetic markers and familial data), it is expected that citizens affected by this breach would become very disengaged in pursuing online interactions. Any digital aspect related to biometric factors would no longer be considered secure for these individuals. There is a possibility that affected users would opt for better password hygiene in future, not reusing passwords, possibly scrutinising the security of other websites where they have reused passwords and implementing two-factor authentication on more accounts.

These examples highlight that cybersecurity incidents often negatively impact public and personal trust relationships. In terms of citizen engagement, the companies involved in the breach may experience a sharp reduction in citizen engagement, whilst the affected users might inversely become more engaged in their cybersecurity considerations for other platforms and services. In addition, the two examples show diverse public framing of the incidents and the impact thereof on the organisation’s reputation. Framing relates crises to different degrees of attributed responsibility. It corresponds to minimal crisis responsibility (victim crisis is outside of the organisation’s control, such as natural disasters or employee misbehaviour), low-moderate crisis responsibility (accidental crisis through an unfortunate series of events), and high crisis responsibility (preventable crisis where the organisation either intentionally caused the crisis or could have avoided the occurrence but failed to do so). The more an organisation is perceived to be accountable for a crisis, the more its reputation will suffer, as it will no longer be trusted to adequately prevent or respond to crises (Kuipers & Schonheit, 2021). In Example 4, the organisation claimed a sophisticated attack, which was a fundamental security omission. In Example 5, the organisation turned on their customers and shifted all the blame. In both instances, the organisations could have better utilised an understanding of cognitive psychology to guide their use of language and communication in a way that would have influenced their customers more positively towards them and actively re-engaged them whilst building trust. In addition, a cybersecurity governance model might have supported them in better handling the consequence management of the events and the subsequent recovery processes.

3.6 Cyber and Cognitive Resilience Building

To adequately prepare for and address cybersecurity incidents, the focus should shift towards the critical cyber and cognitive resilience domains within cybersecurity. This section explores how cognitive resilience can build a cyber immunity that can be adopted in modern cyber governance frameworks. We investigate cognitive resilience that can be strategically incorporated into modern cyber governance frameworks, allowing for a proactive approach to mitigate the ever-evolving cyberthreats that confront organisations and individuals and establish robust cyber immunity. By assessing these dimensions, we aim to underscore the pivotal role that cognitive resilience plays in enhancing cybersecurity strategies and fortifying defences against the multifaceted challenges posed by cyber incidents in the digital landscape.

Within the human-centric cybersecurity domain, the focus is on all things of cybersecurity where humans are involved, particularly on human involvement in the system and processes. That is, understanding how humans represent value but risk to an organisation, how humans and computers interact, and what risks are introduced due to these interactions. This human-centric cybersecurity focus helps build cognitive resilience by looking at cybersecurity with a behavioural lens and considering cybersecurity’s user, usage, and usability aspects (Grobler et al., 2021) as a means towards true cyber and cognitive resilience. It is part of human nature to be cognitively resilient, and by channelling this correctly, humans should be able to govern their cybersecurity efficiently. Therefore, a shift is needed from the adage that humans are the weakest link. The convergence of technical and operational, strategic and human-centred solutions that overlay user competencies on various skill sets and abilities is much needed to build cognitive resilience for truly enhanced cybersecurity governance. Therefore, we must leverage the human brain’s ability to understand, learn, think, and adapt. These metacognitive abilities support the cybersecurity governance framework’s enabler for people, skills, and competencies.

3.6.1 Humans need to know what they are doing to manage their cybersecurity

As mentioned earlier, people cannot keep up with the changes in the digital environment and often, their main focus is not on cybersecurity per se but instead on their primary goal. Although, understandably, not all technology users are experts in deep technology mechanics, there should be an assumption that all technology users have a basic level of cyber literacy. For example, there would be an expectation that all email users are aware of basic phishing techniques and would be able to identify blatant phishing attempts that include poor spelling and grammar, an obviously fake logo, and the lack of a personalised greeting in an email. With advances in technology, social engineering attempts have also significantly improved, and therefore, there are now more targeted attacks, as illustrated in Examples 1, 2, and 3. In these examples, the user should have their wits about them to identify minute but nuanced differences from what is expected.

Users need to understand what is expected of them to protect themselves and manage their cybersecurity and not fall for elementary cybersecurity attacks. For example, certain demographical groups are very trusting and generally trust whatever is on the internet. If they see a pop-up that says Your PC is infected, click here to run malware cleaner. These individuals will click on it with a trusting nature, but instead of ridding them of the malware, they will unintentionally install it. Another example was illustrated in Example 5, where many customers reused the same username and password combination to protect personal genetic data. Cybersecurity advocates have long warned internet users not to follow these practices and to improve their cyber hygiene. A further example, particularly relevant in the organisational setting, is the social engineering variation that uses the organisation’s structure to pressure staff by impersonating authority figures. Although these attempts are often factually correct in terms of content, users should be able to pick up nuances; for example, something simple, such as the way the impersonated person usually greets you or the salutation that is different from their regular writing style, can give a phishing attempt away. However, the user must be vigilant and concentrate on the email, or they will miss it. The same goes for a slightly modified email address. If a user is on a mobile phone view of the email app, it would be easy to miss this, but an expanded desktop email will show that the email address looks different than expected.

Particularly for individuals working in a large organisation with a dedicated IT or cybersecurity team, knowing how to manage their cybersecurity is often a significant problem since they are used to somebody else taking care of it. In a user study conducted by CSIRO in 2019, almost 45% of respondents indicated that they rarely change the password for the internet devices used at home, with a further 28% of respondents stating that they only change these passwords when prompted by the associated software. The survey respondents overwhelmingly stated that they do not consider themselves applying the same level of care in terms of cybersecurity in their home environment as opposed to their place of work or study (CSIRO, 2019). Generally, individuals are not familiar with basic cybersecurity measures and do not apply them in their personal environments. Within organisational settings, the dedicated IT or cybersecurity would be more knowledgeable of what measures are in place, but the details of these measures often do not trickle down to the end users or up to the executives that they can comfortably state that they know what they are doing to protect themselves in cyberspace. The problems come in when these individuals access their organisational accounts and networks from a private mobile phone or home network that is not secured correctly.

3.6.2 Humans need to understand what can go wrong

As mentioned earlier, it is very difficult for humans to comprehend intangible cybersecurity concepts, and it is equally challenging to marry deep technology and cybersecurity applications in a non-cyber business environment. It is for this reason that people still fall for phishing emails that leverage cognitive biases (Example 1), cannot easily distinguish between real and deepfake AI-generated content (Example 2), inherently trust what they see (Example 3), claim ‘sophisticated attacks’ when a cybersecurity omission causes significant disruption (Example 4), and practice poor cyber hygiene (Example 5). To help humans fully understand what can go wrong in the cyber environment, it is necessary to apply proper risk management.

As with any risk, humans can manage cyber risks and cybersecurity incidents in four ways. Risk avoidance occurs when users ignore the things that they do not understand. This links to the initial statement that humans often take on the attitude that if they cannot see it, it is not real, and they ignore it. The website https://haveibeenpwned.com illustrates this by showing that the word ‘password’ has been used as a password in breached accounts more than 9.65 million times, with ’123456’ used more than 37.6 million times. People do not take password security seriously and enter a basic string to satisfy the password requirements without considering the security aspect. Risk acceptance and sharing is another strategy that have mixed success in the cyber environment. Particularly in larger organisations, executives have a cognitive bias that cybersecurity is ‘an IT problem’ and does not relate to the rest of the business. Without the proper backing of the board of directors, cybersecurity is never taken seriously in these organisations. Risk transfer is a strategy where the responsibility for risk mitigation is transferred to a third party. This can be an external cybersecurity consultant, but in many cases, organisations transfer their risk by assuming that their suppliers and third-party vendors have sufficient cybersecurity measures in place. Unfortunately, numerous examples of third-party breaches enabled malicious actors to gain access to a system or network because the third-party cybersecurity measures were not up to scratch. The last strategy is risk mitigation, which takes on the form of appropriate cybersecurity governance, including well-developed cybersecurity functions supported by enablers. Ideally, all cybersecurity risks should be handled through proper risk mitigation, where organisations and individuals understand what can go wrong and adequately act to remediate the risk factors before adverse events occur. Proper risk management and understanding what can go wrong play a prominent role in a correctly set cybersecurity governance framework.

3.6.3 Humans need to be willing to learn from their (and others) experiences

Cybersecurity incidents can significantly impact organisations, affecting their economic, social, and organisational dimensions in traumatic and far-reaching ways. The Cybercrime in Australia report surveyed 13,887 computer users in early 2023. In the 12 months before the survey, 27% of the respondents had been victims of online abuse and harassment, 22% of malware, 20% of identity crime and misuse, and 8% of fraud and scams. Alarmingly, more than half of the respondents experienced more than one type of cybercrime in the 12 months before the survey (Voce & Morgan, 2023).

There are too many variations of cyber incidents and changes in the digital environment for humans to remain ahead of cyberthreats. Not only is it just the sheer number of attacks constantly increasing, but the variations and inclusion of new technologies and approaches in new domains make the cyberthreat space very lucrative. Therefore, it is necessary for humans to acknowledge and learn from their mistakes and also learn from the mistakes of others. If humans do this, they will be in the best position to understand malicious human behaviour, and behavioural economics will provide an alternative lens on cybersecurity governance. Where cybersecurity solutions often focus solely on either technical or operational mechanisms or human-centred solutions, converging technical, operational, strategic, and human-centred solutions that overlay user competencies is much needed to build cognitive resilience for enhanced cybersecurity governance.

3.6.4 Thinking under pressure: navigating the digital world

Malicious actors hook onto news events; they exploit people’s fear and curiosity to get them to click on links and attachments they would have steered clear of if they had thought rationally about it. So, these malicious actors are intent on exploiting human behaviour. With so many things happening in the world around us, it is no wonder that individuals are struggling to connect the real world with malicious online attacks. This is illustrated time and time again when malicious actors adapt their approach to link to a topical issue. Depending on the topic and how interested people are in this, malicious actors can easily exploit human interest and empathy to generate increased traffic to malicious websites and expect increased click-through rates on phishing and scam emails. For example, malicious email traffic increased by almost 600% during COVID-19, cyber-physical attacks rose in the Russian–Ukraine war, Queen Elizabeth II’s passing was used in phishing as well as clickbait attacks, and malicious actors used victims’ personal information during national disasters to steal money (ABC, 2022a, 2022b; Associated Press, 2020; Paganini, 2022; RiskOptics, 2022).

To manage this, humans must think under pressure and see warning signs of malicious activity in new and novel formats to ensure they do not fall victim to apparent scams. In a cross-national study focusing on developing a five-nation phishing resilience benchmark, particular attention was given to cultural and demographical factors that contributed to participants being able to identify malicious indicators under stressful circumstances. Five dimensions were the most significant for impacting people’s behaviour in accurately identifying phishing attempts. Specifically, people are more focused on identifying attacks if they perceive that the risk of becoming a cybercrime victim would have a short-term impact (immediacy) if they perceive the risk of becoming a phishing victim as commonly encountered and close to home as opposed to something happening to a friend of a relative (common dread), and if they perceive the severity of becoming a phishing victim as significant (severity) (Gopavaram et al., 2021). Understanding these cognitive biases and human behaviour will enable organisations to more effectively manage awareness campaigns to support their employees in recognising, resisting, and reporting social engineering attempts.

3.6.5 Humans need to adapt to the fast-moving digitisation of the world

Despite ongoing cybersecurity awareness campaigns and training, evolving malicious approaches remain effective globally. Social engineering poses a significant challenge, necessitating users to leverage their adaptability in a world demanding cognitive awareness. A shift from usage-centred to user-centred cybersecurity is crucial, moving from functional systems to those interacting with and supporting users. This fosters a better understanding of human behaviour, dispelling the stigma of users as the weakest link in a cyber system (Grobler et al., 2021).

In the dynamic cybersecurity landscape, strategic use of awareness campaigns emerges as a powerful defence against social engineering attacks. This investigation explores campaigns’ pivotal role and effectiveness, utilising psychological insights to empower citizens and executives. The primary objective is to equip individuals with the knowledge to recognise, resist, and report social engineering attempts. Cyber awareness campaigns that integrate psychological insights better uplift cybersecurity ecosystems. Effective management, considering psychological and human factors, enables a successful approach to enhancing resilience against multifaceted cybersecurity incidents. Lessons from the organisational setting should extend to personal environments, empowering users in all digital interactions.

3.7 Conclusion

There seems to be a disconnect between the usage of technology, its usability, and the people using it. For the most part, humans do not understand what technology is supposed to do and not what they are capable of doing. Technology is not always that intuitive to use and does not adapt to the needs and tech-savviness of the human. In addition, human nature is complex, and where humans are involved, you need to deal with emotions and agendas, memory, decision-making, and trust. This often gets very messy very quickly. This combination of the user, usage, and usability triad that is not entirely up to scratch introduces weaknesses in the system. To remedy this, organisations must focus on educating their users about cyber hygiene, upskilling their users in correct system usage and educating their management and executives in viewing business objectives with a cyber lens to ensure that their cybersecurity strategy fully covers their fiduciary cybersecurity obligations.

Understanding that humans, in combination with systems and technology, including misuse and misunderstanding of technology in an ever-changing cybersecurity landscape, create a complicated environment where human cognitive abilities are tested and stretched to their limits in adjusting to technological changes and responding to cybersecurity incidents. Therefore, humans need to be aware of the cybersecurity environment in which they find themselves to apply critical thinking and cognitive understanding. Equally, executives of organisations and system designers and implementers need to be aware of human cognition and vulnerabilities, as well as the relationship between these elements with trust dynamics and citizen engagement, to ensure that they can adequately enable users to protect the integrity of digital systems and data.

Understanding that humans alone cannot be solely responsible for the security of systems is essential. Cybersecurity system designers focus only on system aspects to defend against malicious attacks. However, users of cybersecurity systems have diverse perceptions, knowledge, and experience about security risks that drive their behaviours. The failure of any human actor, including system architects, designers, developers, and end users, can cause significant challenges in identifying phishing attacks. Therefore, it is necessary to have a technology system that can double-check and analyse where human errors can occur. This understanding will not only help improve the overall cybersecurity posture of humans but also reduce the impact of and recovery time after cybersecurity incidents.

3.8 Acknowledgement

This chapter has been supported by the Government of Western Australia acting through the Department of Premier and Cabinet, Office of the Digital Government, Western Australia, and the Cybersecurity Cooperative Research Centre Limited, whose activities are partially funded by the Australian Government’s Cooperative Research Centres Programme.
