Chapter 4 Cybersecurity in australian higher education curricula The SFIA framework

Anna J. Griffin and Nicola F. Johnson

DOI: 10.1201/9781032664859-4

4.1 Introduction

Technology has developed exponentially over the past two decades, and the cybersecurity industry has struggled to keep up with the unprecedented rise in cyberattacks. Higher education institutions (HEIs) globally have increased their cybersecurity courses in response to the increased threats. Cybersecurity as a discipline in HEIs is a relatively new area and has been steadily developing. Part of the development process as a new discipline has been establishing processes and developing curriculum within HEIs that provide students with accreditation. This process is not unique to HEIs within Australia. Many countries, including the United States, the United Kingdom, and much of Europe, have established accreditation processes, all unique to each country depending on their needs and criteria. However, processes that were established in the infancy of a discipline should take time to evaluate the effectiveness of its initial procedures and perhaps consider, as a discipline, the best way a discipline might mature. This chapter explores the deployment of skills frameworks in Australian Universities within the cybersecurity discipline. In Australia, the Skills Framework for the Information Age (SFIA, 2021) is the primary framework that underpins professional accreditation from the Australian Computer Society (ACS). The ACS (2022) is the primary accreditation available to cybersecurity courses at HEIs, and as such, the cybersecurity curriculum must consider the SFIA framework in its development. Considering international literature that identifies alternative frameworks are used, this chapter explores the pros and cons of using SFIA within Australian HEIs when developing cybersecurity curriculum and discusses possible alternatives.

4.2 Curriculum Design

Defining curriculum is difficult as the literature has more than 120 definitions. Two definitions of curriculum poignant to Higher Education (HE) are curriculum as a product and curriculum as a set of performance objectives (Marsh, 1997). Curriculum as a product includes the documentation surrounding objectives, goals, content, and assessment, which is publicly available from any unit or course handbook on HEI websites. The curriculum, as a set of performance objectives, focuses on what students should attain, including specific skills or knowledge in the specialised area being covered in a unit. Both these definitions align with the HE sector. The focus of curriculum within this chapter is examining the curriculum as a product and as a set of performance objectives that can be seen through documentation – it is not considering the ‘hidden’ curriculum. The hidden curriculum is the teaching and learning that occurs within HEIs that is not the written curriculum but occurs due to the approaches of staff and the interactions that occur with students, even though they were not the intended learning outcomes (Marsh, 1997). The hidden curriculum is very different to the written curriculum and is not within the scope of this chapter.

Recently, the cybersecurity curriculum has become an area of development within HEI. Santos et al. (2017) succinctly stated that cybersecurity is the ‘preservation of confidentiality, integrity and availability of information in Cyberspace’ (p. 1). Cybersecurity is defined by a Joint Task Force on Computing Curricula as follows:

[A] computing-based discipline involving technology, people, information, and processes to enable assured operations in the context of adversaries. It involves the creation, operation, analysis, and testing of secure computer systems. It is an interdisciplinary course of study, including aspects of law, policy, human factors, ethics, and risk management.

(Bishop et al., 2017; Blaine et al., 2021; Parrish et al., 2018)

Within this explanation of cybersecurity, the discipline’s complexities are highlighted. Historically, cybersecurity has included aspects of several disciplines, including information security, digital forensics, network security, information warfare, etcetera, spurred on through the disciplines’ origins and can be traced in military, business, law, and computer science. With a complex history, it is no wonder that curriculum development may experience difficulties with so many facets to consider. Until recently, cybersecurity was often added to existing information security or computing science courses, identifying it as a standalone cybersecurity discipline. It is not surprising that within the literature, many of the challenges identified regarding curriculum design for cybersecurity courses are as follows: (1) there is little consensus (Blaine et al., 2021), (2) there is limited agreement on what should be known by cyber professionals (Bishop et al., 2017), (3) there is no global curriculum for cybersecurity (Asghar & Luxton-Reilly, 2020), and (4), there is a mismatch between the key stakeholders, including academia, industry, and government (Hajny et al., 2021), argued as one of the main reasons for the skills shortage.

Every HEI is presented with a challenge when it comes to curriculum design. Consideration must be given to course structure, the knowledge, skills, and abilities (KSAs) students are to be taught and assessed, and how to schedule and meet university protocols about course and unit delivery. Further consideration must include the effective implementation of policies regarding assessment, the award of a qualification, and the provision of learning experiences throughout a student’s course enrolment. Parrish et al. (2018) outlined two cybersecurity course design approaches. The first approach is when cybersecurity units and content are integrated into an existing computing course (computer science or information technology, for example), and it is renamed to reflect the addition of cybersecurity. The other approach is to develop a new stand-alone course reflecting the cybersecurity discipline. They explained that it was common practice for the past 20 years to take the first option and adapt an already established course. However, they noted a movement towards creating new stand-alone courses, given cybersecurity’s increasing maturity as a discipline.

There is limited literature on deciding what content to include within the cybersecurity curriculum. Jones et al. (2018) are among the few who provided empirical evidence as to what should be included in the cybersecurity curriculum. Their study interviewed cybersecurity employees who had one of four roles within the ‘Protect and Defend’ specialty area, as defined by the National Initiative for Cybersecurity Education (NICE) framework. Although the research group interviewed the participants, questions were completed on a survey using the Likert scale, leading to quantitative data analysis that analysed the KSAs most important to their job and those that were the most difficult to learn. From this research, Jones et al. (2018) provided clear guidance to educators of what should be included in the curriculum within the ‘Protect and Defend’ specialty area.

Bishop et al. (2017) argued that the cybersecurity education curriculum should be based on a computer science foundation, including core cybersecurity knowledge and skills based on the Cybersecurity Curricula (CSEC, 2017) guidelines. In a similar thought, Blaine et al. (2021) claimed undergraduate courses were developed in one or two ways: first, as a deep exploration of a single technical discipline (so computer-based, similar to Bishop et al., 2017), or second, as a course examining both technical and human elements within a broader curriculum. They contended that courses should specialise in areas in high demand by industry. Both Bishop et al. (2017) and Blaine et al. (2021) asserted that cybersecurity curriculum should have a solid common foundation. For example, within Data Security, the essentials include basic cryptography, digital forensics, end-to-end secure communications, data integrity and authentication, and information storage security.

4.3 Frameworks

The literature on cybersecurity education has a substantial reference to using frameworks and guidelines when developing curricula in HEIs. In a recent scoping review by the first author (Griffin, 2024), 14 of 20 papers referred to using frameworks when developing curriculum. The terms ‘framework’ and ‘guidelines’ were often used interchangeably, with little differences apparent. Within this chapter, the framework terminology will be used throughout to examine skills frameworks used as a reference within the cybersecurity industry and education.

Brown (2020) explained that a skills framework provides stakeholders with a common terminology that can be used within an industry when referring to skills or competencies required for a specific sector of an industry. They discuss how it can facilitate skills management and include ‘technical’ skills specific to the discipline or the ‘soft’ skills transferable between disciplines, for example, communication, teamwork, and leadership. Brown and Parr (2018) state that, in theory, a skills framework should provide the same base of specific skills from which all users can work. Brown and Parr (2018) explain that most skill frameworks have typical goals that they aim to facilitate:

‘Assessment of current skill proficiency for individuals.
Identification of target skill proficiency for individuals.
Assessment of current skill portfolios in an organisation.
Identification of skill gaps in an organisation.
Design of accurate job and role descriptions.
Design training and educational programmes by training/educational providers’ (p. 2).

The focus of this chapter is the use of frameworks in the design of training and education curriculum and courses; however, it is also essential that HEIs consider how to identify and assess particular skills proficiencies for individual students. Therefore, an essential purpose and role of frameworks within cybersecurity education is to help curriculum designers understand what skills and knowledge the cybersecurity discipline, and in turn the graduates, need to develop when completing a cybersecurity course. Using frameworks helps academics define the topics and themes fundamental to course development (Hajny et al., 2021).

Skills frameworks are also utilised in organisations for both employers and employees. For employers, frameworks are helpful when assessing staff skill proficiency and planning future staff upskilling. Accessing a skills framework through a self-service application allows employees to manage their skill profiles and identify training needs. Staff can use it to demonstrate proficiency through their work experience (Brown, 2020). Some argue that a skills framework should also emphasise soft skills, as this is an area employers claim graduates are not prepared in when entering the workforce (Brown, 2020). It is important to note that although there is no doubt a skills framework has a significant role for organisations, this chapter aims to examine the use of skills frameworks within HEIs and the development of curriculum within the cybersecurity discipline.

Many benefits are identified in the literature regarding using frameworks in curriculum development (van Oorschot, 2021). Within the literature, most HEI academics use some form of framework to develop the curriculum (Griffin, 2024). They use these frameworks for their needs and capabilities because they have confidence in them, as they were developed by academics, governmental departments, and computing societies, and they help to define their curriculum (Hajny et al., 2021; Sobel et al., 2019). HEIs in cybersecurity use a framework for curriculum development because they provide rigour and outline the KSAs needed for a particular work task and because the KSAs can easily transform into learning outcomes. The frameworks are also believed to provide a global standard for countries developing their cybersecurity education (Knapp et al., 2017; Read et al., 2017).

One of the critical papers informing this chapter was co-authored by Hajny et al. (2021). Given that it is heavily relied upon in this chapter, we now provide an overview of its content. Hajny et al.’s (2021) seminal study undertook a comprehensive analysis of curriculum design recommendations alongside an analysis of the curriculum content of 89 existing courses (degrees) and mapped it to cybersecurity work role requirements. It then presented the SPARTA Cybersecurity Skills Framework (CSF) based on the structure of the NICE Framework, delineating SPARTA topics with NICE competencies and aligning them with KSAs. Their article is one of the few to extensively engage with curriculum mapping and address the need for industry and cybersecurity education to align better.

4.4 Skills Framework for the Information Age (SFIA)

The Skills Framework for the Information Age (SFIA) was initially developed by the British Computer Society (BCS) alongside a variety of organisations from the United Kingdom and incrementally modified over the last few decades. Over 30 predominantly European organisations were involved in the development of SFIA, including the Department of Education and Employment and Microsoft (Brown, 2020; Bailey & Morrell, 2011). SFIA is a framework designed for Information Technology (IT) professionals and, according to Brown (2020), may be the most widely accepted IT skills framework globally for IT as a discipline.

The purpose of SFIA was to develop a high-level competency framework providing a standardised view of the skills needed by IT professionals (Bailey & Morrell, 2011). The intention was to develop an IT framework that used the best ideas from other frameworks and create a universal framework for common IT skills. It is meant to assist individuals and organisations in managing their skills (Brown, 2020). However, does using SFIA provide Australia HEIs with the best framework for cybersecurity education?

SFIA is currently up to version 8, which was released in 2021. SFIA is structured through professional skills, which are in six skills categories, each with its own subcategories:

Strategy and architecture
Change and transformation
Development and implementation
Delivery and operation
People and skills
Relationships and engagement

There are seven levels of responsibility, basically a grading system for the difficulty of each professional skill. At each level, the expectation is that a person demonstrates different skills associated with that professional skill and can demonstrate progression. The seven levels of responsibility range from level 1, which is the most basic, to the complex level 7. Not all professional skills will have levels one through seven; it depends on the skill (SFIA, 2021). Within each level of responsibility are five generic attributes: autonomy, influence, complexity, business skills, and knowledge (Brown, 2020).

To provide an example, Figure 4.1 outlines the descriptions of the professional skills of Security Operations. As can be seen, this professional skill can be attained through levels one to six and does not have a seventh level. The language used in the description is described as ‘natural’ and does not come from a standardised dictionary on IT.

Bailey and Morrell (2011) explain that using a professional industry framework in the curriculum helps address skill gaps early on and provides industry-wide recognition. However, it should be noted that they wrote about SFIA in 2011, which was before the establishment of other cybersecurity-specific frameworks.

More recently, Brown (2020) stated that SFIA does not provide universal certification criteria, which limits its portability and utility due to its lack of mapping between typical job roles. Also, Brown (2020) asserts that SFIA does not represent soft skills, for example, teamwork, leadership, and communication, that employers often refer to as paramount in any graduate. Brown and Parr (2018) explain that SFIA is counterintuitive with its lack of soft skills and levels of responsibility. They explain that a manager may have high leadership skills but a lack of technical expertise, which cannot be demonstrated in SFIA. They also question the benefits of including generic attributes, sometimes making the framework inflexible and limiting.

Figure 4.1 Example of SFIA Security Operations (SFIA, 2021).

It should be noted that SFIA was not written with education in mind. It would be difficult to use the information in Figure 4.1 to develop a unit or course of work from this set of descriptions alone and would require some work to convert it into learning outcomes, KSAs, and assessments.

4.5 Two Examples of Accrediting Bodies

The accreditation body in the United States is the National Centres of Academic Excellence (CAE) in Cyber Defence. The National Security Agency and the Department of Homeland Security in the United States played a significant role in establishing educational standards for cybersecurity through the CAE accreditation programme, focusing on Cyber Defence and Cyber Operations. While not a framework, the accreditation programme has strict criteria for certification (Hajny et al., 2021). However, due to its prescriptive nature, only a limited number of higher education institutions have achieved accreditation. Despite the challenges with achieving accreditation, the CAE was discussed in 9 out of the 20 papers in the scoping review (Griffin, 2024). Conklin (2018) explains that CAE accreditation courses improve existing training materials, making them suitable for an educational setting by designing recommended knowledge units and topics to be included in the curriculum. The CAE courses aimed to provide structure and guidelines for cybersecurity education.

In Australia, the Australian Computer Society (ACS) established itself as a professional association providing professional accreditation of IT skills. Therefore, many universities that offer courses related to IT seek to align their courses to the requirements of ACS’s professional accreditation. In a current review of the cybersecurity undergraduate courses offered by Australian HEIs, more than 75% stated in their course handbook that they offered professional accreditation through ACS.

The ACS has established its own Core Body of Knowledge (CBoK) for Information and Communication Technology (ICT) Professionals, which uses SFIA as a basis for their CBoK (CBoK, 2015). Van Oorscot (2021) explained that a Body of Knowledge (BoK) encompasses the ‘entirety of facts, current beliefs or understandings, concepts, and practices of an academic domain or profession … commonly divided into knowledge areas (KAs)’ (p. 83). The CBoK has three areas (ICT professional knowledge, ICT problem-solving, and ICT management). The ACS claims its accreditation process ensures ICT degrees are appropriate for people starting their careers in ICT. It also claims that one of its goals is to support the mobility of ICT graduates internationally and, as such, is an authority for skills assessments for immigration purposes (ACS, 2022). It should be noted that in Australia, there is no requirement for people to have a licence to practice; therefore, the accreditation process is not compulsory. However, for people to migrate to Australia as a skilled migrant, they need to have their skills accredited.

The ACS accreditation process covers all ICT professions – not just cybersecurity – and the documentation on the ACS website is generic. The ACS does specify that they have accreditation for cybersecurity courses; however, this information is not easily attainable from their website. The ACS uses SFIA as their main content for the CBoK, stating professionals need to demonstrate level 3 (undergraduate) or level 5 (postgraduate) from SFIA in their areas of specialisation, depending on the specific role of the cybersecurity professional. To obtain accreditation for cybersecurity degree courses, at least eight subjects (units) from CBoK or similar must be included.

It makes sense that the ACS would use an established framework to develop its CBoK for the accreditation process; however, is SFIA the most appropriate framework for cybersecurity education in Australia? Should HEIs offering cybersecurity courses continue to seek accreditation from ACS and a generic ICT accreditation process when alternative and widely used cybersecurity discipline-specific frameworks are available? Two decades ago, when cybersecurity was merely developing as a concept, using ICT or IT generic processes would have been appropriate. However, it could be argued that cybersecurity as its discipline should be represented accordingly.

4.6 Other Frameworks

In a recent scoping review, the first author (Griffin, 2024) identified that SFIA was only mentioned in 1 of 20 cybersecurity curriculum development papers. Therefore, it is essential to identify and elaborate on other frameworks utilised within cybersecurity education. A detailed summary of the NIST NICE framework and the CSCE 2017 curriculum guideline is provided in the following section. The two outlined below are considered the most transferable globally. Following that, but in less detail, are brief overviews of CyBOK UK (2021) (different from the ACS CBoK) and SPARTA from Europe.

4.6.1 Nist national initiative for cybersecurity education (NICE): the United States

The National Initiative for Cybersecurity Education (NICE) is led by the National Institute for Standards and Technology (NIST; Newhouse et al., 2017) in partnership with the private sector, academia, and governments, and since 2010 has produced an array of documents, including the Workforce Framework for Cybersecurity (NICE Framework) (Hajny et al., 2021; Santos et al., 2017). This popular framework is based on 52 cybersecurity roles divided into 7 job categories and includes 33 specialty areas. The individual KSAs are mapped to each of the work roles and, as such, clearly outline the requirements for professionals and educational standards (Hajny et al., 2021; Sobel et al., 2019; Knapp et al., 2017; Parrish et al., 2018). This emphasis on the workforce makes it distinctive from the other frameworks. The NICE framework strengthens employers’ position to (a) enhance the workforce and (b) develop employee careers through developing the employees themselves (Brown & Parr, 2018). The NICE framework links the job market with work roles through an extensive spreadsheet highlighting the necessary KSAs. The NICE Framework defines professional requirements, sets educational standards, and proposes best practices (Hajny et al., 2021; Parrish et al., 2018). The language used to express the KSAs uses the industry and cyber workforce dictionary. The use of this dictionary has created a common terminology. In a recent scoping review on cybersecurity curriculum development, the NICE framework was the most referenced (Griffin, 2024).

Figure 4.2 Example of NIST NICE Framework (Newhouse et al., 2017).

If we compare the work role of the Cyber Defence Infrastructure Support Specialist in the NICE framework to our example in Figure 4.1, both are similar work roles; the difference in the details of the two frameworks is notable as NICE has 34 KSA and task statements for the role. Each statement can be used to help develop a curriculum and is broken down into manageable tasks that could be easily assessed. All of these are very helpful when developing a curriculum. In Figure 4.2, we have included just a few examples of the KSAs and tasks for this work role in NICE. The other noticeable aspect of NICE is that the tasks are written using cognitive verbs from the Revised Bloom’s Taxonomy (Anderson et al., 2001), which indicates to educators the level of cognitive difficulty each task needs to be set at.

4.6.2 CSEC2017 for cyber and CC2020 for computer science

The Computing Curriculum 2020 project produced a report in 2020 by the Joint Task Force, an updated version of a previous report produced in 2005. This report summarises curricula and content for several computing disciplines, including cybersecurity. This task force included individuals who represented numerous organisations and were led by the Association for Computing Machinery (ACM) and the IEEE Computer Society (CC2020 Task Force, 2020). The individual curriculum on cybersecurity that this report refers to is the 2017 Joint Task Force on Cybersecurity Education (CSEC, 2017).

The purpose of the CSCE 2017 was to provide global curriculum guidelines and structure for undergraduate courses in cybersecurity (Hajny et al., 2021). This framework was written with HEI teaching and learning in mind, and while industry, academics, and government stakeholders were involved in its development, its purpose for education is evident. As such, it groups the knowledge areas and units thematically and outlines what cybersecurity students should know as a level of proficiency (Hajny et al., 2021). CSEC 2017 is also not too prescriptive, allowing flexibility for curriculum design developers by using a multidisciplinary lens whilst concentrating on a specific cybersecurity element (Bishop et al., 2017).

As illustrated in Figure 4.3, there are eight of the CSEC 2017 knowledge areas, and each knowledge area is further broken down into knowledge units designed with educators in mind (Cybersecurity Curricula, 2017: Curriculum Guidelines for Post-Secondary Degree Programs in Cybersecurity, 2018). These are designed using Bloom’s Revised Taxonomy (Anderson et al., 2001) using the understanding and applying levels. Students are expected to achieve the learning outcomes demonstrating proficiency in the essential concepts (Jones et al., 2018).

Figure 4.3 CSEC2017 Knowledge Area System Security Curricular Guidelines.
Figure 4.4 CSEC2017 Essential Concept Testing for the Knowledge Area System Security.

Essential concepts and associated learning areas for each knowledge area are shown in Figure 4.4, with corresponding learning outcomes (Cybersecurity Curricula, 2017: Curriculum Guidelines for Post-Secondary Degree Programs in Cybersecurity, 2018). This framework style is easy to use with academics, as it provides detailed learning outcomes that can be included within their curriculum and learning opportunities developed to cover the essential concepts that can be designed.

4.6.3 The cybersecurity body of knowledge (CyBOK) – UK

The Cybersecurity Body of Knowledge (CyBOK, 2021) stemmed from a project funded by the National Cybersecurity Programme at the University of Bristol in the UK (Rashid et al., 2019). The project aimed to define the knowledge base for cybersecurity as a discipline and establish a foundation for educational courses. The project recognised the fragmented and disjointed state of cybersecurity knowledge and sought to follow the example of mature sciences in progressing from secondary to undergraduate education (Hajny et al., 2021). The CyBOK project identified 19 knowledge areas (Kas) and grouped them into five broad categories. It would be beneficial for educators when developing a curriculum. Unlike other frameworks, particularly those outside the United Staes, it clearly outlines aspects of the curriculum for the non-technical elements, such as human, organisational, and regulatory aspects, as well as adversarial behaviours.

4.6.4 Cybersecurity skills framework (SPARTA) – Europe

The paper by Hajny et al. (2021), the Strategic Programs for Advanced Research and Technology in Europe (SPARTA) Framework, was presented. The SPARTA framework was developed to address the skills gap in the European Union by providing a common language, knowledge, and skills management. The SPARTA framework was created through research summarising the global content covered by undergraduates and postgraduates. Hajny et al. (2021) used their research to suggest the best practice for the content that should be covered in a cybersecurity education course. It is important to note that although the authors developed their framework, its main building blocks were drawn from the NICE Framework. The challenge with this framework is that it was drawn from their research, which examined only the six top university courses within each country, according to the Times Higher Education (THE) ranking; however, this is somewhat arbitrary as there is no guarantee the universities included in the research offered the ‘best’ cybersecurity courses in the country.

From the convenience of our workstation in a bid to conduct a brief desktop review, it was easy to identify what frameworks and accrediting bodies are used in other countries to accredit cybersecurity courses, which can be seen in Table 4.1. The bold font points to general frameworks for corporate organisations and government agencies and is not a framework for HE curriculum development.

Table 4.1 Country, Accrediting Body, and Curriculum Framework (Random Sampling)

Country

	

Accrediting Body/Bodies

	

Framework(s)




The United Kingdom

	

National Cybersecurity Centre (NCSC)

The SFIA website (2021) explains that the framework is not designed to be specific to a discipline; it states that the scope is too broad and too specific. SFIA provides the universal framework for skills and competency levels, and then professional disciplines should develop formal bodies of knowledge. Why is Australia using SFIA when other countries and their accrediting bodies use higher education curriculum frameworks specifically developed for cybersecurity as a discipline?

4.7 Discussion

When examining the purposes of the frameworks discussed, it becomes clear that SFIA was not written with the intention of education, as its primary purpose was for organisations and employers to manage IT skills, and it is not specific to cybersecurity. Although it does cover the theme of cybersecurity in its professional skills, it is not explicitly written with cybersecurity in mind, viewing it as a sub-discipline rather than its discipline.

The other frameworks, particularly NIST NICE and CSEC 2017, were written with cybersecurity higher education in mind after the early development stage. It is clear how academics can use them to develop curriculum for units within a degree. CSEC2017 is particularly for undergraduate curriculum in cybersecurity; this framework does not have organisations in mind, but rather, the students and their learning outcomes. This emphasis on learning outcomes can be seen in the structure of knowledge areas, units, and essential concepts. The language is in smaller chunks, easily converted into learning outcomes, providing clear topics and ideas for lessons and assessments.

The NICE framework is unique in that it is for organisations and education. The framework is developed around the different work roles within cybersecurity, and if HEIs decide to use it when curriculum planning, they could backward map from the work roles most in demand. Its structure sets out knowledge, skills, abilities, and tasks, which can all be used in the planning of teaching and learning within a course or individual units.

SFIA’s descriptions at each level would require many adaptions to translate into appropriate learning outcomes for developing a cybersecurity curriculum. It lacks specific details about KSAs and tasks that these roles would demonstrate, and in turn, what students need to demonstrate to be proficient, that is, in terms of completing assignments or exams successfully. It should also be noted that the SFIA Foundation is now mapping itself to the NICE Framework, which demonstrates the global importance of the NICE Framework as it is recognised as the leading global framework in cybersecurity.

In the context of cybersecurity education in higher education, it can be inferred that frameworks have a crucial function in developing courses. The available research indicates specific frameworks may be more widely utilised by academics globally, with the National Institute of Standards and Technology (NIST) NICE Framework being a prominent example. The NICE framework has the potential to serve as a global reference point for work roles and the knowledge, abilities, and skills necessary for the cybersecurity industry. Its adaptable nature makes it a suitable choice for many higher education institutions, and its potential is further expanded with the recent addition of competencies by the NIST. This expansion makes it even more applicable to educational settings. In addition, the CyBOK UK framework (2021) may be more suitable to draw upon than SFIA as even the ACS CBOK V3.2 states that cybersecurity has a well-developed discipline body of knowledge in CyBOK UK. Thus, frameworks are essential tools for developing cybersecurity education in higher education, and certain frameworks hold more prominence in specific regions.

4.8 Conclusion

Two decades ago, when cybersecurity education was emerging, it was appropriate to use SFIA as the skills framework on which to base the HEI curriculum due to a lack of other adequate frameworks. Nevertheless, that is no longer the case as frameworks have been developed with cybersecurity-specific education at the forefront, demonstrating increasing confidence in the maturity of cybersecurity as a discipline. The ACS provides the only accreditation in Australia now, though it is not mandated for cybersecurity industry work requirements, and HEIs do not require it for their courses. It is clear from the discussion in this chapter that perhaps it is time for HEIs to question whether SFIA is the most appropriate framework for cybersecurity courses to be based on and to question the validity of ACS still using SFIA when there are alternative widely used comprehensive frameworks available.

Chapter 5 Dark echoes The exploitative potential of generative AI in online harassment

Adrian Wood

DOI: 10.1201/9781032664859-5

5.1 Background
5.1.1 Introduction to generative AI

Generative AI, a significant branch of artificial intelligence, represents a transformative leap in digital technology. Unlike traditional AI primarily focused on interpreting and learning from data, generative AI extends this concept to creating new, original content that closely mimics human-generated data (Goodfellow et al., 2020). The technology has become increasingly sophisticated, driven by advancements in neural networks and machine learning algorithms. These advancements enable generative AI to not only understand complex patterns in vast datasets but also to use this understanding to generate new data that bears a striking resemblance to the original input. This capability has opened up new possibilities across various domains, transforming how machines interact with data and creativity.

5.1.2 Applications across various domains

The versatility of generative AI is evident in its wide range of applications. In the realm of art and entertainment, it has revolutionised the creative process. Artists and designers are using AI algorithms to generate new visual art pieces, music compositions, and interactive media experiences, pushing the boundaries of traditional creativity (Zhu et al., 2017). Generative AI has been a boon for medical imaging and drug discovery in healthcare. It assists in creating detailed medical images for diagnosis and simulating drug interactions, thus speeding up research and development processes (Miotto et al., 2018). In finance, generative AI aids in complex tasks such as predicting market trends, assessing credit risk, and detecting fraudulent activities, thus enhancing accuracy and efficiency in financial decision-making (Cao et al., 2015; Mizuta, 2016; Vieira & Sehgal, 2017).

5.1.3 Fundamentals of generative AI algorithms

The core of generative AI lies in its sophisticated algorithms, primarily based on deep learning methodologies such as Generative Adversarial Networks (GANs) and Variational Autoencoders (VAEs). GANs, for instance, involve two neural networks – a generator and a discriminator – that are trained simultaneously. The generator creates data, while the discriminator evaluates its authenticity. This adversarial process results in the generation of highly realistic data (Goodfellow et al., 2020). VAEs, on the other hand, focus on encoding data into a compressed representation and then reconstructing it, which is particularly useful in generating new data that retains crucial characteristics of the original set (Kingma & Welling, 2013).

5.1.4 Autonomous creation of lifelike content

One of the most remarkable aspects of generative AI is its ability to produce content virtually indistinguishable from authentic human-created material autonomously. This capability is limited to visual content and extends to text, audio, and video. For example, recent advancements have seen AI generating realistic human faces, writing coherent and contextually relevant articles, and even creating deepfake videos that are incredibly difficult to distinguish from actual footage (Radford et al., 2015). This ability of generative AI to mimic human creativity has profound implications for industries ranging from entertainment to journalism, redefining the boundaries between human- and machine-generated content. In exploring these advancements, DiPaola et al. (2018) discuss how AI generative techniques can be informed by cognitive theories of human creativity, thus bridging the gap between artificial and human creative processes.

However, the darker side of this technology is its potential for misuse, particularly in online harassment. The ability to create realistic synthetic content can be weaponised to fabricate convincing but false narratives, thus contributing to the spread of misinformation and manipulation (Chesney & Citron, 2018).

5.1.5 Ethical and security implications

While promising, the rapid development of generative AI brings forth significant ethical and security concerns. Its capability to create realistic content can be exploited for malicious purposes, such as spreading misinformation or creating realistic deepfakes that can be used to deceive and manipulate public opinion. These concerns are particularly alarming as deepfakes and AI-generated content become more sophisticated and harder to distinguish from authentic material. Partadiredja et al. (2020) highlight the socio- ethical implications of AI-generated media content, underscoring the risk of deepfakes contributing to misinformation and fake news proliferation. The ethical challenges in data-driven AI systems, as discussed by Henderson et al. (2018), underscore the need for careful consideration of the implications of AI in various domains, including generative AI.

5.1.6 Vulnerabilities exploited by malicious actors

The lifelike nature of content generated by AI systems presents a unique vulnerability that malicious actors exploit. There have been instances where generative AI has been used to create deepfake videos for political manipulation or to forge convincing textual content for phishing scams. The technology can also be used to create realistic images and narratives for online harassment, impersonating individuals and fabricating damaging information. Such misuse poses significant challenges to digital security and individual privacy, necessitating the development of robust detection and mitigation strategies (Chesney & Citron, 2018).

5.2 Significance of the Issue
5.2.1 Escalation of online harassment through generative AI

The advent of generative AI has significantly altered the landscape of online harassment, introducing new dimensions and complexities to this pervasive issue. The technology’s ability to create convincing, synthetic content has been co-opted by malicious actors, who use it to automate and amplify harassment campaigns. This misuse of AI blurs the boundaries between reality and fabrication, making it increasingly challenging to identify and combat such attacks. The enhanced realism and believability of AI-generated content can exacerbate the psychological impact on victims, intensifying the harm caused by online harassment (Chesney & Citron, 2018).

Online harassment, amplified by generative AI, has far-reaching consequences beyond individual victims. It threatens the integrity and inclusivity of digital communities, undermining the sense of safety and belonging essential for constructive online engagement (Citron, 2020). The pervasive nature of such harassment can lead to a chilling effect on free speech and expression, as users may self-censor to avoid becoming targets. This erosion of trust and safety within online spaces highlights the urgency of addressing the issue for individuals’ well-being and online communities’ health.

5.2.2 Legal and ethical quandaries

The rapid advancement of AI technologies, particularly those capable of generating content autonomously, challenges existing legal frameworks, which often lack the provisions necessary to address the intricacies of AI-generated content. This gap in legal preparedness becomes especially evident when considering the difficulties in tracing the origins of such content and in attributing responsibility, thereby complicating the pursuit of legal remedies for victims of AI-facilitated harassment.

From an ethical standpoint, using generative AI in harassment underscores profound concerns about accountability. This includes the responsibility of AI developers and platforms to safeguard against misuse of these technologies. The works of Partadiredja et al. (2020) and Liu et al. (2020) provide insightful perspectives on these challenges. Partadiredja et al. explore the socio-ethical implications of AI-generated media, shedding light on how these concerns manifest in online harassment scenarios. Meanwhile, Liu et al. offer an in-depth analysis of the legal disruptions caused by AI, highlighting the pressing need for legal systems to adapt and respond to the evolving AI landscape.

Further adding to this discourse, Henderson et al. (2018) emphasise the critical role of ethical considerations in AI development and governance. Their insights advocate for a proactive approach to ensuring accountability in AI usage, particularly in applications that might influence public discourse or individual rights.

5.2.3 Strategies for detection, prevention, and redress

The advent of generative AI marks a critical turning point in online harassment, highlighting the pressing significance of this evolving issue. The inherent capacity of generative AI to fabricate convincing synthetic content has become a tool for malicious actors, enabling them to orchestrate more sophisticated and damaging harassment campaigns. This misuse of AI technology represents a significant challenge, as it blurs the distinction between reality and artificiality, complicating identifying and mitigating such attacks.

In their comprehensive analysis, Flynn et al. (2021) detail the exploitation of AI for generating deepfakes, a form of digital impersonation that can be used for defamation or psychological harm. The legal and ethical implications of these AI-generated deepfakes accentuate the need for a robust response from both the legal system and law enforcement agencies.

Birks and Clare (2023) bring to light the impact of AI in academic environments, demonstrating how it can be utilised to facilitate academic misconduct. This extends the issue’s significance into educational integrity, underscoring the importance of adapting prevention and detection frameworks to include AI’s evolving role.

Montasari (2022) sheds light on the national security risks posed by AI-facilitated cyberattacks. Instances such as AI-enabled phishing and adaptive malware highlight the critical need for advanced security measures and ethical guidelines in AI applications to safeguard against these threats.

The examples provided by Flynn et al. (2021), Birks and Clare (2023), and Montasari (2022) not only illustrate the diverse ways in which AI technology can be misused but also emphasise the profound necessity of addressing AI-facilitated online harassment. The advancement of AI technology, with its growing sophistication and accessibility, escalates the potential for its exploitation in harmful ways. This advancement necessitates a proactive and comprehensive approach to safeguard individuals and communities from the detrimental effects of such harassment.

Developing integrated, multifaceted strategies is crucial in responding to these challenges effectively. Technologically, this involves creating advanced AI detection systems capable of distinguishing between authentic and AI-generated content and implementing robust cybersecurity measures to prevent AI-driven cyberattacks. Legal strategies require updating existing laws and regulations to encompass the new forms of abuse enabled by AI, ensuring that legal frameworks are equipped to handle cases of deepfake abuse, AI-facilitated academic misconduct, and other AI-driven cybercrimes.

Additionally, educational responses are vital in raising awareness and understanding of AI technology, its potential for misuse, and the ethical considerations surrounding it. This could involve integrating AI literacy into educational curricula, conducting public awareness campaigns, and training law enforcement and legal professionals on the nuances of AI-facilitated crimes.

Furthermore, there is a need for cross-sector collaboration involving policymakers, tech companies, educators, and legal experts to ensure a cohesive and effective response to the challenges posed by AI-facilitated online harassment. This collaboration is essential for staying ahead of the rapidly evolving technology and for developing strategies that are adaptable and responsive to future advancements in AI.

5.3 Generative AI and Online Harassment: An Unholy Alliance
5.3.1 Exploitative potential

The advent of generative AI, particularly Generative Adversarial Networks (GANs), has ushered in an era of unprecedented lifelike content creation, blurring the lines between reality and synthetic fabrication. While this technological advancement holds immense potential, it also harbours a darker side, manifesting in the exploitative potential of AI in augmenting online harassment and disinformation campaigns. This emerging capability of AI to produce content that is virtually indistinguishable from authentic material raises profound concerns, especially as it becomes increasingly implicated in creating deepfake videos and generating fake news. The consequences of this technological abuse are far-reaching, impacting individuals through reputational damage and emotional distress and posing severe challenges for detection and response by law enforcement and online platforms.

5.3.2 Lifelike content creation

The realism generated by AI, mainly through GANs, raises significant concerns. These algorithms have become adept at creating images, videos, and text, often indistinguishable from authentic content. This capacity has been exploited in creating deepfake videos and generating fake news, where the distinction between natural and synthetic becomes increasingly blurred. The implications of this are profound, as victims of online harassment find themselves embroiled in false scenarios, leading to reputational damage and emotional distress (Paris & Donovan, 2019).

5.3.3 Automation of harassment tactics

The automation capabilities of generative AI have allowed harassers to scale their activities, amplifying the impact and reach of their attacks. Traditional harassment methods, such as personal attacks, spreading false information, or cyberstalking, can now be automated and executed with unprecedented efficiency and scale. This automation not only increases the volume of harassment but also makes it more difficult to trace and counter. For instance, AI-generated text can be used to automate the creation of harmful or abusive messages, while deepfakes can create false images or videos to damage reputations or blackmail individuals. The speed and scale at which this content can be disseminated pose significant challenges for detection and moderation for individuals and online platforms.

The ease with which AI can now replicate human-like content also means that harassment campaigns can be sustained over prolonged periods with minimal effort from the perpetrators. This makes the harassment more persistent and pervasive, exacerbating its psychological impact on victims.

5.3.4 Challenges for detection and response

The sophistication of generative AI complicates the detection of synthetic content. Traditional content moderation tools, which rely on identifying known patterns of abusive language or imagery, may be ineffective against AI-generated content that is constantly evolving and can be tailored to evade detection algorithms. Additionally, the realistic nature of this content can make it challenging for human moderators to discern its authenticity.

Law enforcement agencies and online platforms face considerable challenges in responding to AI-facilitated harassment. The anonymous and decentralised nature of the internet, combined with the difficulty of attributing AI-generated content to specific individuals, complicates efforts to hold harassers accountable. This situation is further complicated by the international scope of the internet, which can involve different jurisdictions with varying laws and regulations regarding online harassment and the misuse of AI.

5.4 Nefarious Uses of Generative AI
5.4.1 Social media

Facebook recognises that deepfakes can be misused on social media in several harmful ways, primarily due to their ability to create convincingly altered videos that depict people saying or doing things they never did ( Meta, 2020).

Firstly, deepfakes present a significant risk in spreading disinformation. Their ability to generate fake news or misleading narratives that are hard to differentiate from actual footage makes it easy to deceive viewers, potentially swaying public opinion. Additionally, deepfakes can severely damage the reputations of individuals, including public figures, politicians, and celebrities, by fabricating scenarios or statements that harm their personal and professional lives.

Another area of concern is the manipulation of political processes, such as elections. Deepfakes can be used to misrepresent politicians or public figures, thereby influencing voter perceptions and sentiments. This technology also contributes to social and political polarisation by creating divisive and controversial content, which can heighten societal tensions and conflicts. Moreover, deepfakes’ deceptive nature can psychologically impact viewers, leading to a general mistrust in media and confusion about what is real. Finally, there is a risk of exploitation for financial gain, where deepfakes could be used in scams and fraudulent activities to deceive individuals.

5.4.2 CEO fraud case

A notable case of generative AI misuse occurred in 2019 involving a UK-based energy company. The CEO was tricked into transferring $243,000 by fraudsters who used AI to simulate the voice of his German parent company’s CEO. The synthetic voice mimicked the German CEO’s accent and mannerisms so convincingly that the UK CEO believed he was speaking to his boss (Stupp, 2019). This incident highlights the emerging risks of AI in corporate fraud and the urgent need for enhanced security measures to protect against such sophisticated attacks.

5.4.3 Virtual kidnapping scam

The advent of AI in cybercrime has led to a new wave of virtual kidnapping scams. In a notable case from April 2023, Jennifer DeStefano received a call with a ransom demand, backed by a distressing voice recording eerily similar to her daughter’s voice, creating a believable kidnapping scenario (Gibson & Hagen, 2023).

Criminals initiate these scams by collecting voice biometrics from online sources like social media. Tools like VoiceLab process these biometrics to create convincing, deepfake voices. This technology enables attackers to convincingly impersonate victims, using the generated audio in extortion schemes (Gibson & Hagen, 2023).

Virtual kidnappers combine AI with social engineering to make their scams more effective. They target individuals, especially youths and public figures, whose online presence makes their biometrics more accessible. The scams exploit misinformation and emotional manipulation, causing not just financial harm but also significant emotional trauma to the victims (Gibson & Hagen, 2023).

AI tools, including ChatGPT, are employed to scale these scams. Attackers can efficiently target victims by processing large datasets of potential victims and integrating them with AI-generated voice and video data. This approach significantly increases the profitability and scalability of virtual kidnapping operations (Gibson & Hagen, 2023).

5.4.4 Revenge pornography

The advent of deepfake technology, a remarkable outcome of advancements in generative artificial intelligence, presents a paradoxical challenge. While it offers innovative possibilities in various fields, its misuse in creating non-consensual deepfake pornography is alarming. This digital abuse involves superimposing individuals’ faces onto pornographic content without their consent, resulting in a disturbing form of image-based sexual abuse that raises serious ethical, psychological, and legal issues.

The emergence of deepfake technology in pornography dates back to around 2017. This technology leverages sophisticated AI algorithms, specifically, Generative Adversarial Networks (GANs), developed in 2014 by Ian Goodfellow and his colleagues (Goodfellow et al., 2020), to generate convincingly realistic videos. These AI models can seamlessly superimpose faces onto different bodies, initially targeting celebrities but quickly expanding to include private individuals. This trend is particularly worrying as it signifies a lowering of barriers to creating deepfakes, making this technology accessible to a broader audience with varying intentions (Hao, 2021; Rawle, 2023).

The psychological toll on victims of non-consensual deepfake pornography is substantial and far-reaching. A notable example Hao (2021) highlighted was Helen Mort, a UK poet who found her face illicitly superimposed onto explicit content. The images were taken from her social media profiles, leading to a profound sense of violation and powerlessness. This case underlines the ease with which personal images can be exploited in the digital era, transforming them into instruments of abuse.

In a similar vein, an Indian journalist became a target of a deepfake pornographic campaign following her report on a sensitive issue. This malicious act not only caused her immense personal distress but also had professional repercussions, demonstrating the potential of deepfake technology to inflict widespread damage to individuals’ reputations and mental health (Rawle, 2023).

These incidents underscore a troubling pattern where deepfake pornography serves as a means for harassment and abuse, often with gender- specific implications. The simplicity of creating and disseminating such content heightens the risk of harm, making it a critical concern in digital ethics and legal discourse.

5.4.5 Manipulation in financial markets

The financial sector has seen AI-driven misinformation used to manipulate markets. For instance, on 22 May 2023, an AI-generated image of black smoke coming from a supposed government building near the Pentagon was spread online, stoking fear in investors and causing a drop in the S&P 500 by 0.29%. The image was quickly identified as fake, and the US Department of Defense confirmed no attack (McCarthy, 2023).

According to Bloomberg, it is possible this was the first instance of an AI-generated image influencing the market. The image first appeared on Facebook and quickly spread to Twitter. While the image was quickly identified as fake, organisations including RT and ZeroHedge reported on the false image (Sorkin et al., 2023).

5.4.6 AI-Generated phishing emails

The emergence of AI-generated phishing emails has significantly impacted the cybersecurity landscape. Using generative AI tools, notably those similar to ChatGPT, has substantially increased sophisticated phishing attacks.

WormGPT, developed by an anonymous creator known as ‘last’ or ‘laste’, exemplifies this trend. Introduced in March 2021 and later sold on hacker forums, WormGPT is designed to assist cybercriminals in creating tools for phishing and other cyber-related activities (Erzberger, 2023). It is available for a monthly fee of $200 or an annual subscription of $1,700, indicating its accessibility to a wide range of malicious users (Erzberger, 2023).

According to a CNBC report, there has been a 1,265% increase in malicious phishing emails since the fourth quarter of 2022, a surge closely aligned with the advent of generative AI tools like ChatGPT (Violino, 2023). This increase includes a 967% rise in credential phishing, suggesting a shift towards more targeted and sophisticated phishing efforts.

The ‘State of Email Security in an AI-Powered World’ report further delves into these concerns, highlighting the transformative impact of AI on email security. The report underscores the necessity for advanced detection methods and a deep understanding of the evolving nature of phishing attacks in the AI era (Darktrace, 2023).

5.4.7 Synthetic identity fraud

Synthetic identity fraud involves using AI to create entirely new identities, combining natural and fake personal information to bypass security checks. This advanced form of identity fraud exploits the capabilities of generative AI, which can analyse vast datasets to synthesise credible but entirely fictional personal identities.

Generative AI learns from a large corpus of real-world identity data, including names, social security numbers, addresses, and other personal details. It then uses this data to fabricate new identities, often indistinguishable from authentic ones. These synthetic identities can include credible-sounding names, plausible addresses, and social security numbers that pass basic validation checks. However, unlike traditional identity theft, synthetic identity fraud does not directly steal a natural person’s identity; instead, it creates an entirely new identity that can be harder to trace and identify as fraudulent.

This fraud poses significant challenges to financial institutions, credit agencies, and security systems. AI-generated synthetic identities can be used to apply for credit, open bank accounts, or engage in other financial activities, often leaving a trail of financial losses and credit issues. Moreover, these activities can remain undetected for extended periods, as no real victim can report the fraud.

One of the primary challenges in combating synthetic identity fraud is the sophistication of the AI algorithms used. These algorithms are constantly evolving, becoming more adept at mimicking real-life patterns of personal information. For instance, Wakefield Research (2021) highlights the increasing prevalence of this type of fraud in the digital age, emphasising the need for more advanced detection methods to keep pace with the evolving techniques of fraudsters.

5.4.8 Fabricated evidence in legal cases

In the dynamic realm of artificial intelligence (AI), the legal sector is confronting significant challenges due to the advent of fabricated digital evidence. Grossman et al. (2023) discuss the difficulty faced by legal systems in distinguishing between authentic and AI-generated evidence, such as documents, videos, and audio recordings, especially with the rapid advancement of GenAI technologies. They note that these developments pose substantial challenges in determining the authenticity of evidence, leading to increased litigation costs and complicating the judicial process (Grossman et al., 2023).

Grossman et al. (2023) emphasise the risks associated with AI tools capable of producing highly convincing deepfakes. These technologies, including notable examples like OpenAI’s ChatGPT and Google’s LaMDA, have advanced language processing capabilities, enabling them to manipulate original recordings and create new, misleading narratives. The concern is that defendants might struggle to refute profound fake evidence unless they can afford expensive expert analysis, posing a threat to justice (Grossman et al., 2023).

Despite AI’s potential benefits in fields like healthcare, Grossman et al. (2023) urge caution against its unchecked deployment. They argue for a balanced approach, advocating for regulations to safeguard against misuse while avoiding outright bans. The EU Parliament’s (2023) draft legislation on facial recognition technology is an example of comprehensive regulation. Nonetheless, Grossman et al. (2023) acknowledge AI regulation challenges, such as the lack of public sector expertise and the risk of hindering innovation.

5.4.9 Examination of tactics employed by harassers

The exploitation of generative AI by harassers demonstrates their ability to blend traditional harassment tactics with advanced technology. The use of deepfake videos in political manipulation, the creation of non-consensual pornography, and the fabrication of legal evidence show a disturbing trend of using AI to harm individuals and institutions. These tactics often rely on the shock value and believability of AI-generated content, exploiting trust and gaps in legal and technological defences. Understanding and anticipating these tactics is crucial in developing comprehensive strategies to counter this new form of digital harassment.

Examining these case studies reveals the multifaceted nature of online harassment augmented by generative AI. It underscores the critical need for interdisciplinary efforts involving legal, technological, and educational measures to address the evolving landscape of digital harassment and ensure the responsible use of AI technologies.

5.5 Legal and Ethical Quandaries

The advent of artificial intelligence (AI) heralds transformative changes across societal, ethical, and legal dimensions, challenging existing paradigms and prompting a re-evaluation of traditional frameworks. The rapid evolution of generative AI technologies, exemplified by large generative AI models (LGAIMs), brings to the forefront the complexities of AI-driven innovation and its implications. These technologies, capable of generating synthetic content indistinguishable from actual human output, pose unique challenges, especially in online harassment, copyright issues, and the broader societal impacts of AI (Hacker et al., 2023).

Generative AI’s ability to blur the lines between reality and fiction introduces significant legal hurdles, particularly in addressing AI-driven harassment. Traditional legal frameworks designed for more straightforward forms of abuse find it challenging to tackle the nuances and complexities introduced by generative AI. This discrepancy stems from the technology’s capability to produce lifelike content, complicating the legal identification and prosecution of harassment. Furthermore, the scalability and anonymity afforded by AI technologies enable perpetrators to conduct extensive harassment campaigns efficiently and often undetectably, raising critical questions about the adequacy of existing laws and the need for adaptive, forward-looking legal approaches (Hacker et al., 2023).

As we delve into the ensuing sections, we will examine the ethical, societal, and legal implications of AI-generated content, the necessity for robust legal and regulatory frameworks tailored to the realities of generative AI, and the overarching goal of fostering a ‘Good Society’ in the age of AI. Through this analysis, we aim to underscore the imperative for collaborative efforts among lawmakers, technologists, and society to navigate the complexities of AI governance and ensure that AI serves the public good while mitigating potential risks and harms.

5.5.1 Legal challenges in addressing generative AI-driven harassment

The rapid advancement of generative AI significantly challenges existing legal frameworks to address online harassment. Traditional laws, designed for simpler abuse forms, struggle to tackle the complexities of AI-driven harassment. This discrepancy stems from generative AI’s ability to create lifelike content that blurs reality and fiction, making it difficult to define and prosecute harassment legally. Additionally, the anonymity and scalability provided by AI technologies allow perpetrators to conduct extensive harassment campaigns efficiently and often undetectably.

The sophistication of AI-generated content complicates the identification of authentic versus synthetic media, challenging victims, legal practitioners, and policymakers alike. Existing legal statutes, reliant on clear definitions of abuse, fall short of addressing the nuanced issues presented by AI. The international nature of the internet further complicates the jurisdictional enforcement of laws against AI-enabled harassment.

Addressing these challenges requires re-evaluating and potentially overhauling current legal approaches to online harassment and adapting them to the rapid development of AI technologies. Legal systems need flexible, technology-agnostic definitions of harassment and international cooperation to tackle AI-facilitated abuse effectively. Collaborative efforts among lawmakers, legal scholars, and technologists are crucial to developing protective measures against AI-enabled abuse while promoting responsible AI use.

5.6 AI in Creative Industries and Copyright Challenges

Integrating artificial intelligence (AI) into creative industries has sparked a significant re-evaluation of copyright laws, particularly as generative AI technologies advance. These technologies can produce creative content, including music, literature, and visual arts, challenging traditional notions of authorship and ownership. The case of Stable Diffusion, where AI-generated images led to copyright infringement lawsuits, exemplifies the growing tensions between AI innovation and copyright protections (Shroff, 2024).

Generative AI systems, such as GPT models used in text generation and Stable Diffusion for image creation, rely on massive datasets, often containing copyrighted materials, to train their algorithms. This raises critical questions about the rights of original creators whose works contribute, without explicit permission, to training these AI systems (Shroff, 2024). The legal quandary extends to determining ownership of AI-generated content, applying ‘fair use’ principles, and compensation for creators of source materials.

The rapid development of AI technologies introduces unique forms of creative expression not fully anticipated by existing legal frameworks. Hacker et al. (2023) note that the legal landscape surrounding generative AI and copyright law is ambiguous, necessitating a nuanced understanding of the AI value chain and targeted regulatory approaches. The complexity of AI-generated content complicates the identification of original versus synthetic media, challenging legal practitioners and policymakers to adapt traditional copyright statutes to the digital age’s realities.

Moreover, the international nature of the internet further complicates the jurisdictional enforcement of copyright laws against AI-enabled content creation. Addressing these challenges requires a collaborative effort among lawmakers, legal scholars, and technologists to develop protective measures that encourage responsible AI deployment and use while ensuring creators are duly recognised and compensated.

5.6.1 Insufficiency of legislation in the face of AI advancements

The rapid advancement of generative AI technologies has created significant challenges for existing legal frameworks. Current legislation, primarily developed before the widespread adoption of sophisticated AI systems, struggles to address the complex issues arising from these technologies. One of the critical areas affected is intellectual property law. As generative AI continues to evolve, legal and ethical questions regarding the impact on artists’ rights have become more prevalent (Poland, 2023). These issues highlight the need for updated laws that adequately address the nuances of AI-generated content and its implications.

Moreover, the legal lexicon is expanding to include new terms such as AI hallucination, AI bias, and generative AI, which reflect the emerging complexities in the field (Sall, 2024). This evolution in terminology indicates a shift in the legal landscape, necessitating a re-evaluation of existing laws to encompass the broad implications of AI technologies. The development of generative AI has outpaced the current legal structures, leading to a gap in regulation and oversight. This gap presents challenges in adequately protecting individuals and entities from potential abuses or infringements facilitated by AI technologies.

Additionally, the impact of evolving AI models on the legal sector is profound. Generative AI tools, ranging from language models like GPT technologies to art generation applications, reshape these technologies’ legal considerations and ethical boundaries (Legaltech News, 2024). The legal community is now tasked with understanding and staying abreast of these advancements to ensure that laws remain relevant and effective in safeguarding rights and maintaining ethical standards in the digital age.

The insufficiency of current legislation in the face of rapid AI advancements is evident. It underscores the urgency for lawmakers and legal professionals to adapt and develop new regulatory frameworks to address the unique challenges of generative AI technologies.

5.6.2 Legal and regulatory frameworks for AI

The legal and regulatory landscapes for artificial intelligence (AI) are evolving in response to the rapid advancement of AI technologies and their profound societal implications. These frameworks are crucial for addressing ethical, privacy, security, and accountability issues, ensuring AI’s development and deployment align with societal values and human rights.

Global Perspectives on AI Regulation: The European Union has been at the forefront of developing comprehensive AI regulation, notably through the proposed AI Act. This legislative framework categorises AI systems based on their risk levels, from unacceptable to minimal risk, imposing specific obligations on AI developers and deployers accordingly. Before deployment, high-risk AI applications, such as those involving biometric identification or critical infrastructure management, are subject to stringent requirements, including transparency, data governance, and human oversight (European Commission, 2021). Similarly, the General Data Protection Regulation (GDPR) sets a high data protection and privacy standard, impacting AI systems processing personal data by mandating user consent, data minimisation, and the right to explain automated decisions.

In contrast, the United States adopts a more sector-specific approach, focusing on guidelines and policies rather than a comprehensive national framework. Initiatives like the National AI Research and Development Strategic Plan and executive orders on AI outline principles for fostering innovation while ensuring ethical, secure, and trustworthy AI development. This approach reflects the dynamic nature of AI technology and its diverse applications across different sectors (National Science and Technology Council, 2016).

Challenges in AI Regulation: One of the primary challenges in regulating AI is the technology’s rapid evolution, which can outpace legislative processes. Legal uncertainty arises when existing laws struggle to accommodate new AI capabilities, creating a need for legal frameworks that are both flexible and technology agnostic. This uncertainty is compounded by AI’s dual status as agent and object, challenging traditional legal categories and necessitating clarification or refinement of laws concerning responsibility and liability (Liu et al., 2020).

Moreover, international collaboration is essential for harmonising AI regulations, given the global nature of AI development and deployment. Efforts by international organisations, such as the OECD’s AI principles, aim to provide a foundation for ethical AI governance, emphasising respect for human rights, democratic values, and diversity. These principles guide national policies and foster international cooperation in addressing AI’s global challenges.

Future Directions: As AI continues to transform societies, the development of legal and regulatory frameworks must be anticipatory, adaptable, and inclusive. Engaging a broad range of stakeholders, including policymakers, technologists, civil society, and the public, is crucial for crafting regulations that balance innovation with ethical considerations and social welfare. The ongoing dialogue on AI regulation reflects a collective effort to steer AI development towards beneficial outcomes for humanity, emphasising the need for ethical AI development, transparency, and accountability.
5.6.3 Balancing free expression and user protection

The evolution of generative AI technologies brings the challenge of balancing the right to free expression with the need to protect individuals from AI-driven harassment. Current legal frameworks, developed in a pre-AI era, grapple with the complexities introduced by AI’s creative capabilities. De Vries (2022) delves into this predicament by examining the status of AI-generated speech under Article 10 of the European Convention on Human Rights (ECHR). Unlike traditional expressive tools, AI’s role in content creation blurs the lines of speech attribution, raising the question of whether machine-generated speech falls under the purview of freedom of expression rights.

This challenge is further complicated by the emergence of new types of AI-generated content, such as synthetic media and deepfakes, which create normative grey areas in legal discourse. De Vries discusses how such content can skirt established legal constraints, including copyright, data protection, and privacy. For instance, the use of AI in generating virtual child pornography or in propagating fake news poses significant ethical dilemmas. These instances highlight the difficulty distinguishing between protected and unprotected speech, a crucial distinction in a legal context.

Legal reforms that can keep pace with AI’s advancements are critical in this rapidly evolving landscape. Policymakers, legal experts, and technologists must collaborate to develop frameworks that allow the free expression of AI-generated speech and ensure it does not infringe on individual rights.

5.7 Ethical Concerns

The emergence of AI-generated content, notably through generative AI technologies like GPT models and deep learning systems, has significantly blurred the lines between human- and machine-generated content, raising profound ethical and societal implications. The ability of these technologies to produce highly realistic text, images, and videos not only challenges our understanding of creativity and authorship but also poses risks related to misinformation, privacy, and the erosion of trust in digital media (Partadiredja et al., 2020).

One of the primary ethical concerns revolves around the potential for AI-generated deepfakes and synthetic media to propagate misinformation, affecting public opinion and democratic processes. The realistic nature of these creations can lead to confusion and difficulty distinguishing between authentic and manipulated contents, undermining the integrity of information and contributing to a post-truth society. This phenomenon necessitates a critical examination of the responsibilities of AI developers and platforms in mitigating the spread of harmful content while also considering the implications for freedom of expression and innovation (Qian et al., 2024).

Furthermore, using AI to create content that mimics human attributes raises significant privacy concerns. Generative AI’s capability to replicate individuals’ likenesses without consent introduces new forms of harassment and exploitation, highlighting the need for legal and ethical frameworks that protect individuals’ rights in the digital age. Developing AI governance mechanisms that ensure transparency, accountability, and ethical use of AI technologies is crucial in addressing these challenges.

Additionally, the societal impact of AI-generated content extends to the labour market and the future of work. As AI technologies become more capable of performing tasks traditionally reserved for humans, including creative endeavours, there is a growing concern about job displacement and the creation of a ‘useless class’, as predicted by some scholars (Qian et al., 2024). This shift necessitates rethinking economic models, social systems, and education to prepare for a future where human and machine labour coexist.

A multi-stakeholder approach involving policymakers, technologists, and the public is essential to navigate AI-generated content’s ethical and societal implications. Developing ethical guidelines, regulatory measures, and educational initiatives that promote responsible AI use while fostering innovation and protecting societal values is imperative. Collaborative efforts to enhance AI literacy and critical thinking among the general public can empower individuals to critically engage with AI-generated content, fostering a more informed and resilient digital society.

Invasion of Personal Space through AI-Generated Content: Generative AI enables content creation that can mimic an individual’s appearance, voice, or personal attributes with alarming accuracy. Such technology can generate false representations or deepfakes, leading to serious privacy intrusions. As Guleria et al. (2023) discuss, the forensic, legal, and ethical issues surrounding AI applications like ChatGPT underscore the potential for these technologies to invade privacy and cause reputational harm.
Implications for Reputation and Psychological Well-Being: The misuse of generative AI in fabricating content can have dire consequences for individuals’ reputations. False narratives or misleading representations created using AI can be spread rapidly online, damaging personal and professional reputations. The psychological impact of such attacks can be profound, leading to distress and trauma for victims. Elgesem (2023) highlights the ethical challenges of generative AI models under the AI Act, particularly concerning protecting personal privacy and preventing misuse.
Challenges in Legal Recourse and Redress: Victims of AI-driven privacy violations often face challenges in seeking legal recourse due to the novelty and complexity of the technology involved. Current privacy laws may not fully address the unique aspects of AI-generated content, leaving victims without adequate legal protection. Ferrara (2023) emphasises the need for legal systems to evolve and adapt to the advancements in AI to provide effective remedies for those affected by such privacy violations.
Ethical Responsibility of AI Developers: Developers and researchers involved in generative AI bear a significant ethical responsibility to consider the privacy implications of their creations. This includes implementing measures to prevent the misuse of AI technologies for violating personal privacy. As Meskys et al.
