Chapter 6 Trust and risk Psybersecurity in the AI era

Shasha Yu, Fiona Carroll, and Barry L. Bentley

DOI: 10.1201/9781032664859-6

6.1 Introduction

Artificial intelligence has penetrated every aspect of our lives in the rapidly evolving landscape of the AI era. From education, healthcare, recruitment, management, shopping, and smart assistants to self-driving cars, artificial intelligence is changing how we work, live, think, and behave.

When we use artificial intelligence to perform tasks for us, it is based on the belief that it is trustworthy and that the risks, if any, should be manageable. In other words, the benefits outweigh the risks. In the past, some scholars categorised security threats brought by AI into digital security, physical security, and political security (Bundage et al., 2018). Other researchers have expanded the scope of security threats to include infrastructure, endpoint, network security, security operations and incident response, threat intelligence, mobile security, and human security (Jain, 2021). These studies typically focus on protecting physical assets, digital data, or systems. It involves strategies and measures that can be objectively assessed, such as technological tools, surveillance, and physical barriers.

Some recent research has focused on individuals’ perceptions, experiences, and well-being in AI environments (Chattopadhyay & Beyene, 2023; Franklin & Chattopadhyay, 2021). This has given rise to an emerging topic and research area called ‘Psybersecurity’ (Chattopadhyay & Beyene, 2023). Psybersecurity goes beyond the traditional security paradigms, aiming to safeguard mental health by addressing the psychiatric implications of technology use. It involves an examination of the mental health attack surface and vectors within the broader context of human security (Franklin & Chattopadhyay, 2021).

6.1.1 The emerging importance of securing mental health in the digital age

The relationship between mental health and the use of technology is a complex and multifaceted topic. Mobile device ownership and social media usage have reached unprecedented levels, and concerns have been raised that this constant connectivity harms people’s mental health (Odgers & Jensen, 2020). Moreover, people who have experienced cybersecurity attacks frequently endure negative mental health impacts, such as anxiety and depression, to name just two. In fact, cyberattacks can have devastating consequences for vulnerable people, those close to them, and many other stakeholders (Ives, 2016). However, it seems to be a bit of a chicken-and-egg scenario.

On the one hand, technology can contribute to the development of mental health issues, whilst on the other, people are also turning to technology as a means to address and alleviate these very challenges. For example, accompanying the rising rates of reported mental distress during the COVID-19 pandemic, there was also a reported increase in the use of digital technologies to manage health generally and mental health more specifically (Abd-Alrazaq et al., 2021). Considering this, and particularly as AI technologies continue to advance, taking proactive steps to prioritise mental health in the digital age is essential for the well-being of society.

6.2 Trust and Risk in AI

Understanding the psychological foundations of trust is crucial for the sustainable development of AI technologies. Building and maintaining trust requires a combination of elements and may vary depending on the specific circumstances of the technology and people involved. As researchers (Gulati et al., 2019) point out, the more the tasks get delegated to intelligent systems, the more the nature of user interactions with them becomes increasingly complex, and the more critical it is to understand the amount of trust that a user is willing to place on such systems. Indeed, user trust in AI-enabled systems has been increasingly recognised and proven as a critical element in fostering adoption (Bach et al., 2022). Despite its great theoretical importance, a limited amount of research has still examined how and why trust develops, is maintained, and occasionally unravels in relationships (Simpson, 2007). From the AI perspective, there is also a risk of bias and lack of fairness, misinterpretation of data, lack of transparency and over-reliance on AI, and security vulnerabilities, to name but a few. Balancing trust and managing risks in AI requires a combination of technical and ethical as well as a commitment to continuous improvement and accountability. Nowadays, organisations must actively work towards building and maintaining trust while mitigating potential risks.

6.2.1 Psychological foundations of trust

Trust is a complex, multidimensional construct, making it difficult to operationalise, measure, and interpret (Simpson, 2007). Indeed, there is more than one way to define and describe trust. As Simpson (2007) shows, ‘trust can be construed in different ways, and it might have varied importance at different stages of relationship development’. Moreover, user trust in AI-enabled systems is influenced by three main themes: socio-ethical considerations, technical and design features, and user characteristics (Bach et al., 2022). The theoretical concept of trust is fundamental to the successful design of intelligent technologies (Raats et al., 2020).

Researchers, such as Kaewkitipong et al. (2022), have even found that trust plays a more significant role than human–computer interaction (HCI). The psychological foundations of trust can refer to cognitive and emotional factors that lead to a person’s willingness to have confidence in a person and/or entity (e.g., technology). This trust is often built when a person and/or technology consistently behaves reliably. As Zenger and Folkman (2019) note, consistency is an essential element of trust for leaders (e.g., they do what they say they will do, etc.). It is true to say that when a person’s or a machine’s actions and behaviours align with people’s expectations, trust tends to grow. Moreover, trust can be influenced by the person or technology’s perceived competence, knowledge, and expertise. If a technology consistently affords competence, it is more likely to be trusted.

Regarding leadership, a decisive factor in whether people trust a person as a leader is the extent to which a leader is well-informed and knowledgeable (Zenger & Folkman, 2019). Trust is also closely tied to perceptions of honesty and integrity. For instance, Gefen et al. (2003) found that online trust is built through (1) a belief that the vendor has nothing to gain by cheating, (2) a belief that there are safety mechanisms built into the website, and (3) by having a typical interface, (4) one that is, moreover, easy to use. Trust is more likely to develop when individuals believe that applications are truthful and transparent and act with integrity.

Moreover, trust can be enhanced when people feel understood and supported. Demonstrating empathy and showing that the application is designed to care about the well-being of people can foster trust. Regarding humans (leaders), trust is partly based on how much a leader can create positive relationships with other people and groups (Zenger & Folkman, 2019). Emotions also play a crucial role in how people trust technology and other people. As Ajenaghughrure et al. (2018) say, trust and emotion are critical indicators for persuading users to change their behaviour towards a technical artifact. Establishing an emotional connection, such as through shared values or experiences, can deepen trust. Previous experiences with a person or technology can significantly shape trust. For example, in online banking, the service value affects customer loyalty to the bank through trust and the bank’s reputation (Burhanudin, 2022). Positive experiences can build trust, while negative experiences can destroy it.

Finally, open and transparent communication is also fundamental for trust. As Wiencierz and Lunich (2020) show, the more the information users obtain regarding open data, the more trustworthy they perceive the app provider to be and the more likely they are to use the app. Trust is more likely to flourish when information is freely shared, and there are no hidden agendas. Trust is also nurtured when people consistently meet commitments and fulfil their obligations. In their study, Alhaji et al. (2021) detected four factors while trust accumulates (perceived reliability, perceived dependability, perceived predictability, and faith) which do not appear while it dissipates. Furthermore, dependability can be described as a foundation for trust.

6.2.2 Context and significance of trust and risk in the AI era

Trust plays a vital role in the psychological factors affecting security in AI. Trust forms the cornerstone of successful AI adoption, impacting users’ willingness to engage with AI technologies and shaping their expectations of reliability, transparency, and ethical behaviour. In April 2019, the Ethics Guidelines for Trustworthy AI were adopted by the European Commission’s High-Level Expert Group on AI, emphasising that trust in the technology is essential for people to fully and confidently harness the advantages of AI (European Commission & Content Directorate-General for Communications Networks, and Technology, 2019).

However, trust is a multifaceted concept, encapsulating the belief that another party will act with benevolence, integrity, competence, and predictability (McKnight & Chervany, 2001). When people trust another party, it implies they are willing to take risks, such as being vulnerable, in a relationship (Schoorman et al., 2007).

Nevertheless, the complexities and potential limitations of AI systems introduce various risks. These risks include bias and fairness, data privacy breaches, lack of interpretability, unintended consequences, and the potential for malicious use of AI. If, for any reason, the AI product or device fails to act with benevolence, integrity, competence, and predictability as expected, it may pose risks to individuals and adversely affect mental health.

6.2.3 Benefits of trust in AI

Trust in AI significantly improves efficiency at the individual level by expediting decision-making, reducing cognitive load, automating routine tasks, enhancing time management, optimising resource allocation, facilitating learning, enabling proactive issue resolution, and boosting overall confidence and job satisfaction. Decisions made automatically by AI were often evaluated as being on par with, or even superior to, those made by human experts in certain circumstances (Araujo et al., 2020). Trust in AI systems leads to greater confidence in their decisions and recommendations. Individuals can make decisions more quickly and with less hesitation, resulting in streamlined workflows and faster progress. A study demonstrates that ChatGPT, a widely used large language model–based chatbot, significantly improves average productivity: the time taken decreases by 0.8 standard deviations (SDs) and the output quality increases by 0.4 SDs (Noy & Zhang, 2023). Previous studies show that employees’ trust in AI helps motivate collaboration with AI, thus benefiting career well-being and productivity (Kong et al., 2023).

6.3 Risks Stemming from Trust in AI

While vital for its successful integration and widespread adoption, trust in AI carries inherent risks that can have significant societal and economic implications. Trust will impact users’ willingness to adopt AI products. For instance, individuals are more inclined to embrace driverless vehicles after receiving positive information about them but less willing to do so after hearing negative information about them (Anania et al., 2018). This can lead to a mismatch between the user’s trust in the AI and the AI’s trustworthiness. These risks manifest as over-trust and under-trust in AI products, leading to distinct challenges. Disuse, misuse, and abuse are dangerous when trust and trustworthiness are misaligned. Specifically, trust exceeding trustworthiness leads to misuse, while trustworthiness exceeding trust leads to disuse (Parasuraman & Riely, 1997). Figure 6.1 illustrates the interplay between a user’s trust in AI and AI’s trustworthiness, influencing the user’s engagement with AI.

Optimal utilisation and benefits from AI products and services occur when users’ trust aligns with the AI’s trustworthiness, resulting in a healthy use of AI (Figure 6.1). However, if an AI system exhibits low trustworthiness due to inherent unreliability or malicious third-party use, excessive trust from users can lead to misuse or potential abuse (Figure 6.1).

Conversely, when users lack trust or have lower trust than the AI system’s actual trustworthiness, this under trust may result in the underutilisation or disuse of AI (Figure 6.1). The misalignment between user trust and AI trustworthiness introduces risks in over-trust and under-trust scenarios.

In the context of AI environments, we can define misuse as the excessive dependence on automation, such as using AI when it is not appropriate or failing to supervise its functions effectively. Disuse would be the inadequate utilisation of automation, such as disregarding or deactivating AI-driven alerts or safety mechanisms. Abuse refers to the inappropriate design or management of AI systems, where AI is applied without due consideration for its impact on human performance within the resulting system (Parasuraman & Riely, 1997).

Figure 6.1 Risks Stemming from Trust in AI.
6.3.1 Over-trust in AI products: abuse and misuse

While trust in AI offers numerous benefits, it can also introduce risks if there is an over-trust in AI applications or devices. One significant risk is the potential misuse of AI. Over-trusts in AI can lead to emotional dependence and privacy risk. Positive experiences with AI applications or devices can elicit feelings of affection, including perceived intimacy, passion, and commitment (Hernandez-Ortega & Ferreira, 2021). Experiences characterised by perceived intimacy, enjoyability, and commitment can give rise to para-friendships (Chung-Wa et al., 2020). The introduction of anthropomorphism, in particular, poses significant risks, as individuals may place undue trust in a robot (Lewis et al., 2018).

Interestingly, a study showed that when participants were asked to choose the correct answer for complex mathematical problems provided by different robots, they tended to trust a robot with specific facial aesthetics, even though it provided the wrong answer (Pinney et al., 2022). These emotions might cause individuals to overlook the privacy risks of using and communicating with AI. For example, some people may expose their private information to ChatGPT without being aware that their chat might be stored for up to 30 days, used for model training, and, in some cases, exposed to human reviewers (Hunter, 2023).

Over-trust in AI can reduce vigilance and neglect of potential risks and threats. AIs inherently have limitations and deficiencies, and blind trust in them may cause people to overlook these risks. Previous studies have shown that individuals with solid trust in others tend to dismiss evidence of potential untrustworthiness (Holmes, 1991). Placing blind trust in AI systems might lead to overlooking security vulnerabilities, making them susceptible to cyberattacks and breaches. For example, Samsung workers utilised ChatGPT for code generation and debugging, inadvertently integrating confidential company information, potentially compromising privacy, and illustrating the risks associated with undue reliance on AI technologies (Maddison, 2023). Researchers found that people often over-trust robots, with individuals and groups granting a robot access to a locked dormitory door about 70–80% of the time, even when they suspect it might pose a bomb threat (Booth et al., 2017). Researchers studied human reactions to a robot’s guidance in an experiment involving simulated fire emergencies. Surprisingly, 95% of participants either followed the robot’s directions or remained with it, even when the robot made mistakes, as they believed the robot was more knowledgeable or incapable of leading them astray (Robinette et al., 2017). In particular, the more accurate the AI system, the more likely it is to be trusted, to the extent that even professionals such as police officers may abandon professional intuition in favour of machine results (Kearns & Muir, 2019).

In addition, over-trust in AI can lead to dependency and a lack of critical thinking. Many people trust AI to make more informed decisions than they can. A study of 1,506 participants reveals that co-writing with opinionated language models affects users’ views (Jakesch et al., 2023). In this context, if the AI-generated information is inaccurate or false, it can lead to harmful outcomes for users. Researchers have investigated that ChatGPT can generate fake peer-reviewed citations and references that do not exist in the journal to which they are attributed or anywhere else (Day, 2023). AI-generated information looks so trustworthy that even highly trained professionals and experts can be misled by it. For example, two lawyers were sanctioned for using fictitious case citations generated by ChatGPT in a legal brief (Merken, 2023).

Furthermore, over-trust can prevent a critical examination of AI algorithms, inadvertently perpetuating biases and discriminatory practices. A researcher tested a ChatGPT detector from Turnitin, a software already utilised by 2.1 million teachers to identify plagiarism. It falsely flagged an innocent student, potentially causing trouble (Fowler, 2023). Furthermore, the AI text detectors displayed bias against non-native English speakers, inadvertently flagging work produced by those who use a limited range of words and phrases (Williams, 2023).

Another notable risk is the potential abuse of AI. When individuals interact with others through customisable AI assistants, they may configure their agents to engage in less ethical behaviour and increase deception compared to their actions (Mell et al., 2020). Research has shown that people are more willing to deceive through AI, receiving less blame for their AI’s negative behaviour and predicting less blame when using AI for their actions (Gratch & Fast, 2022). On the other hand, AI technology allows people without expertise to easily create professional images, audios, videos, and texts that only professionals could create in the past. This could lead to the abuse of AI, causing harm to others, such as the creation and dissemination of fake content, furthering misinformation and causing damage to individuals and society. As an illustration, DeepTrace, an artificial intelligence company, identified 15,000 Deepfake videos online in September 2019, marking a nearly twofold increase within nine months (Sample, 2020). Significantly, image-based abuse, involving the creation or modification of an individual’s image without their consent, disproportionately impacts women. According to a Deepfake research report, it was revealed that approximately 96% of all online Deepfake videos were categorised as Deepfake pornographic videos (Patrini, 2019). These Deepfake images or videos use photos from the internet or social media without the victim’s consent or even knowledge, not only hurting the victim emotionally and psychologically but also damaging their reputation. The victims impacted by these Deepfakes may feel horrified, mortified, devastated, ashamed, humiliated, embarrassed, frightened, and paranoid (Melville, 2019).

6.3.2 Under-trust in AI products: disuse

Although AI’s prevalence and various applications have increased substantially, many individuals still struggle to trust this technology (Gillath et al., 2021). A study showed that only half of the respondents said they trust companies that use AI as much as they trust other companies (Myers, 2022). Insufficient trust in AI products can result in disuse, hampering potential benefits and opportunities (Lee, 2008). Another study revealed that only one-third of respondents rely on AI for various entertainment-related recommendations or would let it produce goods made by automated machinery. Even more concerning, only 9.3% of consumers would allow AI to manage their finances, and merely 4% would trust it with HR-related work. Additionally, the percentage of those who trust medical diagnostics made by learning and decision-making algorithms is even smaller (Dujmovic, 2017).

People may adapt their use of AI based on changing levels of trust, potentially adding or removing tasks from AI workflows or adjusting their reliance on the AI (Dorton et al., 2022). With the prevalence of large language models (LLM), AI now causes more concern for its potential threat to humans. A group of influential computer scientists and tech industry figures has raised alarm about the potential dangers of highly advanced AI systems and called for a six-month pause in training AI models more powerful than GPT-4, citing concerns for society and humanity (O’Brien, 2023). Following this call, additional concerns about the threats posed by AI have been voiced, with some arguing that a longer pause is needed (Noonan, 2023) and others going further to suggest shutting down large AI training development worldwide, advocating for an indefinite moratorium (Yudkowsky, 2023).

However, it is worth noting that the disuse of AI due to under-trust can hamper potential benefits and opportunities, such as inefficient operations, missed opportunities, reduced competitiveness, and customer dissatisfaction. Understanding and mitigating these risks associated with trust in AI are crucial for establishing a balanced and informed approach to AI integration. Striking the right balance between trust and vigilance is essential to harness AI’s benefits while mitigating potential harms and ensuring its responsible and beneficial use in society.

6.4 Psybersecurity Risk Framework

As previously discussed, the misalignment between people’s trust in AI and the actual trustworthiness of AI can significantly influence attitudes and behaviours towards AI. This misalignment may result in disuse, misuse, or abuse of AI, leading to associated risks. Figure 6.2 illustrates these risks.

Figure 6.2 Psybersecurity Risk Framework.

In the era of AI, its impact is pervasive, affecting everyone, whether or not they are directly using AI. It is crucial to emphasise the risks of mass impact when discussing Psybersecurity in the AI era; these risks are situated within the ‘Disuse’ layer. Simultaneously, psychological impacts stem from users’ direct interactions with AI systems or third parties’ malicious use of AI. These factors constitute the risks within the ‘Misuse/Abuse’ layer.

6.4.1 Mass impact

In our modern society, where AI algorithms play a prevalent role in various aspects of our lives, everyone – even those who may not be directly using AI systems – is potentially impacted by AI to varying degrees.

While people enjoy the benefits brought by AI, such as increased efficiency and productivity, they also face the threats and tensions introduced by AI, including the risk of job displacement (World Economic Forum, 2020). According to a PwC study, the progression of AI towards greater autonomy could lead to potential automation risks, affecting approximately 30% of jobs and 44% of workers with lower educational backgrounds by the mid-2030s (Hawksworth et al., 2018). Similarly, the World Economic Forum anticipates that the impact of artificial intelligence and automation may replace 85 million jobs by 2025. However, they also project the creation of 97 million new jobs across 26 countries during the same period (World Economic Forum, 2020). This impact is not limited to non-skilled jobs; even highly professional work faces challenges.

An illustrative example is the recognition of AI-generated artwork winning the top prize in an art competition, showcasing AI’s popularity at a lower cost and quicker response times than human artists (Roose, 2022). This may have a potential psychological impact on individuals with concerns about job displacement by AI, economic uncertainties, and fear of changes in the workforce. Furthermore, many AI-driven applications are developed by extracting data from the open web, teaching algorithms to recognise patterns and relationships, and generating new work in the same style. Professionals who upload their work to the internet may inadvertently contribute to training their algorithmic competitors. This can lead to feelings of anxiety, insecurity, and a loss of pride in one’s work, causing demoralisation and a sense of meaninglessness among specific segments of the population.

Nowadays, AI algorithms are increasingly being utilised for decision-making. Algorithmic prejudice is a factor that can lead to unfairness and have adverse effects on mental health. Psychological harm may result from biased algorithms, leading to unfair or discriminatory outcomes for individuals affected by algorithmic decisions.

AI learns from the data it is fed, making the data quality critical to its effectiveness. Biased or contaminated data can lead to biased outputs from artificial intelligence. For instance, when AI recommends jobs to jobseekers based on their information, it might suggest lower-paying jobs to women due to gender wage gap biases in the database, even if the jobseeker has an excellent educational and professional background (Dastin, 2022). Such biases can make individuals feel ignored and humiliated, leading to psychological harm, including feelings of anger, helplessness, self-doubt, and lowered self-esteem. Algorithms built upon biased datasets or designed with inherent biases can perpetuate and exacerbate existing societal prejudices.

Furthermore, algorithmic bias can contribute to social isolation and stigmatisation. For instance, during the COVID-19 pandemic, China implemented health codes to label individuals based on risk factors, assigning a red code to those needing immediate quarantine (Yu & Carroll, 2023b). If certain groups consistently face unfair treatment or discrimination due to algorithmic decisions, they may experience feelings of frustration, helplessness, marginalisation, diminished self-worth, or exclusion from societal opportunities.

In recent years, AI technologies have been increasingly employed to generate and disseminate news and information, contributing to the proliferation of fake news and misleading information. An investigation into verified true and false news stories distributed on Twitter reveals that falsehoods spread significantly farther, faster, more profound, and more broadly than the truth across all categories of information (Vosoughi et al., 2018). Although more novel than actual news, false news inspires fear, disgust, and surprise in replies. In contrast, true stories evoke feelings of anticipation, sadness, joy, and trust (Vosoughi et al., 2018). Additionally, exposure to false or misleading information generated by AI may lead to psychological distress, such as distorted perceptions of reality.

AI algorithms rely on training with vast amounts of data, much of which is collected from the open web. This raises concerns about the potential breach of users’ personal data without their awareness. Large AI training datasets, such as the LAION-5B dataset with over 5 billion images, raise privacy concerns (Xiang, 2022). These datasets gather diverse internet data without individuals’ awareness, including images of living artists, photographers, medical content, and people who may not expect their data to be used for AI training. Notably, individuals cannot opt out of inclusion in these datasets.

Additionally, some datasets contain disturbing content like manipulated celebrity porn, non-consensual porn, and graphic images, including those of ISIS beheadings (Xiang, 2022). In addition, AI excels at inferential analytics, which involves generating derived or inferred data from a combination of various pieces of information (Wachter & Mittelstadt, 2019). This capability has the potential to pose a threat to people’s privacy as more data is collected and aggregated.

Psychological threats may arise from the widespread use of mass facial scanning and surveillance networks in public places. Various biometric information, such as voiceprints, gait, fingerprints, and faces, can be captured without individuals being aware, leading to breaches of personal privacy. Facial recognition systems can accurately identify people even when they wear masks (Singh, 2017). Additionally, high-resolution cameras can identify faces and license plates in photographs taken from several kilometres away (Schneier, 2019). The surveillance of people’s behaviour can increase psychological considers, manifesting as anxiety and distrust among citizens about how their data is used. This sense of being under suspicion or feeling unsafe may lead to self-censorship, creating a chilling effect. Surveillance is also associated with behavioural changes, including fear of expression, avoidance of public gatherings, and reluctance to exercise the right to protest due to fear of reprisals (Starr et al., 2008).

6.4.2 Direct AI impact

Compared to non-AI users, individuals who actively engage with AI technologies may experience more significant psychological impacts from AI. While moderate use of AI is beneficial to people’s lives and work experiences, excessive reliance on AI can lead to psychological harm, such as dependency issues, reduced critical thinking, and potential addiction.

With the increasing prevalence of AI tools in education, they play a dual role. On the one hand, these tools assist individuals in mastering various skills and expanding their abilities. On the other hand, they introduce challenges and potential risks (Becker et al., 2023). AI tools can potentially make students overly dependent on them, fostering overconfidence that may impede individual learning and training. This over-reliance can lead to students facing difficulties performing as expected once they no longer use these tools, consequently hindering the development of self-confidence (Yu & Carroll, 2023a).

AI in social media, while facilitating efficient communication and providing users with personalised content, can have psychological ramifications for users. Many major tech companies utilise psychographic analysis for customer profiling. Psychographic analysis seeks to examine the subjective attributes of users to comprehend or forecast their actions. This involves scrutinising personality traits, activities, interests, opinions, needs, goals, values, and attitudes (Wells, 1975). Typically, this information is acquired through the analysis of diverse behavioural data. For instance, researchers can unveil users’ opinions, emotions, and attitudes by examining their social interactions on social networks and their sentiments (Chen et al., 2022). AI-driven psychographic analysis can accurately predict a user’s preferences and potential future actions based on their past behaviour or the conduct of other users within the same cohort.

Additionally, it can deploy tailored engagements, such as displaying ads, delivering news, or suggesting connections with people they are likely to connect with. Based on psychographic analysis, the personalised content promoted by social media platforms has the potential to create echo chambers, reinforcing users’ existing beliefs and limiting exposure to diverse viewpoints (Cinelli et al., 2021). This becomes especially concerning when negative thoughts are exposed on social media, as they may be further entrenched and reinforced. Previous studies have indicated a correlation between social media use and mental health issues, including depressive disorders and suicidal behaviour, particularly among young people (Sedgwick et al., 2019). Additionally, research has demonstrated a link between comments on Instagram and the escalating severity of self-injury, suggesting that social media platforms may inadvertently contribute to the reinforcement of harmful behaviours (Brown et al., 2017). In addition, algorithmic distortions can lead to distorted perceptions of reality and self-worth, resulting in negative consequences such as the spread of misinformation or manipulated behaviour. For instance, young people may be susceptible to suicidal behaviour when exposed to information about the suicidal behaviour of others (Dunlop et al., 2011; Haw et al., 2013).

In addition, concerns about supernormal stimuli arise in AI and technology, suggesting that individuals may become overly attracted to and immersed in artificial stimuli designed to be more enticing or rewarding than real-life experiences (Luxton & Watson, 2023). This concern is particularly pronounced with AI technologies such as virtual reality environments and chatbots, which can generate rich and engaging content. Overexposure to these extraneous stimuli raises concerns about the social and emotional well-being of individuals, potentially leading to reduced connectedness in real-life relationships and adverse mental health outcomes. This may include feelings of isolation and disconnection from the tangible world. It can also distort the user’s perspective on interpersonal relationships. For example, instructing a digital assistant to perform tasks on one’s behalf can create the illusion of having power over another person (Fast & Schroeder, 2020).

The COVID-19 pandemic has profoundly transformed the way people work and live globally. We have witnessed a growing shift in learning, shopping, and socialising from offline to online (Caroppo et al., 2021). Technologies associated with the metaverse, such as augmented reality (AR), virtual reality (VR), and mixed reality (MR), are introducing real-world experiences into new virtual spaces. Virtual reality technology utilises various hardware and software tools that interact with our senses (sight, hearing, touch, smell, sense of balance, etc.) to construct an environment recognised as real by people’s bodies and minds (Dremliuga et al., 2020). However, the experience of being in a virtual environment can have tangible effects on the body and mind. Studies have indicated that individuals in VR may exhibit fear when walking across a plank of wood on the ground in the real world, even if it is placed between virtual roofs (Wang et al., 2019). Virtual reality users may even experience physical harm, such as a heart attack induced by a game (Cleworth et al., 2016). In addition, individuals may become overly immersed in AI-created virtual environments, potentially leading to detachment from reality.

6.4.3 Malicious use of AI

With the increasing intelligence of AI, AI-driven applications have become widely accessible, exposing people to the malicious use of AI and AI-enabled crimes (Blauth et al., 2022). AI algorithms heavily depend on data training, creating vulnerabilities that can be exploited for nefarious purposes. Precisely, injecting specially crafted adversarial data into a target training dataset can manipulate machine learning results – a technique known as data poisoning (Wang & Chaudhuri, 2018). An illustrative example involves perturbed data altering the results of self-driving cars’ recognition of traffic signs (Ding et al., 2019). Detecting data poisoning is challenging and may leave a backdoor for manipulation if AI models adopt the poisoned data (Chen et al., 2017). Consequently, individuals affected by AI trained on poisoned data may be misled or manipulated, leading to unpleasant or unfair experiences that could be potentially harmful to their well-being.

In addition, AI has been increasingly used for fraud or deception. One example is the misuse of deepfakes for deception, where artificial intelligence is employed to create and disseminate convincingly fake or misleading images, videos, and media. This technology poses serious risks by manipulating content in ways that are challenging to detect, potentially leading to deception or misinformation. Instances have been reported where malicious individuals use Deepfake-generated composite portraits and fake social media profiles to approach and connect with thousands of people on LinkedIn. When a target accepts these connections, other users may perceive it as an endorsement, potentially lowering their guard (Raphael, 2021). The negative impacts of AI-enabled deception can lead to feelings of betrayal, mistrust, and fear, with detrimental effects on mental health. Some criminals exploit AI software for illicit purposes, such as using voice synthesis software to mimic the voices of victims’ friends and relatives for fraudulent activities. This results in financial losses, undermines interpersonal trust, and instils a sense of insecurity, causing emotional distress. Concerns arise about using deepfakes to spread misinformation or propaganda, contributing to a decline in trust in information and institutions. This erosion of trust can lead to breaking social norms and values and increased social polarisation.

AI can also be exploited for social engineering attacks. A study shows that social robots could employ psychological manipulation techniques to deceive people into providing private information without their awareness (Abate et al., 2020). Chatbots can also be manipulated for malicious purposes. For instance, employing a method called DAN, or ‘Do Anything Now’, researchers have successfully bypassed ChatGPT’s safety features, triggering the generation of content that violates OpenAI’s policies against violence, offensive material, and sexually explicit content. There have also been reported cases of Bing being exploited to threaten users (Perrigo, 2023).

6.5 Discussion

Based on the preceding discussion, addressing the risks and psychological impact arising from the misalignment of people’s trust and AI’s trustworthiness can be approached through a user-centric approach and an AI-centric approach. The user-centric approach focuses on increasing users’ awareness of AI’s risks and threats, cultivating a balanced trust in AI, and ultimately using AI in moderation. Promoting a better understanding of AI and cultivating appropriate risk awareness through educational initiatives and awareness training is crucial. Comprehensive awareness training enables users to assess the potential risks and impacts associated with AI accurately. This, in turn, promotes the healthy and informed use of AI and mitigates the potential for disuse, misuse, or abuse resulting from over- or under-trust due to misconceptions.

The AI-centric approach focuses on increasing trustworthiness from technical, ethical, and legal perspectives. From a technical perspective, increasing transparency can help reduce bias or errors and increase the reliability and trustworthiness of AI. From an ethical perspective, creating and refining an industry standard could increase accountability and benefit the created responsible and ethical AI. Moreover, a robust legal framework is the bottom line for avoiding and defeating malicious or illegal creation and use of AI.

6.5.1 Awareness training

As discussed above, proper awareness is crucial to using AI to benefit people’s lives in modern society. Both over-trust and under-trust can hinder the healthy use of AI. Awareness training is essential in today’s AI era, not only for AI professionals and AI users but also for every ordinary person, because AI is indeed affecting everyone’s lives, even if they do not directly use AI. The public should have the right to know if their data is being used for AI training or decision-making. They should also be informed if an AI is being used for a purpose related to them and what steps they can take to mitigate the impact.

Perceptions of risks, whether accurate or not, significantly impact trust in AI (Sanders et al., 2011). Users should understand the potential vulnerabilities, security threats, and unintended consequences of AI and the ethical and psychological implications of AI technologies. People can trust and use its capabilities better when they understand how AI works.

Education on the proper use of AI is critical for the public, especially youth, to promote a balanced use of AI technologies. This education aims to prevent over-dependence and mitigate potential adverse psychological effects associated with dependency. Managing the psychological impact of immersive virtual worlds by promoting healthy boundaries and awareness of virtual experiences is essential for informed decision-making and effective use of AI tools.

6.5.2 Transparency in AI

Transparency in AI is crucial in artificial intelligence, serving as the bedrock for building trust and mitigating risks associated with AI systems. It is pivotal in enhancing trust among AI developers, users, and the broader public. When individuals comprehend how AI systems operate, make decisions, and draw conclusions, they are more inclined to place trust in the technology.

AI autonomously learns from existing data, identifying patterns and rules for new data. While users are familiar with the inputs and outputs, the internal process remains opaque, known as the ‘black box effect’ of AI processing (Castelvecchi, 2016). Although this learning process is highly efficient and can quickly adapt to vast amounts of data, it cannot provide the reasons and logic behind generated outputs, leading to potential errors, such as misidentifying an image with orange and black stripes as a school bus. The opacity of the black box hinders people from understanding AI, resulting in a lack of trust in its operations.

To address the problems posed by the black box effect, a possible solution is to employ explainable artificial intelligence (XAI) to generate explanations for AI recognition systems that elucidate the causes of the activity leading to a given classification (Das et al., 2023). For end users, understanding how AI systems reach conclusions is of paramount importance. Explainable AI provides users with insights into the decision-making process, enhancing the user-friendliness of the technology and enabling individuals to make informed judgements based on AI-generated outputs, thus reducing the negative psychological impact of AI (Castelvecchi, 2016). When system users clearly understand the factors influencing a specific output and the scenarios in which the system may not function effectively, they are more likely to trust the decisions (Shin, 2021).

6.5.3 Industry regulations

In the rapidly evolving landscape of the AI industry, establishing industry standards and regulations is crucial for cultivating a healthy environment of competition and collaboration. While legislation usually lags far behind the development of science and technology, industry self-regulation is a pivotal approach to guide the development of AI along ethical and responsible pathways.

Ethical considerations and responsible AI practices should be inherent in the industry. Regulations must provide a framework for delineating ethical standards in both the development and application of AI, thereby setting clear guidelines for AI practitioners and organisations. This will ensure accountability and prevent the misuse of AI technologies that could lead to ethical violations. AI developers should protect users’ tangible and intangible assets and recognise and address the psychological impact on individuals. In addition, industry standards should be created to address the potential biases in AI algorithms by mandating fairness assessments and providing guidelines for mitigating biases. This will ensure that AI systems do not perpetuate discrimination or exacerbate existing social inequalities.

Furthermore, international regulation cooperation becomes imperative as AI technologies transcend national boundaries. Global harmonisation of regulations builds trust in AI systems and facilitates the responsible use of AI technologies globally. This collaborative effort underscores the shared responsibility of the global AI community to steer the trajectory of AI development towards ethical and socially responsible practices.

In recent years, an increasing number of industry standards and regulations have been proposed, focusing on the trustworthiness of AI. In April 2019, the European Commission’s High-Level Expert Group on Artificial Intelligence presented Ethics Guidelines for Trustworthy Artificial Intelligence, outlining that trustworthy AI should be lawful, ethical, and robust (European Commission, 2019). The International Organization for Standardization (ISO) published an overview of trustworthiness in artificial intelligence in 2020. The survey covered topics such as establishing trust in AI systems, addressing engineering pitfalls and associated threats, and assessing factors like availability, resilience, reliability, accuracy, safety, security, and privacy (International Standards Organisation, 2020). Similarly, the US Government Accountability Office (GAO) published an AI accountability framework to ensure accountability and responsible use of artificial intelligence (AI) in government programmes and processes (US Government Accountability Office, 2021). In June 2023, the IEEE Standards Association approved a standard for security and trustworthiness requirements in Generative Pretrained AI Models. The standard aims to enhance the reliability and trustworthiness of generative pre-trained AI models throughout their entire life cycle, covering development, deployment, and usage (European Union, 2021).

6.5.4 Legal frameworks

Legal frameworks play a crucial role in safeguarding individual rights and privacy in the context of advancing AI technologies. Regulations can set boundaries on the collection, use, and storage of personal data, ensuring that AI systems operate within ethical and legal constraints, thus fostering trust among users.

Legal frameworks can provide a basis for establishing accountability and assigning responsibility for the actions and decisions made by AI systems. In order to enhance control over the potential risks associated with AI systems, many countries and regions are actively working towards developing responsible AI practices (Greenleaf, 2019). In addition, more than 130 countries worldwide have enacted privacy laws and regulations to protect personal privacy (Wachter, 2019). This is essential to ensure that individuals or entities developing, deploying, or using AI technologies are held accountable for any adverse consequences or risks associated with AI applications.

However, we should also recognise that many existing laws concentrate on the unlawful collection or disclosure of data but provide limited coverage for the improper use of data post-collection (European Union, 2022). The issue of ‘lawful acquisition and subsequent misuse’ remains unaddressed, mainly in the context of big data. In addition, while data protection laws legally govern the procedures of collecting, transmitting, processing, and preserving data from data subjects, it is worth noting that data controllers can generate new information through inference that may not be fully safeguarded by existing data protection laws (Wachter & Mittelstadt, 2019).

The EU’s proposed AI Act, introduced in April 2021, categorises AI applications into three risk levels, establishing a framework where applications with unacceptable risks would face prohibition, high-risk applications would be subject to specific legal requirements, and applications not explicitly banned or labelled as high-risk would remain largely unregulated (European Union, 2022; Greenleaf, 2019). Nevertheless, some scholars have raised concerns about the merging of ‘trustworthiness’ with the ‘acceptability of risk’, highlighting the potential for a misalignment between the perceived level of trust and the actual trustworthiness of the implemented AI (Laux et al., 2022).

6.6 Conclusion

In an era marked by the growing reliance on artificial intelligence, individuals are increasingly impacted by AI, either directly or indirectly. While reaping the benefits of convenience and efficiency offered by AI, there are also inherent risks. Previous research has predominantly focused on tangible risks associated with AI, such as digital, physical, and political security, which are somewhat measurable. However, the deepening interaction between humans and AI necessitates an exploration of their psychological impact on individuals. When AI is assigned tasks, it is essentially entrusted with trust by people, acknowledging the acceptance of potential risks. The interplay between people’s trust and AI’s trustworthiness significantly contributes to the adoption of AI and the subsequent risks.

Ideally, when individuals possess a moderate understanding of AI, are aware of its risks, and apply appropriate trust in its applications, there is a healthy use of AI. However, over-trusting AI may lead to misuse, such as neglecting risks, lacking critical thinking, or compromising moral standards for AI abuse. Conversely, under-trusting AI may result in disuse, causing potential inefficiencies, missed opportunities, and reduced competitiveness in a society embracing AI advancements.

The consequences of disuse, misuse, and abuse resulting from the misalignment between people’s trust and AI’s trustworthiness can lead to psychological impacts, whether individuals use AI directly or indirectly. Non-AI users may experience mass impacts like anxiety and insecurity due to the threat of job replacement. Furthermore, potentially biased algorithms, unfair treatment, fake news, and misleading information generated by AI may contribute to psychological distress and distorted perceptions of reality. Privacy concerns arise from AI training data collection, particularly with facial scanning and surveillance networks, leading to chilling effects, self-censorship, and behavioural changes such as fear of expression and avoidance of public gatherings.

For active AI users, excessive reliance on AI can result in over-dependency and a loss of self-confidence when AI is unavailable. The echo chamber effect may limit exposure to diverse information, hindering personal advancement and reinforcing negative beliefs, leading to mental health issues. Additionally, supernormal stimuli created by AI can overly engage individuals in AI-generated virtual environments, causing detachment from reality and harming social and emotional well-being.

Individuals are also impacted by the malicious use of AI, including deception through AI-generated text, video, audio, and connections from individuals with AI-generated profiles. This malicious use not only leads to digital and financial losses but also induces feelings of betrayal, mistrust, and fear, with detrimental effects on mental health.

The authors advocate for balancing user trust and AI trustworthiness to address these risks. This involves cultivating appropriate risk awareness through training, encompassing an understanding of vulnerabilities and security threats, and the development of critical-thinking skills. Simultaneously, efforts should be directed towards enhancing AI’s trustworthiness by increasing transparency, refining industry regulations, and establishing robust legal frameworks.

Although ‘artificial intelligence’ was only invented a few decades ago, it has fundamentally changed our lives. In today’s era of artificial intelligence, no matter whether we trust it or use it, everyone will inevitably be affected by it. The impact of artificial intelligence on human mental health should receive more and more attention. Establishing balanced trust in artificial intelligence, using artificial intelligence wisely, and making full use of its capabilities are the best ways for us to deal with it in the AI era. Only in this way can we fully harness AI’s potential benefits while mitigating potential risks, ensuring a future where technology positively contributes to the betterment of humanity.
