===== FILE 16/19: xhtml%2fch07.xhtml =====

Chapter 7 Security through influence over mandate

Josh Armitage and Oliver Guidetti

DOI: 10.1201/9781032664859-7

7.1 Introduction
7.1.1 Make security tangible, not another compliance slideshow

Security must be more than a checklist or a set of abstract principles presented in compliance meetings. It should be embedded in the daily workflows and processes, making it a tangible part of the organisational culture. This means creating real-world scenarios and simulations that mirror the organisation’s potential security threats and challenges. By doing so, employees can understand the practical implications of security breaches and the importance of adhering to security protocols. Interactive training sessions, gamified security challenges, and hands-on workshops can make security concepts more relatable and engaging, ensuring they are taken seriously across all levels of the organisation.

7.1.2 Ease, not pain, should be synonymous with security

Often, security measures are seen as cumbersome, slowing down operations and hindering productivity. This perception needs to change. Security protocols should be designed with the user experience in mind, ensuring they are seamless and non-intrusive. This can be achieved by integrating user-friendly security tools, automating repetitive security tasks, and adopting single sign-on (SSO) systems to reduce password fatigue. By making security measures easy and convenient, employees are more likely to adopt and adhere to them, enhancing the organisation’s overall security posture.

7.1.3 Resistance is necessary and good

Resistance in this context refers to the organisation’s resilience to security threats and ability to adapt to new challenges. It is about building a culture where continuously questioning and challenging the status quo is encouraged to improve security measures. This involves regular security audits, penetration testing, and encouraging a culture of open feedback where employees feel comfortable reporting potential security issues. By fostering an environment where resistance is seen as a means to strengthen security, organisations can become more agile and responsive to emerging threats.

7.1.4 Security is achieved through continuous, small steps

Implementing robust security measures is not a one-time event but a continuous process. It involves regular updates, patches, and improvements to security protocols. This pattern emphasises the importance of taking incremental steps to enhance security rather than attempting to overhaul systems in one go, which can be overwhelming and disruptive. Continuous integration and continuous deployment (CI/CD) practices can be adapted to include security checks and balances, ensuring that security considerations are integrated into every stage of the software development life cycle.

7.1.5 Security is enablement, not gatekeeping

Traditionally, security has been viewed as a barrier to innovation and speed. However, this perspective must shift towards seeing security as an enabler of business agility and growth. Integrating security into the early stages of project development and decision-making processes can facilitate faster time-to-market and innovation. This requires a collaborative approach where security teams work closely with other departments to understand their needs and tailor security measures that support their objectives rather than hinder them. By embracing these patterns, organisations can foster a security culture that is proactive, integrated, and aligned with their business goals. This protects the organisation from potential threats and enhances its competitiveness in the digital age.

7.2 Pattern 1: Make Security Tangible, Not Another Compliance Slideshow

Companies often say security is critical, yet their actions tell a different story. This gap is evident in the widespread use of generic, compliance-focused training modules that fail to engage or educate employees meaningfully. The use of monotonous, artificial voices in training materials, especially in the era of advanced generative AI (GenAI), further alienates employees, making the experience even less engaging. Most security training is poorly crafted compliance slideshows, with poorly generated voices that sound all the worse in the world of GenAI. The measure of success is the compliance percentage, not any visceral security improvement. The conversations off the back of the training come in two flavours: the irritation at slogging through the slides and the competition around how fast you can complete it. When security is genuinely seen as critical, to quote the suffragettes, you see it in deeds, not words. When elevating security across organisations, you reap what you sew.

7.2.1 Adding to the cultural fabric

Actual behavioural change in cybersecurity is achievable when training transcends traditional methods and becomes an intrinsic part of organisational life. Investing time and resources into practical, hands-on activities that resonate with employees can significantly enhance an organisation’s security posture far beyond the superficiality of ‘vanity metrics’.

By working in a consultancy where time is money, I have seen tangible security training drive the business forward. Routinely, we stopped all client work, and therefore all revenue, for an entire day to attack simulations that made the security impacts of our decisions real and tangible. The stories from these events have since become the myths on which the security culture is founded. Slides cannot shape culture, but behaviour change does. The typical security training standards used across businesses cannot drive real change.

Practical security training changes behaviour and culture; success cannot be measured in a single metric. If taking people away from their day jobs to invest in security is infeasible, then security is not a proper priority. When it is a priority, you can see the effects, such as Amazon Web Services’ track record and their oft-repeated quote, ‘Security is job zero’ (Rodgers, 2018).

Although it often feels like there is no time in the day for security or other supposed priorities, when looking at healthcare, we can see how, even in emergency departments, it is possible to make time for the things that truly matter. In Feel-Good Productivity, Abdaal (2023) talks through his first emergency department shift, where, although the emergency department is overloaded, he is told to ensure he takes a break; otherwise, he risks making avoidable mistakes by pushing through. As you will see in pattern 4, the same applies to security. If we do not make the time, risks compound until a cataclysm eventuates. The right approach is more impactful than the typical playbook we are generally subjected to; a single game day, while seemingly more expensive, will deliver more change than a million slides.

7.2.2 Accelerating the spread of knowledge

In the intricate dance of cybersecurity, the weak link phenomenon is not just a possibility but an inevitability. The stark reality is that a single oversight can unfurl into a full-blown breach, spotlighting the critical need for a uniformly fortified team. Therefore, when one person can inadvertently be the source of the breach, how do you ensure everyone levels up through security exercises?

The crux lies in the human inclination to defer to a central figure during upheavals. This single-person dependency, often latent and at the worst possible moment, emerges in the throes of a crisis, exposing vulnerabilities at the most inopportune juncture.

The imperative is to equip everyone with the acumen to navigate security incidents adeptly. Drawing inspiration from the collaborative ethos of pair programming, one can orchestrate scenarios where knowledge dissemination is encouraged and necessitated. Deliberately assigning seasoned personnel to observer roles sets the stage for the less experienced to engage actively, thereby democratising expertise. By borrowing from the school of pair programming, there are methods where you can channel expertise to uplift less experienced team members. When running security training, by locking certain people into a hands-off role, you force them to let others get hands-on, maximising the learning opportunity. For cyber resilience, institutional knowledge is far more potent than tacit knowledge. A robust cyber posture cannot be built from heroism; it is constructed from ever-raising standards.

The cornerstone of cyber resilience lies in the organisation’s collective wisdom rather than isolated champions’ prowess. Heroic acts, though commendable, are ephemeral in their impact. In contrast, a culture of continuous improvement and shared learning lays the groundwork for a resilient cyber posture. Therefore, you must ensure everyone’s cyber competence is continually raised. While not everyone can or should be experts, your cyber posture is not defined by your peaks but by your troughs. The better you spread cyber knowledge, the more resilient the organisation is. The goal is not to mould every member into a cybersecurity virtuoso but to elevate the baseline understanding and preparedness across the organisation. The vulnerabilities, not the strengths, delineate the boundaries of your cyber defence. By broadening the dissemination of cyber knowledge, you fortify the organisation’s resilience.

In times of crisis, people tend to cluster around a select few individuals. However, this instinctual behaviour can be counterbalanced by empowering others with the necessary skills and confidence. Creating an environment where competence and confidence are distributed equally is essential, so everyone is prepared and ready to act when a crisis occurs. The focus should not be on relying on a few individuals but on the collective readiness of the entire group.

Team of Teams by McChrystal et al. (2015) argues for optimising effectiveness rather than efficiency in complex environments. By spreading security knowledge through the organisation, you build the resilience and adaptability needed to perform in a crisis. It is the same reasoning behind first aid courses, the FAST acronym for identifying strokes and DRSABCD for choking. Not everyone can be a medical expert, but lives are saved daily through a distributed level of competence.

7.2.3 The difference between reading and knowing the incident response plan

Acknowledging that just because procedures like an incident response plan are read does not necessarily mean they are fully understood and retained. The efficacy of traditional methods of familiarising oneself with such critical procedures is often questionable. It is crucial to explore alternative means of learning that may be more effective in retaining information. Typically, the engagement with such a plan is limited to obligatory annual readings, which, while ensuring nominal familiarity, fall short of ingraining the details and protocols deeply into the organisation’s collective consciousness. The challenge lies in ensuring that individuals have read the document and in verifying that they comprehend its contents and can recall and apply this knowledge when necessary.

The stark reality is that, despite having ostensibly read the incident response plan, many might struggle to recount its specifics or even pinpoint its location when pressed. This superficial engagement underscores the gap between mere exposure to information and meaningful internalisation.

Transitioning from passive reading to active practice significantly enhances retention and understanding. Engaging in practical exercises that simulate potential incidents can elevate retention rates dramatically, from the paltry 10% associated with reading to a more robust 75%. Furthermore, taking on the role of an instructor and imparting this knowledge to others can further solidify one’s grasp of the material, potentially boosting retention to an impressive 90%, as Hallam et al. (1955) suggested.

Simulating realistic scenarios ensures that the theoretical knowledge of the incident response plan imparted is tested and reinforced, making it second nature to the team members. This hands-on approach ensures that, even though ‘no plan survives contact with the enemy’, the core principles and steps of the plan are so well internalised that they serve as a reliable guide under duress. The plan, thus internalised, transforms into a mental checklist that guides individuals through the desired actions even in high-pressure situations.

It is crucial, therefore, that familiarity with the incident response plan is cultivated well in advance of any actual crisis – regular drills. As explored in ‘The Checklist Manifesto’ (Gawande, 2014), checklists improve outcomes under pressure, irrespective of anyone’s expertise. They are a cornerstone of the safety culture in both aviation and medicine. Nevertheless, given that it should be the cornerstone of our incident response, the plan rests collecting dust on a virtual shelf.

The ability to respond to an incident is a matter of experience, often acquired by dealing with unforeseen situations. Incidents are the most expensive way to gather said experience. However, incidents are unpredictable and expensive, primarily when they damage or harm people or property. Simply reading the plan once a year is insufficient to ensure that people retain the information or take appropriate action when needed. To be able to act in a planned and effective manner, people must not only read the plan but also internalise it and make it a part of their daily routine. This way, the plan becomes a living document that they can use to guide their actions and decisions, rather than a static document that is rarely consulted.

Being able to respond to an incident is a matter of experience. Incidents are the most expensive way to gather said experience. If all people do is read the plan annually, you cannot expect them to retain the information, let alone act on it. If you want people to act in a planned fashion, they do not need to read the plan; they need to live it.

7.2.4 The discipline of security chaos engineering

Security chaos engineering is a technical practice that prepares organisations for inevitable cyber incidents (Shortridge & Rinehart, 2023). It involves creating and running realistic security scenarios that simulate potential attacks. By adopting a proactive approach, security chaos engineering can help organisations identify system vulnerabilities and improve incident response. This emerging field is based on Netflix’s chaos engineering approach but has been adapted to prioritise cyber resilience. Security chaos engineering recognises that our systems today are socio-technical and that vulnerabilities can arise from both security’s technical and social aspects. Unlike traditional techniques like penetration testing and threat modelling that only focus on the technical side, security chaos engineering takes a holistic view. It assumes vulnerabilities exist and exercises teams’ incident response to build true cyber resiliency. Security chaos engineering can help organisations prepare for cyber incidents and minimise their impact by exploring the social side of incidents. Security chaos engineering is required for modern organisations, as cyber incidents are inevitable. It is impossible to prove that a system has no vulnerabilities, so organisations must assume that it is a matter of when they will be successfully attacked, not if. The effectiveness of incident response is determined by people more than any process or tool. As all our systems are socio-technical, security chaos engineering effectively validates the security posture of an organisation’s systems. By including the human factor in security testing, organisations can improve their incident response and minimise the impact of cyber incidents.

7.2.5 Summary
The array of slides in front of us as ‘security training’ does not work.
Companies undermine their security by taking actions incongruent with their rhetoric.
Valuable security training becomes part of the organisation’s fabric, ensuring the lessons are remembered and embedded.
Ensure that security training optimises knowledge sharing, not building single-person dependencies.
If all people ever do is read, you cannot expect them to act as you intend during a crisis.
Security chaos engineering is an emerging practice that embodies this sentiment and is built for the reality of socio-technical systems.
You need to make security real and tangible to expect people to care genuinely.
7.3 Pattern 2: Ease, Not Pain, Should Be Synonymous with Security

There is a widely accepted belief that making something more secure often makes it harder to use. However, if we make something too complex when trying to change behaviour, we discourage such change. Like lightning, humans tend to seek the path of least resistance. Therefore, we must ensure that the secure option is also the easiest. Unfortunately, this aspect is often overlooked when implementing new security measures. Making something more complex when trying to change behaviour disincentivises the change. Humans are like lightning; they seek the path of least resistance. We need to make the secure option also the easiest option. Unfortunately, this point is often missed when implementing new security controls. Make something too complicated, and people will find a workaround, undermining everything you try to achieve. It is essential to influence people’s behaviour towards security rather than mandating it.

7.3.1 Mindfully managing tension

Cybersecurity is a delicate balancing act that requires managing the needs and requirements of different groups with competing interests. As a cybersecurity professional, your primary responsibility is maintaining security while ensuring operations run smoothly. However, it is equally important to understand and empathise with the people affected by your decisions. Failure to do so can lead to significant resistance and pushback.

Incentivising compliance is essential to strike the right balance instead of punishing non-compliance. As Princess Leia Organa famously said, ‘The more you tighten your grip, the more star systems will slip through your fingers’. When implementing security measures, you have two options to reduce the burden on people: abstract the control or provide training to make it easier to observe. Abstraction involves simplifying the process using fingerprint logins instead of complex password schemes. On the other hand, training is about helping people understand the importance of security and handling sensitive data correctly. It is not just about the control itself but also about the cognitive load the control places on people (Sweller, 1988). By understanding this delicate balance, you can ensure that your cybersecurity measures are effective and that your organisation’s operations run smoothly.

You need to avoid having blinkers on when looking at security controls; there is not just a risk impact of introducing a control; there is an operational one as well. Managing this tension between mitigating risk and impeding operations is crucial to long-term success. As Sisyphus would tell you, you can only push uphill for so long (Fry, 2018). Every control is a trade-off between risk and operations; you can lessen the operational impact through abstraction and training, building momentum within your company instead of resistance.

7.3.2 Dealing with a complex world

A robust protective posture is the central goal of cybersecurity. This requires a strategic approach that goes beyond merely implementing a large number of controls. Unfortunately, many organisations adopt a shallow approach that solely relies on adding controls without considering their value. As a result, the cybersecurity landscape becomes cluttered with redundant, ineffective, or even counterproductive controls. Such an approach can make it challenging to identify and address the real threats and can ultimately lead to a false sense of security.

The Cynefin framework shows how you must understand your domain before taking appropriate action. The organisation you work for and its technical systems are complex adaptive systems (Miller & Page, 2009; Snowden, 2020). Therefore, you are working within the complex domain. This means you cannot predict the consequences of an action before you take it. It would be best to verify that the controls you are implementing are causing the correct behaviour changes and do not have unintended side effects that outweigh the value of the control.

It is essential to ensure that control has the desired impact when implemented. Many controls are put in place despite not providing much value. For example, password rotation policies are ineffective but still used in many places. In complex systems, it is hard to predict cause and effect. So, when introducing a new control, it is essential to carefully examine its impact and ensure it causes the intended changes in behaviour without any adverse effects. Since these systems are complex, what works in one situation might not work in another. That is why having a tailored and adaptable approach to cybersecurity is essential.

One crucial aspect of cybersecurity that is often overlooked is the need to thoroughly assess the impact of control after it has been implemented. A classic example of this is the practice of password rotation policies, which are widely adopted but may not always be effective and can even lead to user fatigue and insecure password practices. Verifying that controls have the desired impact and eliminating ineffective ones is essential. This requires the ability to choose and implement controls and the discernment to identify and remove those that fail to add value. By continuously evaluating and adjusting the cybersecurity framework, it can remain agile, relevant, and effective without being weighed down by outdated or ineffective measures.

7.3.3 Beware premature abstractions

While abstractions can be valuable, creating a helpful abstraction is tricky; for example, Hohpe (2020) emphasises the inherent value of abstractions while cautioning against the complexities of creating constructive ones. Abstracting a problem is not merely an act of simplification but requires a profound understanding of the underlying issues. This understanding is often overlooked, leading to premature abstractions that bring more harm than good, causing frustration and inconvenience for those interacting with these abstractions, particularly in contexts as demanding as developer workstations.

Developers, known for their rigorous requirements, often find themselves at odds with overly simplified solutions that aim to provide security through an ‘out-of-the-box’ experience. These solutions, while well-intentioned, fail to encompass the myriad of unique and intricate needs that developers face. The challenge lies not in creating a one-size-fits-all abstraction but in empowering developers to tailor solutions within a framework of guidelines. This approach acknowledges the limitations of abstractions and the importance of providing flexibility and ownership to the end users.

Recognising the limitations of one’s abstractions is essential in developing effective solutions. Abstractions can be powerful tools that alleviate problems when executed with precision and care. However, when done hastily or without sufficient understanding, they can exacerbate issues, eroding trust within an organisation. Each unsuccessful abstraction compounds the difficulty of gaining acceptance for future endeavours, underscoring the necessity of a cautious and informed approach.

When dealing with cybersecurity, it is essential to use abstraction cautiously. Although it can help simplify complex issues and make them more manageable, it is essential to consider the particular requirements and challenges of the situation before applying it. Only then can we fully leverage its potential to enhance security measures significantly. The key lies in striking the right balance between simplification and customisation, ensuring that abstractions empower rather than hinder the individuals they are designed to assist.

7.3.4 Security is dynamic, not static

It is essential to understand that security is a dynamic system that is not static. This aligns with the business adage that change is the only constant, highlighting the need for cybersecurity strategies to be adaptable and forward-thinking. To effectively deal with the ever-evolving landscape of cybersecurity, it is necessary to employ thoughtful methods for decision-making. This ensures that security measures are robust and responsive to emerging threats and vulnerabilities.

The goal of enhancing a business’s security posture extends beyond merely attempting to halt change; it involves actively shaping it to foster a secure yet flexible environment. This approach is exemplified in managing developer workstations, where the emphasis is on enabling developers to implement rapid changes securely. For instance, when developers require the installation of new software, providing an automated, dynamic risk assessment facilitates their progress while maintaining security. This focus on enabling movement rather than maintaining a static position acknowledges the inherent nature of change and the importance of securing it.

The concept of ‘desire paths’, as discussed by Tommes (2022), serves as a poignant metaphor in this context. In urban planning, desire paths emerge as individuals naturally seek the most efficient routes, often diverging from the meticulously designed pathways. Rather than attempting to restrict access to these user-created paths, formalising them is more pragmatic and beneficial, thereby ensuring their safety and utility. This analogy aptly illustrates the necessity of being attuned to the dynamic nature of systems, including cybersecurity, and the value of adapting to rather than resisting natural tendencies and behaviours.

Viewing security posture as a dynamic system is crucial for accurately reflecting the fluid nature of cybersecurity threats and solutions. It is not about controlling the security posture at any given moment but influencing its trajectory over time. This perspective encourages a focus on shaping positive changes and trends in security practices rather than fixating on static metrics such as the number of control violations or findings from penetration tests.

The concept of living in the ‘first derivative’, as articulated by Hohpe (2020), emphasises the importance of focusing on the rate of change and trends in cybersecurity rather than absolute positions at any given time. By adopting this mindset, organisations can navigate the complexities of cybersecurity more effectively, ensuring that their strategies are responsive to current challenges and anticipate future developments, focusing on trends, not specific numbers.

7.3.5 Take the product mindset

Adopting a customer-centric product mindset when delivering security solutions significantly shifts from traditional project-based approaches. This perspective focuses on creating compelling security solutions that meet users’ needs and encourage their adoption by being inherently desirable and impactful. The essence of a product mindset lies in its emphasis on providing value and impact, aiming to align security measures with user convenience, challenging the conventional dichotomy that positions security and ease of use as mutually exclusive.

Historically, security has often been framed within an adversarial context, emphasising restrictions and limitations instead of enabling and empowering users. This approach has frequently led to resistance and a lack of progress in enhancing organisational security. On the other hand, when security solutions are designed to be appealing and to integrate seamlessly with users’ workflows, they gain traction and facilitate widespread organisational adoption. This concept aligns with Marc Andreessen’s idea of product-market fit. The market eagerly adopts and pulls the most successful products rather than being pushed onto users (Griffin, 2024).

Achieving product-market fit with security solutions can fundamentally alter the security landscape within an organisation. It shifts the focus from completing security projects to creating sustainable, user-friendly security products that resonate with end users. This approach emphasises outcomes over outputs, encouraging security teams to take ownership of challenges and prioritise actions that benefit the organisation most.

The principle of treating internal users with the same level of consideration as external customers is crucial in this context. Just as a product approach is employed to meet the needs and preferences of external customers, internal users deserve security solutions designed with their needs, preferences, and conveniences in mind. This philosophy underpins the move towards transformative security practices that are effective and embraced by users across the organisation.

7.4 Pattern 3: Resistance Is Necessary and Reasonable

Adopting a customer-centric product mindset, rather than viewing security as a mere series of projects, fundamentally shifts the approach to delivering security solutions within an organisation. A product mindset prioritises the creation of solutions that are not only compelling but also inherently secure, thereby making security an integral, desirable feature rather than a cumbersome add-on. This approach is predicated on the impact of the security measures, aiming to make secure practices both accessible and attractive to users. It challenges the traditional, adversarial view of security, which often pits ease of use against security, creating a barrier to widespread adoption and genuine progress.

Marc Andreessen’s concept of product-market fit, as cited by Griffin in 2017, is particularly relevant here. Achieving product-market fit in the context of security means developing solutions that users actively want and need, effectively pulling the product into the market. This alignment transforms the role of security within an organisation, moving it from a mandatory, often resisted requirement to a valued and sought-after feature. By focusing on outcomes rather than outputs, a product mindset empowers security teams to assume ownership of challenges, prioritise effectively, and make a tangible impact. It raises the question of why internal customers – essentially, the organisation’s employees – should receive less consideration than external customers in developing and implementing security solutions.

Embracing change and managing resistance is critical to implementing a product-centric approach to security. As Block (2023) suggested in 2023, resistance is inevitable and a sign of meaningful change. Organisations are composed of individuals with established patterns of behaviour, often resistant to alteration, encapsulated in the phrase ‘that is how we do things here’. Understanding that both excessive and insufficient resistance can herald failure is crucial. Successfully navigating this resistance, identifying its sources, and employing mitigation strategies are essential for enacting lasting change.

In this context, the ability to manage resistance effectively is directly linked to the strength of an organisation’s cybersecurity posture. Implementing change on a large scale, particularly in cybersecurity, requires a nuanced understanding of human behaviour, organisational culture, and the delicate balance between innovation and tradition. By approaching security with a product mindset and adept at managing resistance, organisations can foster a culture that accepts and embraces secure practices, leading to a more resilient and adaptable cybersecurity posture.

7.4.1 People react differently to change

Models help to reason about how change permeates through an organisation. The model introduced by Wardley (2023), categorising organisational members into explorers, villagers, and town planners, offers a valuable framework for understanding how change is adopted and implemented within an organisation. This model suggests that an organisation comprises three broad categories of people and teams, each with distinct attitudes towards change and innovation.

Explorers are characterised by their affinity for novelty and eagerness to venture into uncharted territories. They are the pioneers within an organisation, always looking for new methodologies, technologies, and processes that could potentially enhance its capabilities. Their role is crucial in initiating change, as they are the primary source of innovation and new ideas.

Following the explorers are the villagers. This group is less inclined to seek out discoveries independently but is receptive to adopting and expanding upon the innovations introduced by the explorers. Villagers play a vital role in refining and adapting these new ideas, making them more accessible and practical for broader use within the organisation. Their efforts ensure that the innovations introduced by explorers are not isolated but integrated into the organisation’s existing frameworks and practices.

Finally, the town planners are responsible for industrialising and standardising the innovations the villagers have proven and accepted. They focus on scalability and efficiency, ensuring that new practices can be implemented widely across the organisation with minimal friction. The town planners enable the organisation to achieve economies of scale, thereby maximising the benefits of the innovations introduced by the explorers and refined by the villagers.

Organisations can strategically manage change initiatives by recognising these three groups’ distinct roles and contributions. Initiatives should ideally commence with the explorers, who are most likely to embrace and champion new ideas. As these ideas become more established and validated, the responsibility can then transition to the villagers, who will adapt and expand upon them for broader application. Ultimately, the town planners will take over, focusing on standardisation and broad implementation, ensuring that the innovations are firmly embedded within the organisation’s operations.

This model helps identify the right people to be involved at different stages of an initiative and also understand the natural progression of change within an organisation. By aligning initiatives with the inherent tendencies of each group, organisations can facilitate smoother transitions, reduce resistance, and enhance the overall efficiency and effectiveness of their change management processes.

The Japanese principle of Nemawashi, ‘Turning The Roots’, is the idea that change needs to be prepared for. In a business context, this can be first identifying and then leveraging a stakeholder map where people are engaged and given the ability to voice concerns. There is a human tendency when blind-sided or challenged to entrench existing positions; you can counteract this through thoughtful and careful preparation, ensuring different parties are aligned ahead of time.

Throughout my years as a consultant, there have been cases where our findings have been quite incendiary, and we needed to take great care to ensure our message was received with appropriate intent. Especially in more prominent organisations, with more scar tissue and greater inertia, the differentiator of success is how the message is delivered, more than the content itself.

7.4.2 The nature of bureaucracy

The perception of bureaucracy as predominantly negative is widespread, yet it is crucial to recognise its role as an emergent characteristic of successful organisations. Bureaucracy, at its core, aims to codify practices that have historically led to success, thereby facilitating scalability and efficiency within an organisation. The contention arises not from bureaucratic systems per se but from instances where the original intent of these systems becomes obscured, leaving behind rigid, inflexible rules.

In ‘The Delicate Art of Bureaucracy’, Schwartz (2020) delineates what constitutes effective bureaucracy, emphasising that it should be lean, enabling, and capable of learning. These qualities, however, are often elusive in many organisations, leading to the widespread frustration associated with bureaucratic processes. Schwartz proposes three innovative strategies for navigating and mitigating the challenges of bureaucracy, each embodying a unique approach to problem-solving within constrained systems:

The Monkey: This approach is characterised by an optimistic belief in possibilities. By operating within the established rules, ‘Monkeys’ leverage their relationships and open-mindedness to discover creative solutions amidst bureaucratic challenges. Their ability to navigate the system without directly challenging its foundations allows them to achieve their objectives through ingenuity and tact.
The Razor: ‘Razors’ adopt a more analytical stance, focusing on eliminating unnecessary costs and inefficiencies within bureaucratic processes. They quantify the financial impact of delays and other bureaucratic impediments, using data-driven arguments to advocate for change. This approach often garners support from higher levels of management, as it presents a clear business case for streamlining processes to enhance value creation.
The Sumo Wrestler: This strategy involves confronting bureaucratic obstacles by identifying and leveraging their points of vulnerability. ‘Sumo Wrestlers’ demonstrate the ineffectiveness or counterproductivity of specific bureaucratic measures, particularly how they may exacerbate risks or inefficiencies, such as extended lead times that increase the vulnerability window for security threats.

These approaches underscore the importance of empathy and understanding in overcoming bureaucratic resistance. One can identify mutually beneficial solutions that address individual and organisational needs by comprehending the motivations, objectives, and incentives of those entrenched in the bureaucratic system. This perspective facilitates the navigation of bureaucratic landscapes and fosters a more collaborative and constructive approach to reforming and improving these systems.

7.4.3 Use data to attack the problem

In organisational environments, pressures often exacerbate divisions, leading to an ‘us versus them’ mentality that can hinder effective collaboration and problem-solving. A data-driven approach can be instrumental in counteracting this tendency and shifting the focus from personal conflicts to the issues at hand. Leveraging data allows for an objective assessment of situations, facilitating a more constructive dialogue across different factions within an organisation.

When implementing new policies or controls, such as those related to security, it is crucial to understand and quantify their implications comprehensively. For instance, introducing a new security control might enhance protection but could also introduce additional workload or complexity for the affected teams. One can present a nuanced perspective that acknowledges the trade-offs by measuring the induced friction, such as the extra effort required to comply with the new control. This approach enables transparent communication and fosters a culture of integrity, as it demonstrates a commitment to considering the impacts on all parties involved.

The ‘in tension’ metrics concept is pivotal in this context. It involves developing metrics that capture the dynamic interplay between competing priorities, such as the need to maintain high throughput while ensuring stability and minimising risk. This balanced approach prevents the overemphasis on a single aspect, which could lead to unintended consequences elsewhere in the organisation. For example, excessively focusing on maximising throughput without regard to stability can increase the risk of errors and system failures, negating the benefits of the increased output.

By adopting ‘in tension’ metrics, organisations can navigate the delicate balance between various objectives, ensuring that efforts to optimise one area do not detrimentally affect others. This holistic perspective promotes adopting sustainable practices that yield net positive outcomes for the organisation. It encourages a collaborative approach to problem-solving, where diverse teams work together to achieve a common goal rather than working at cross-purposes due to narrowly defined or conflicting objectives.

7.4.4 Summary
Resistance is a sign of change; too much and too little resistance are problematic.
Using models like Wardley’s (2023) explorers, villages, and town planners allows you to engage the right people at the right time.
The principle of Nemawashi reminds us to mindfully set the groundwork for change before prematurely attempting to enact it.
Bureaucracy is an inevitable aspect of organisational functioning. Therefore, it is essential to understand how to navigate it. Bureaucracy is an inevitable aspect of organisational functioning. Therefore, it is essential to understand how to navigate it.
By leveraging data, you can break away from the us versus them divides that pervade organisations. The correct data transcends boundaries, enabling open and productive conversations.

Security is an infinite game; success is continually being able to keep playing. Managing resistance is critical to ensuring that you can.

7.5 Pattern 4: Security Is Achieved through Continuous Small Steps

In organisational security, the ideal scenario often involves ample time to plan and implement security measures meticulously. However, time is scarce, relying solely on hope for security initiatives; as Murphy et al. (2016) cautioned, ‘Hope is not a strategy’. In an organisation’s complex and dynamic environment, the predictability of outcomes from specific actions is limited, underscoring the need for a more agile and experimental approach to change and security enhancements.

Navigating change through a series of small experiments offers a pragmatic alternative to the traditional, overly optimistic planning methods. This approach entails initiating minor, manageable experiments to test hypotheses related to security improvements. The outcomes of these experiments then inform the following steps: whether to escalate the initiative based on successful results, adjust the strategy if the results are promising but not definitive, or abandon the approach if it proves ineffective. This iterative process facilitates a more responsive and adaptable security strategy, allowing organisations to refine their approaches based on empirical evidence and real-world feedback.

Security initiatives have traditionally been approached with a waterfall methodology, as Royce (2021) outlined, characterised by sequential, non-iterative stages. This method assumes that requirements can be fully defined upfront and that changes will be minimal, an assumption that is frequently at odds with the volatile nature of security threats and organisational dynamics. While structured and straightforward, the waterfall approach often leads to rigidity and challenges adapting to new information or changing circumstances.

In contrast, treating security initiatives as a series of small iterative experiments aligns more closely with agile methodologies predicated on flexibility, continuous improvement, and adaptation. Organisations can develop more resilient and effective security postures by embracing a mindset encouraging experimentation, learning from failures, and rapidly iterating on successes. This shift from large, inflexible projects to a more agile, experimental approach increases the likelihood of identifying effective security solutions. It fosters a culture of continuous learning and adaptability within the organisation.

7.5.1 The flow of work

In the landscape of modern organisations, the concept of value streams plays a pivotal role, serving as the channels through which knowledge workflows, from inception to delivery, encapsulating the journey from ‘please’ to ‘thank you’ or from idea to revenue. As delineated by the Business Architecture Guild (2023), every organisation comprises one or more such value streams, within which security work either contributes to or directly influences the outcomes.

As Kersten (2018) advocates in ‘Project to Product’, the transition from a project-centric approach introduces the Flow Framework, which offers a nuanced understanding of the dynamics within value streams. A critical element of this framework is the Flow Distribution, a method that categorises work into four distinct classes: feature, defect, debt, and risk. This categorisation helps identify the origin of each type of work – features from product owners, defects from customers, debt from the team, and risk from security and other risk-focused entities – and also facilitates an analysis of how teams prioritise and execute security-related tasks over time.

However, a challenge emerges in cybersecurity within value stream-aligned teams, as highlighted by Skelton and Pais (Skelton & Pais, 2019). The inherent focus on delivering immediate value often results in the marginalisation or deferral of security work, a phenomenon that can be attributed to hyperbolic discounting, a concept described by Grüne-Yanoff (2015); this cognitive bias leads individuals and teams to prioritise short-term gains over long-term benefits, thus relegating crucial security tasks.

Organisations must implement mechanisms that safeguard against the natural inclination to deprioritise security work to mitigate this tendency and ensure a balanced approach to addressing security concerns. One effective strategy involves setting minimum allocations for addressing technical debt and managing risk, ensuring that these critical areas are not overshadowed by the immediate allure of new features or the urgency of fixing defects. This approach of ‘weeding and feeding’ is essential to maintain the health and security of the organisational ecosystem.

Moreover, postponing tasks to ostensibly less busy future dates often results in a perpetual cycle of deferral, as future calendars appear deceptively open only because upcoming tasks have not yet been scheduled. This scenario underscores the necessity of systems that guarantee the availability of resources for ongoing, incremental progress in recognition of the principle articulated by Parkinson (1955) that ‘work expands to fill the time allotted for its completion’. Such systems ensure that security and maintenance efforts are continuously advanced, preventing the need for overwhelming corrective measures down the line.

7.5.2 Delivery constraints are security constraints

The planning and execution of security initiatives often revolve around the capacity of the security team, which overlooks a critical aspect: the bulk of implementation responsibilities fall on teams outside of security. This discrepancy necessitates a shift in planning focus from the security team’s capacity to orchestrate initiatives to the capacity of other teams to implement them effectively.

The Tame Flow approach, as presented by Tendon (2022), offers a structured methodology for applying the principles of the Theory of Constraints to the realm of knowledge work, particularly within complex environments characterised by multiple projects, events, stakeholders, and teams (PEST environments). In such settings, understanding and addressing constraints are paramount for achieving security objectives (Goldratt & Cox, 2016).

The Theory of Constraints outlines five focusing steps that serve as a roadmap for continuous improvement in security execution:

Identify the Constraint: The first step involves pinpointing the bottleneck or constraint that limits the system’s throughput. In the security context, this could range from a lack of awareness and training among non-security teams to technical or procedural bottlenecks that hinder the implementation of security measures.
Optimise the Constraint: Once identified, the next step is to optimise the functioning of the constraint to improve its throughput. This could involve enhancing skills, streamlining processes, or introducing tools that alleviate the bottleneck without overwhelming the rest of the system.
Subordinate to the Constraint: This step requires aligning the entire system’s workflow with the constraint’s capacity, ensuring that it is not burdened with more work than it can handle. It is about pacing the work according to the constraint’s rhythm to avoid exacerbating bottlenecks.
Elevate the Constraint: If the constraint continues to limit throughput despite optimisation efforts, this step involves making structural changes to elevate the system’s overall capacity. This could mean adding resources, making substantial process changes, or investing in significant technological upgrades.
Iterate: The final step is to recognise that once a constraint is addressed, another will eventually emerge as the system’s throughput increases. The process then iterates, starting from the first step, to identify and address the new constraint.

The initial three steps for security teams are particularly crucial as they can be undertaken internally to improve the flow of security work without requiring direct action from external teams. This involves effectively managing the security work backlog, contingent upon having the correct data to identify constraints. Data might be sourced from agile planning tools like Jira, facilitating informed decisions about what should be included in the backlog and its prioritisation.

Organisations can significantly improve the throughput of security initiatives by adeptly shaping the security work backlog. This approach enhances the efficiency of security teams and ensures that the broader organisation is better positioned to implement necessary security measures, thereby strengthening the overall security posture.

7.5.3 Aligning empowerment with incentives

The challenge of achieving security goals within an organisation is often compounded by the structure of the organisational operating model, particularly in terms of where operational and risk accountability and empowerment are allocated. Commonly, the responsibility for managing risk is placed on the shoulders of security teams, yet the authority and capability to resolve many security issues reside with delivery teams. This misalignment can hinder the prompt and effective resolution of security vulnerabilities.

Winters et al. (2020), in their work Software Engineering at Google, explore an alternative approach for managing certain classes of vulnerabilities, which could serve as a model for other organisations. At Google, the onus of service health is on the delivery teams, fostering a culture where those directly involved in service development are also accountable for its security. This model is supported by a shared empowerment structure between delivery and security teams, allowing both to take autonomous actions when necessary. Such an approach is convenient in the context of managing dependency vulnerabilities.

The log4shell vulnerability is a pertinent example, illustrating the efficiency of Google’s model (Wortley et al., 2021). With minimal coordination required, Google’s ability to swiftly deploy a patched version of the affected library starkly contrasts with the protracted processes observed in many traditional enterprises. Updating a vulnerable dependency often entails extensive coordination across numerous teams, leading to significant delays and operational strain.

Transitioning to this more autonomous and empowered model necessitates considerable technical enablement, including implementing robust automated testing and quality assurance processes. These systems are crucial for maintaining service health, especially when changes are introduced externally or by other teams within the organisation.

Organisations’ immediate objective should be to optimise the existing network of value streams, enhancing the efficiency and responsiveness of the current operating model. The ultimate aim, however, should be to evolve towards an operating model that reduces the reliance on extensive human-to-human coordination for executing security measures. This not only streamlines the process of addressing security issues but also aligns with broader organisational goals of agility, efficiency, and resilience in the face of evolving security threats.

7.5.4 Summary
As you are working in a complex space, security is best achieved through many small experiments and pieces of work.
Organisations are made up of value streams; if you can understand how work gets done, you can maximise the amount that is done.
The Flow Distribution allows you to identify the flow of security work amongst all other kinds.
By applying the theory of constraints, you can shape the work to fit the value streams you have today.
Over the longer term, the security limits come from your operating model, so you must design one that enables security.

In contrast to the large-scale programmes businesses use today for security, success is found in taking many small steps. One of the critical functions of cybersecurity is making it possible for teams to take those steps continually.

7.6 Pattern 5: Security Is Enablement, Not Gatekeeping

The evolution of security practices within modern organisations underscores a significant shift from a traditionally restrictive approach to one that prioritises enablement and informed decision-making at every step. This transition is driven by the rapid pace of technological change and the increasing frequency of deployments in contemporary software development environments.

The term ‘The Church of No’, often used to describe enterprise security departments, reflects the historical posture of security as a gatekeeper positioned squarely on the path to production. When system changes were infrequent, the impact of this gatekeeping role on business operations was minimal. However, in the current landscape, where a significant portion of teams deploy updates multiple times daily or at least weekly, the traditional security model becomes untenable. The demand for agility and speed in development cycles necessitates reevaluating security’s role in production.

The transformative power of code in accelerating development processes has led to the realisation that keeping pace with engineering teams requires a deep integration of security principles into the engineering discipline. This evolution began with testing automation, expanded into operations and site reliability engineering, and has now culminated in the emergence of DevSecOps and policy as code. While not every security professional needs to be proficient in coding, understanding and engaging with code has become a crucial competency for security departments.

This paradigm shift from a prevention-centric to an enablement-centric approach in security marks a profound change in how organisations view and implement security measures. Rather than acting as a barrier to production, modern security practices aim to integrate seamlessly with development workflows, providing the tools, policies, and frameworks that enable developers to make secure decisions proactively. This approach facilitates a more dynamic and agile development process and fosters a culture of security awareness and responsibility across all levels of the organisation. By empowering teams to incorporate security considerations into their work from the outset, organisations can achieve a more resilient and secure operational posture without sacrificing the speed and innovation that modern markets demand.

7.6.1 One size does not fit all guardrails

The insights derived from Kurtz and Snowden’s (2003) Cynefin framework offer a nuanced understanding of how controls can be categorised within organisations, particularly distinguishing between complex and complicated domains (Figure 7.1). Complicated controls, which are well-understood and can be addressed with expert knowledge, lend themselves to automation, allowing organisations to establish automated guardrails that pre-empt known issues. On the other hand, complex controls, due to their inherent uncertainty and the interdependent variables involved, necessitate more adaptive approaches, such as threat modelling, to manage the associated risks effectively.

Figure 7.1 Process Plant Operations Certificate II Syllabus Related to IT/OT Brownell (2022).

The likelihood of compliance with these controls is significantly enhanced when individuals grasp the underlying reasons for their implementation. Tyler and Balder (2003) emphasise the importance of understanding the rationale behind rules to foster compliance. However, this understanding is often hindered by the curse of knowledge bias, as described by Camerer et al. (1989), where experts may inadvertently assume that everyone shares their specialised knowledge. This assumption can lead to a disconnect between security professionals and engineers, where what seems evident to the former may not be apparent to the latter. Building momentum becomes a Sisyphean task without ensuring everyone understands the why (Fry, 2018). Ensuring a common understanding of the ‘why’ behind security measures is crucial for building momentum and achieving organisational buy-in.

As detailed in Team Topologies by Skelton and Pais (2019), the development and implementation of organisational guardrails typically undergo a natural progression. The authors outline three modes of team interaction: collaboration, facilitation, and as-a-service. Security controls, often by happenstance rather than deliberate design, may fall along this spectrum. The collaborative approach, characterised by security’s direct involvement in the production process, is increasingly seen as costly and restrictive. Conversely, the as-a-service model, where policies are imposed rather than co-developed, may lack the engagement necessary for effective implementation.

The transition from collaboration to facilitation and ultimately to an as-a-service model should be managed carefully, especially when new controls are not immediately intuitive to delivery teams. This transition involves creating supporting documentation and reference implementations that facilitate understanding and adoption. It is essential to avoid prolonged stagnation in collaboration or facilitation phases, as this can constrain the scalability and effectiveness of security measures across the organisation.

Moreover, the dynamics of inter-team communication within many organisations tend to develop organically rather than being intentionally structured. This lack of deliberate design, particularly in environments dominated by bureaucratic or pathological cultures instead of generative ones (Westrum, 2004), can lead to entrenched communication patterns that hinder collaborative efforts towards shared objectives. The prevalent us versus them mentality between security teams and other organisational units further exacerbates these challenges, undermining the collective pursuit of enhanced security outcomes. Addressing these cultural and communicative barriers is essential for fostering a more integrated, efficient, and practical approach to organisational security.

7.6.2 Building shared understanding with threat modelling

A deep understanding of the system to be protected is imperative for effectively managing controls in the complex security domain. This goes beyond checkbox approaches and complete automation, necessitating a more nuanced and participatory method like threat modelling. Shostack (2014) outlines threat modelling as a collaborative process to secure a system by identifying potential threats and corresponding countermeasures. This approach addresses security challenges and integrates security considerations into the development process, making security a tangible aspect of system design and implementation.

A significant hurdle in threat modelling is altering the perspectives of various stakeholders regarding the system. Developers often focus on creating value, and security personnel, charged with protecting value, tend to have divergent viewpoints. Bridging this gap is crucial for a cohesive and effective security strategy. The concept of enabling constraints can be instrumental in fostering creativity and innovative problem-solving within defined boundaries. The Elevation of Privilege card game, developed by Shostack, exemplifies this approach by gamifying threat modelling. This innovative tool provides developers with structured prompts that stimulate their ability to identify and mitigate potential security threats, thereby transforming the process from a daunting task into an engaging and enjoyable activity.

As teams become more proficient in threat modelling, it evolves into a self-service function, empowering teams to proactively address security concerns while allowing security professionals to concentrate on strategic objectives. This transition embodies the essence of security as an enabling function, facilitating a proactive and integrated approach to security within the organisation. Simply put, gamification transforms security from a stressor into something playful, potentially something that ‘Sparks Joy’ (Kondo, 2015).

The contrast in how security is approached in technical versus non-technical domains further highlights the need for a more hands-on, practical approach to security education and enablement. Drawing parallels to the role of a chief fire warden, where practical drills and the distribution of authority are critical components of preparedness, a similar approach could enhance security readiness. Regular, hands-on engagement with security practices, akin to fire drills, could ensure that organisations are better equipped to respond to security incidents. This analogy underscores the importance of treating security with the seriousness it deserves, integrating practical, real-world exercises into the broader security strategy to foster a culture of readiness and resilience.

7.6.3 Summary
Due to the required pace of change in modern businesses, security needs to focus on enabling teams rather than being the ‘Church of No’.
An engineering capability within security is necessary to scale enablement across an organisation.
Through Cynefin, you can classify controls into complex and complicated, each requiring different approaches.
Complicated controls are best turned into guardrails that prevent common security issues from ever occurring.
Complex controls require a context-specific approach, such as threat modelling.
By maturing capabilities, such as threat modelling, security can chase higher-order objectives consistently.

Enablement is how security can scale sub-linearly, covering more ground without needing more people. Through engineering and decentralising authority, you can push the organisation forward rather than firefight in perpetuity.

7.7 Conclusion

Cybersecurity is pivotal in an era of rapid digital transformation and the critical need for business agility. Esteemed technology behemoths like Google and Amazon exemplify the principle that security forms the cornerstone upon which their rapid innovation and operational speed are built. Despite this, many enterprises persist in the archaic practice of retrofitting security measures onto existing frameworks rather than integrating these measures as fundamental components from the outset.

This chapter has unveiled five quintessential patterns instrumental in reshaping an organisation’s security culture, transcending the realms of security and software delivery to offer universal relevance across sectors. These patterns advocate for a reimagined approach to security – transforming it from a mere compliance requirement into a palpable, intrinsic part of the organisational ethos, from a perceived impediment to productivity into a facilitator of seamless and secure operations, and from a rigid gatekeeping entity into a dynamic enabler of innovation and growth.

Exploration of these patterns revealed a compelling narrative: organisations must pivot from passive, compliance-driven security postures to proactive, action-oriented strategies that make security tangible and integral to daily operations. The juxtaposition of security and productivity as mutually exclusive goals was challenged, underscoring the potential for harmonising security with operational efficiency through a product and customer-centric security perspective.

Resistance, often the knee-jerk response to change, was identified as a critical element to be mindfully managed. Leveraging the principles of Nemawashi, navigating bureaucratic landscapes with agility, and employing data-driven strategies emerged as critical tactics for mitigating resistance and effecting sustainable, long-term security enhancements.

The discourse further illuminated the inadequacy of grand, sweeping security overhauls that dominate traditional roadmaps, advocating for a nuanced understanding of the organisational workflow. This understanding enables the tailoring of security initiatives to fit the unique contours of the operating model, ensuring maximum value and efficiency.

Finally, the anachronistic role of security as the proverbial gatekeeper was contested, highlighting the evolving digital landscape’s demand for a shift towards proactively enabling teams to uphold security protocols. The chapter critiqued the practice of imposing security controls either too collaboratively or too prescriptively through policy-as-code, advocating for a paradigm where security functions as a catalyst for right action rather than a deterrent against wrong.

In synthesising these insights, the chapter culminates in a compelling vision for organisational security – a future where security is not an afterthought but a keystone of business agility and innovation. By embracing the outlined patterns, organisations can steer through the complex cybersecurity terrain, fostering an ecosystem where security is seamlessly interwoven with the fabric of organisational processes, thereby empowering all members to contribute to a more secure, resilient, and dynamically agile enterprise.
