Chapter 8 Human skills and sociocultural impacts of K-12 cybersecurity education

Leslie F. Sikos, Marnie McKee, Ahmed Ibrahim, and Nicola F. Johnson

DOI: 10.1201/9781032664859-8

8.1 Introduction

Ubiquitous computing and the ever-increasing prevalence of computing device use raise cyber safety and security concerns that are challenging to address without bloating existing school curricula or extracurricular training. Considering that even digitally literate students experience high exposure to cyberthreats, teaching cybersecurity from a young age is crucial. While many aspects of cybersecurity require a technical background, human factors such as attitude, contextual awareness, critical thinking, motivation, and trust have also played a substantial role in shaping cybersecure behaviour, which can be established through relevant teaching approaches. There are many different approaches to pass on cybersecurity knowledge, skills, and awareness to K-12 students, to increase safe and secure device usage, responsible social interactions and behaviour online, and to promote cybersecurity career opportunities, including integrating cybersecurity content into existing subjects, in-school studies as collaborative projects (both year-long and as intensives), gamification strategies, out-of-school cyber-focused outreach camps, and the development of educational resource packs. However, international approaches to date have almost all focused on secondary school students. However, little is available for primary school students, whose digital device use is increasing; this is true even at younger ages. Educational initiatives for children must include the professional development of teachers, whose competence in teaching digital technologies is often highly dependent on their confidence and experience. In addition, in many countries’ primary schools, computing may not have specialised teachers in the first few years if taught at all from early on.

There are a variety of approaches and opportunities for teachers to develop professional knowledge and confidence. However, a sporadic one-off workshop is unlikely to bring behavioural change in the teachers’ own safe and secure digital device usage, let alone become capable of developing ideal behavioural patterns in students. Instead, a range of effective professional learning strategies are needed that can be employed by school leaders and classroom teachers to support student learning in cybersecurity successfully (Johnson et al., 2022). Teachers must have access to engaging teaching resources and professional learning because users are the weakest link in cyberspace. Hence, the early establishment of appropriate behavioural patterns is critical. In an earlier work (Johnson et al., 2022, p. 17), the authors stated:

The digital environment is constantly changing as new technology is released for consumers and in the world of work. For this reason, the skills and knowledge required by students now will be superseded within a short time. Therefore, cybersecurity curriculum skills and knowledge are required to provide a set of skills to equip students with key skills to manage and protect their data, understand the risks associated with cybersecurity threats and be prepared for ongoing learning. In addition, the senior secondary curriculum has a key role in developing skills and knowledge for students studying to work in ICT industries, including emerging careers in cybersecurity.

The rampant and sophisticated nature of cybercrime underscores the increasing concern over insufficient cybersecurity coverage in schools. Daily, there are reports of individuals being scammed, hacked, or having their identities stolen. Education is a crucial strategy to mitigate or prevent cyber incidents by equipping young people with the knowledge and skills to combat common cyberthreats. Schools have initiated various educational programmes to address issues such as cyberbullying. However, it is imperative to note that cyberbullying is nothing but the digital manifestation of traditional bullying (Olweus, 2012). This digital form of bullying can lead to severe consequences for children, including social withdrawal, low self-esteem, anxiety, self-harming behaviours, and even suicidal thoughts, as noted by Lonergan et al. in 2021.

Furthermore, there is evidence of a correlation between online harassment and behavioural issues within the school environment, as indicated by research from Ybarra et al. in 2007. Addressing the complex nature of cyberbullying effectively requires a comprehensive approach that considers psychological, sociological, institutional, and legal factors. De Luca et al. (2022) emphasise the importance of a multi-perspective approach in understanding the cultural-psycho-socio dynamics of cyberbullying and devising effective interventions.

Not having sufficient cybersecurity coverage in schools is an increasingly growing concern. Cybercrime is rife and increasing in sophistication. People learn about individuals who have been scammed, hacked, or had their identities stolen daily. Education is often viewed as a means to – at least partially – prevent or overcome cyber incidents by helping young people become aware of and able to fight common cyberthreats. To this end, various educational initiatives are implemented by schools. For example, a range of initiatives have been introduced to address cyberbullying, yet cyberbullying is merely bullying in another form (Olweus, 2012), with a range of potential adverse effects on children, such as social withdrawal, low self-esteem, anxiety, self-injurious behaviour, and suicidal thoughts (Lonergan et al., 2021). In addition, studies show linkages between online harassment and school behaviour problems (Ybarra et al., 2007).

Students are not spending time learning to recognise the mindset behind malintent and other cognitive general capabilities crucial for cybersecurity awareness. Instead, bad habits are being established within educational settings as students’ digital device use grows. Nevertheless, positive changes in attitude will result in whole school communities identifying cybersecurity’s immense benefits: peace of mind, increased user confidence, reputation protection, reduced stress, and ensuring individuals, family, and friends do not lose their hard-earned money to threat actors. Humanity is embedded within cybersecurity, and it needs to be recognised to lead a nation onto a path for overcoming industry workforce shortages and building a cybersecure nation.

Two fundamental aspects of cybersecurity education must be promoted. One surrounds technical competencies; the other covers behavioural competencies and the psychology behind those behaviours. The digital technologies learning area of the Australian curriculum aims to develop knowledge of how to use and apply technologies (ACARA, 2023b). The internationally recognised standard IEEE 3527.1–2020 for digital intelligence promotes teaching students about digital citizenship, literacy, and readiness (Digital Intelligence, 2021). Such initiatives support statements, for example: ‘One of the shortcomings in K-12 education is that students are taught to use various technologies, but they are not introduced to the threats they face while using them’ (Javidi & Sheybani, 2018).

One of the main foci for those responsible for students’ security and safety in cyberspace is to bring about behavioural change at the individual level, i.e., to apply best practices (cyber hygiene) and use protective technologies effectively. Behavioural competencies need to be built up alongside the technical competencies. Cybersecurity awareness is the funnel for that behavioural change. Hence, a second critical area of focus is on identifying malicious intent. Increasing vigilance and suspicion of criminal deception and strengthening risk assessment are also part of the many behaviours and associated awareness needed to bring about change to mitigate the consequences of inaction.

According to a survey conducted in 2022 among high school students, teachers, and parents in nine European countries (Romania, Slovenia, Greece, Cyprus, Italy, Spain, Germany, the Netherlands, and Poland), the following topics are the most important to be introduced into secondary school curricula (Blažič & Blažič, 2022):

Being safe in online social platforms
Recognising fake accounts
Ensuring privacy in online activities
Creating strong passwords
Securely using email applications

As this self-reported data, what is being emphasised in K-12 curricula to bring about change that includes both behavioural and technical competencies? What is evident is that children and young people (not to mention adults) are using digital devices increasingly and from earlier ages compared to previous generations. However, the use of technology does not necessarily, and often not at all, correlate directly to an increase in knowledge and skills. This is mainly because the user interface of modern computing devices is far more user-friendly and accessible than those in previous decades. This often causes overconfidence in device use and, importantly, casual approaches to data protection, privacy, and safe device use, resulting in a misbelief about the actual capabilities of children in these areas.

Acknowledging that no two children have the same digital knowledge background is essential in developing a scheme for cybersecurity education. Children’s exposure to digital services at home and with friends is difficult to measure, as is the interest, attention, and knowledge of parents and occasional caregivers, with whom access to devices and digital services is often extremely different from that at home. In such environments, establishing a baseline of measurement for assumed knowledge is highlighted as a particularly challenging task to understand children’s collective and individual learning needs.

A strong argument is that students and teachers (as well as parents and extended school communities) need to obtain in-depth knowledge of technology and critical literacy (the questioning and examination of ideas). This is primarily because digital technologies are often used for entertainment and education (particularly since the COVID lockdowns). Following this logic, behavioural change is the final objective because behaviour is the source of security and unsecured devices, services, and infrastructures.

Being able to use a digital service does not constitute knowing that service. Instead, the security space is at the point where an individual has evaluated information and is responding to it through critical action. There is a requirement to identify the baseline for educational needs based on teachers’, parents’ and students’ use of digital devices and services, according to those users’ actions, or lack thereof. By extension, we must also identify that schools at the holistic level require this same skill and knowledge set. Schools are particularly vulnerable to attacks and must first be aware and vigilant to maintain secure learning environments. For this chapter, cybersecurity refers to the safe and secure use of digital technology and services (as a discipline to be taught) rather than focusing on protecting computer systems and networks by professionals, devices, and tools. Both privacy and ethics define the core of cybersecurity.

8.2 International Context Addressing Human Factors in Cybersecurity Education

Several key international organisations (or at least across multiple continents) have made significant headway in addressing cybersecurity education and its inherent human factors. The bigger picture addresses where the international knowledge of children’s cybersecurity education is situated within the broader realm for digital citizens or everyday users of digital devices and services.

Cybersecurity education is at an infancy stage, requiring a structuring of knowledge. An internationally focused organisation, Digital Intelligence (DQ) Institute, works extensively across industry, not for profits and education, developing a framework fundamental to empowering citizens within the digital age through digital intelligence.

8.2.1 Digital intelligence

The DQ Institute provides a common framework (or ‘global standards’) for digital intelligence competencies that encompass the eight areas of digital life: digital literacy, digital use, safety, security, emotional intelligence, communication, rights, and identity (DQ Institute, 2023b). Based on the IEEE 3527.1 standard, the digital intelligence (DQ) Global Standards incorporate 24 digital competencies developed at three levels of citizenship, creativity, and competitiveness (Digital Intelligence, 2021). The specified knowledge, skills and attitudes/values outlined for each digital competency overlap behavioural and technical competencies, with knowledge and skills emphasising (but not exclusively) technical competencies and attitudes/values emphasising behavioural competencies. The DQ Framework encompasses OECD’s Better Life Initiative (11 indicators of well-being) and the 17 UN Sustainable Development Goals and aims to ‘guide digital practices towards achieving individual and societal wellbeing across all aspects of one’s life’ (DQ Institute, 2023b).

8.2.2 Children’s needs missing from cybersecurity education frameworks

The DQ Institute positions itself as a comprehensive, collaborative platform, uniting various stakeholders, including funders, programme providers, and educators from various sectors, all dedicated to the shared objective of bridging the digital skills divide. This commitment is embodied in their DQ Global Standards, which are grounded in extensive international research and aim to establish a universal framework for digital competencies (DQ Institute, 2023a). Despite the Institute’s holistic approach, it is noteworthy that a significant portion of these standards – about one-third – do not cater to the needs of children. This oversight is particularly striking given the recent introduction of the fourth iteration of the DQ Global Standards, titled ‘Digital Connectivity’, which, at first glance, appears to align more closely with the learning requirements of K-12 students. However, this alignment necessitates further scrutiny (DQ Institute, 2023b).

A critical examination of the Digital Competitiveness component of the DQ Global Standards reveals a language and framework that resonates more with professional environments than young learners’ educational contexts. This discrepancy underscores a broader issue: the emphasis on adult digital competencies in the workforce while overlooking the specific digital skill sets necessary for children’s development and safety online. Such oversight highlights a gap in the research and structured knowledge concerning digital education for the younger demographic and questions the inclusivity of global frameworks to close the digital skills gap.

The authors of this critique advocate for a more nuanced approach to digital education standards, one that recognises and addresses the unique requirements of children’s digital literacy and safety. By shifting the focus from a predominantly adult-centric model to one that encompasses the educational needs of children, the goal is to contribute to a more comprehensive strategy for narrowing the digital skills gap on a global scale. This shift necessitates additional research and the reorganisation of existing knowledge to ensure that global standards are equally applicable and beneficial to the cyber educational needs of the younger population.

8.2.3 Terminology not yet relevant to students’ needs

Internationally, ‘vigilance’ describes a specific core cybersecurity competency, featured in nearly half (43%) of research papers on cybersecurity education to date (Ibrahim, McKee, Sikos & Johnson, 2023) and referenced widely in industry. Variations of precise definitions exist across psychology and the computer science/cybersecurity fields. However, the authors have previously published an argument for vigilance to refer to ‘sustained conscientious attention’ from an interdisciplinary perspective (Ibrahim et al., 2023). Recent research in occupational health consistently points to vigilance as unsustainable in security technicians (for instance, Guidetti et al., 2023), who must stay alert to identify cyberattacks. In this context, the concern for any definition is that it is relevant to the needs of young learners. Children with their level of use of digital devices and services do not need to be alert in this way.

8.3 Competencies in Cybersecurity Education

STEM and non-STEM disciplines differ significantly in terms of ontology and epistemology (Charlesworth & Banaji, 2019). This determines how students perceive computing-related subjects and, in turn, can substantially affect which educational approaches may effectively teach psychological principles to cybersecurity students (Taylor et al., 2017). Incorporating psychology into cybersecurity education requires an understanding of student motivations and expectations, as well as perspective-shifting because students tend to be interested more in the how and the what than the why, which prevents a deeper understanding of cyberattacks (Taylor-Jackson et al., 2020). Taylor et al. (2018) argue that ‘to understand the potential risks of socio-technical systems, cybersecurity students need to understand and consider how people perceive, remember, feel, think, and solve problems’ (p. 99).

Two main categories of cybersecurity competencies need to be developed in K-12 students: behavioural and technical competencies. They are equally important. We begin by discussing the most critical behavioural and essential technical competencies.

8.4 Behavioural Competencies

Children learn cyber behavioural patterns from parents, siblings, teachers, and peers (Renaud & Prior, 2021). Understanding learners’ online behaviour requires a multidisciplinary approach incorporating brain research, biology, psychology, human cognition, developmental theory, sociology, motivation theory, and social behaviour (Zheng, 2012).

8.4.1 Definitions

For this chapter, the authors reference behaviour as broadly inclusive of all the above-mentioned areas outlined by Zheng. Cognitive, meta-cognitive, and socio-emotional competencies are called ‘behavioural competencies’. This tryptic division of competencies is used by DQ Institute, where they state that ‘Digital Intelligence (DQ) was developed to encompass a comprehensive set of technical, cognitive, meta-cognitive, and socio-emotional competencies, which are grounded in universal moral values and enable individuals to face the challenges of digital life and adapt to its demands’ (Digital Intelligence, 2021). The idea of ‘universal moral values’ being objective, delineates a strong line in the sand whereby the foundation of cybersecurity education (and especially for children) is as a ‘lived ethic’ aligned with shared community values, particularly privacy and freedom. This is as opposed to cybersecurity founded on, for instance, principles designed to protect a nation.

Behavioural cybersecurity competencies define what students’ online secure and insecure actions are; examine the impact on an individual’s well-being, health and safety; and delineate action (and non-action) that both protect internet users and promote safe and responsible use of technology (Türker & Çakmak, 2019).

8.4.2 Order of learning

Cybersecure behaviour is the result of cybersecurity awareness. Parsons et al. (2017) developed the Human Aspects of Information Security Questionnaire (HAISQ), measuring information security awareness in employees based on the KAB learning model (knowledge–attitude–behaviour). This learning model claims, ‘As an employee’s knowledge of safe information security behaviours in the workplace is increased, their attitude improves, resulting in improved information security behaviour’ (Parsons et al., 2017).

8.4.3 Teachers’ modelling behaviour

Regarding learning, student competencies and awareness of online threats and privacy need to start with teachers modelling the behaviour, but currently, they are not (Hipsky & Younes, 2015). This situation is despite teachers in countries such as the United States being aware of, for instance, federal privacy policies.

8.4.4 Learning environments

Just like learning cybersecurity terminology, learning cybersecurity behaviours and competencies requires the flexibility to be activated within nuanced, novel, and varied environments. Scenario-based activities necessitate learners’ critical thinking (a cognitive competency) across various environments and vigilance to pick up and act on various threats (the core behavioural competency) within different sets of circumstances. Scenario-based learning enables students to understand security through ‘doing’ security. This cross-relational capacity building activates learners’ situational intelligence, a core behavioural competency the authors argue is required for digital literacy (Johnson et al., 2023).

A student’s exposure to scenarios within their learning experiences propels individual learners towards self-directed cybersecure behaviour, with the transferability of these behavioural (or technical) skills from one domain to another. Students apply their improved knowledge of how their cybersecure thinking can be applied to many more new, unknown and unregulated digital environments and situations they find themselves in, and therefore act as their first line of defence.

8.4.5 Being aware of cyberthreats

While technical countermeasures, such as content filtering, can be applied to protect minors (Meeder, 2005), it is neither enforced to be used at schools or home nor can be considered 100% reliable, which is why being aware of cyberthreats is crucial. Cyber awareness is an established term with quality published reference points, furthered with arguments explaining how a term translates into the experience and behaviours of children or the cause and effect of cyberthreats on those experiences. Javidi and Sheybani (2018) reference the definition of cybersecurity awareness from the US National Institute of Standards and Technology (NIST) with a focus on teaching, describing it as ‘teaching students about security concerns and threats and teaching them to respond accordingly’ (p. 2). This focus may or may not encompass an industry definition; the example highlights how variations in the definition of key terms can be tricky at best when required to cross disciplines, as is the case between education and cybersecurity.

The development of safer online environments for children, and specifically, to identify and prevent online harassment and victimisation such as cyberbullying and cyberstalking, which occur regardless of socio-economic status or cultural background, requires not only technological tools but also policy and legislation reforms (Güllü et al., 2023; IRMA, 2020). Even contemporary online learning environments, such as those of massive open online courses, are prone to cyberbullying and cyber-mobbing (Grella et al., 2017).

8.4.6 Being able to apply best practices (for cyber hygiene)

As previously mentioned, cyber hygiene signifies the online practices required to stay secure, as referred to by a Canadian study (Witsenboer et al., 2022). Some of these practices are not trusting strangers online, not sharing sensitive information online (and not oversharing), prioritising secure activities over simplicity/convenience/time pressure, and being vigilant (paying attention to detail and knowing when to be suspicious, for example, being able to detect anomalies always to recognise spam). These can be taught to become second nature without necessarily knowing technical details. As addressed earlier, vigilance is overkill for the needs of children practising cybersecurity. Habit precedes vigilance, and the reoccurrence of experiences across different environments is the reinforcer towards best practice becoming second nature, for best practice becoming habitual.

Cyber wisdom, the ability to do the right thing at the right time when using computing devices and services, can be cultivated via formal education through four components: cyber wisdom literacy, cyber wisdom reasoning, cyber wisdom self-reflection, and cyber wisdom motivation. These can be built on character education literature and practice, such as understanding the nature of and applying virtues such as honesty, evaluating and prioritising these virtues, reflecting on moral dimensions, and acting online with the common good in mind (Polizzi & Harrison, 2022).

8.4.7 Being able to distinguish between ethical and unethical online behaviour

Ethics has been identified as a competency within organisational psychology (ethics competency), defined as ‘the ability to apply knowledge, skills and abilities to incorporate values and principles that distinguish right from wrong when making decisions and choosing behaviours’ (Cooper et al., 2017). As an example of its current application in schools, in the Australian curriculum, ethical understanding is a general capability taught across the different learning areas from K-12, introduced into the national curriculum in 2010. The papers collectively suggest examining how empathy as a learned state relates to ethics competency, a topic possibly covered in education and psychology, offering insights for cybersecurity education.

8.4.8 Not sharing personal data online

An essential behavioural competency that can be taught to young children is the ability not to share personal data online (or offline). First, understanding what comprises personal data is needed, and this information is unique to an individual. The ability to keep personal information private enables the prevention of more sophisticated applications of identity theft. Within this behavioural competency, we can consider appropriate personal information to share with particular people. Educating children about what is appropriate to share with a parent, compared to a teacher or a supermarket worker, may increase understanding about privacy protection. While 3- to 5-year-olds may not have their device, they will likely be temporal users of their parent’s or carer’s smartphone. The children must be aware of not only not sharing their personal information (such as name, age, where they live) but also realise that if they share their parent’s or carer’s phone number, that is sharing someone else’s personal information, and that this is a breach of their privacy.

In many classrooms, it is a common yet suboptimal practice to share passwords for convenience and ease of use (for instance, leaving children’s passwords in plain sight on the classroom wall – a cybersecurity nightmare). Two significant problems emerge from this practice. Firstly, the fact that classroom teachers engage in such insecure behaviour sets a negative example for their students. Secondly, the same credentials are connected to their accounts for an extended period (often years) without any systematic encouragement to change (unless a policy to do so is enforced via the school’s infrastructure). School protocols and policies often drive behavioural change in such circumstances, particularly at the school administrative or governance levels.

One of the issues with behavioural-based competencies is that they are not simple to measure. The recent revision of the Australian national curriculum outlined many specific and explicit updates within the learning areas section (Maths, etc.). The same level of detail does not apply to updating the general competencies section, which, at the broadest educational level from K to university, speaks to the development of the transferrable skills required for individuals and a cybersecure nation.

8.5 Technical Competencies

The following sections discuss the main technical competencies for cybersecurity education. From the educational perspective, human competencies are implicitly intertwined with technical competencies. Without comprehending these technical aspects, we cannot affect behavioural change nor achieve desired learning outcomes.

8.5.1 Having digital literacy

Various definitions of digital literacy have been refined over the years as the societal use of digital devices has increased. While literacy focuses on reading and writing, digital literacy includes accessing and using online information safely and appropriately and evaluating and creating content (Law et al., 2018).

Within previous versions of the national curriculum of Australia, the general capability was called Information and Communication Technology (ICT), which encompassed appropriate and effective use of digital technologies across all learning areas (ACARA, 2000), alongside investigating, communicating, and creating with ICT. This general capability is designed to be developed or applied within the context of learning areas (subjects). The latest Australian Curriculum version 9.0 retitled this general capability as Digital Literacy (ACARA, 2023a). Digital literacy includes investigating, communicating, and creating with digital tools but also incorporates the practice of digital safety and well-being, the management and protection of content, and the respect of intellectual property. Digital literacy is arguably a required technical competency of cybersecurity as the general capabilities are highlighted as equipping Australians to ‘live and work successfully in the twenty-first century’ (ACARA, 2023c). From a cybersecurity perspective, digital literacy is an essential technical competency to meet the needs of a cyber workforce and a cybersecure society, forming the basis for behavioural and other technical competencies to develop.

8.5.2 Being able to use private browsing

Web browsers allow excessive user tracking and profiling, actively used for social engineering. However, modern browsers provide a private browsing feature that blocks websites’ attempts to track users with cookies, web beacons, etc. While web browsers are amongst the first software tools users get familiar with, many users use browsers with default, rather than strict, settings, which enables user tracking.

8.5.3 Being able to set up a strong password or passphrase

Even in the era of multi-factor authentication, passwords and passphrases continue to play their role in information security. While even beginner users regularly use (basic) passwords, often automatically assigned by their organisation, very few are familiar with complex passphrases and the best practices around these (Bitwarden, 2023). Despite being particularly important for children, this is because the password principles they learn early in their lives will be the ones they keep using later (Renaud & Prior, 2021).

8.5.4 Being able to utilise antivirus software

Understanding the importance and efficient use of antivirus software is imperative in cybersecurity education. This is because, despite the common misbelief, antivirus software remains vital in maintaining the security posture of computing devices.

8.5.5 Understanding where data is stored with cloud accounts and data ownership

Users focusing on the convenience of cloud services are often unaware of the potential threats of unsecured data transfer to and from the cloud, nor the question of data ownership, which might affect data use, alignment with company policies, and data retention. Once a cloud account is created, users might never be able to delete the account. In addition, it happens all too often that user data is replicated in near real time to multiple servers that users have no access to (only the service provider does), and in case the user initiates a file deletion, the service provider will still preserve a copy of the file, without the consent, let alone permission, of the user.

8.5.6 Being able to back up data

The rise of ransomware attacks in recent years necessitates a global understanding of the importance of regular data backups. When such attacks succeed, one of the best options is to restore computing systems, virtual machines, operating systems, and user files from the latest backup. This is not feasible without a robust backup infrastructure and proper configuration, regularity, occasional verification, and functional redundancy. Although students often save data on corporate (in-house, data centre, or cloud) infrastructure, there are many situations when they need to be able to back up their data themselves, such as when using their own devices.

8.6 The Sociocultural Effect of Human Cybersecurity Competencies

Society does not value cybersecurity (Johnson et al., 2023), instead seeing it as a ‘problem for the IT department’. Nevertheless, parents and teachers are deeply concerned for the safety and security of young people in their care when on digital devices. Every day, the media includes horror stories of data breaches and cyberattacks whilst hearing and telling their own stories of deceptive text messages and phishing emails received daily. One of the critical components required in generating positive learning environments for students, teachers, and parents is the wider stance taken by society. As malintent across digital services occurs across the board for all of us, within the greater context of minimal national regulation for digital apps and other services, cybersecurity education for children cannot be considered in isolation. Again, we are all our first line of defence.

This chapter has addressed how cybersecurity knowledge indicates attitudes towards valuing cybersecurity. This erudition must be considered at the whole society level (and with the whole-of-school approach engaging and activating those secure learning environments) because a ‘whole system initiative’ (Johnson et al., 2023, p. 17) is ‘needed across all industries and all workers to keep our identities and workplaces secure’ (p. 17). Society needs a shift in attitude, preceded by cybersecurity awareness.

8.6.1 Addressing the workforce shortage

K-12 cybersecurity education is essential to teach primary and secondary school students knowledge and skills and prepare those interested in a cybersecurity career for post-secondary degree programmes offered in cybersecurity (Prickett et al., 2022).

The current shortage and future demand for skilled cybersecurity professionals is well-documented in Australia and internationally as both a trending and an emerging need (National Skills Commission, 2022). These trends are escalated by the ubiquitous nature of cybersecurity, itself being industry-agnostic, embedded within other industries, and has become an industry in its own right. Globally, we have companies that provide cybersecurity services across all sectors. Within all industry sectors, cybersecurity specialists operate in other industry-specific environments. Hence, career skills extend far beyond the more expected technical knowledge sets to also encompass psychology, organisational management, business development and strategy, administration, education and training, human resources, design, investigative services, communications, and marketing, to consider a few, as well as industry-particular knowledge paired with security knowledge. The skill sets required for cybersecurity to be integrated across sectors, including K-12 education (and then further integrated into everyone’s daily activities), are tremendously broad.

8.6.2 Diversity is not in technology use: it is in people

The interdisciplinary nature of cyber-based roles accentuates the industry’s need for diverse people with broad interests. These interests must be cultivated within educational settings to build a strong workforce, protect our economic sovereignty, and maintain the way of life people take for granted. By bringing people to the forefront of the image of cybersecurity, we align with the reality of cybersecurity not being simply technical. It is also behavioural. It is thinking, being, acting, and aligning with protective practices and ethical practices. The benefits of utilising adequate protections on everyday digital devices must be shown to bring about peace of mind, increase confidence, protect reputation, lessen worry, reduce stress, and ensure individuals, family, and friends do not lose their hard-earned money to threat actors.

8.6.3 Positive representation of cybersecurity

A societal standpoint of ‘diverse people’ being the public face of cybersecurity needs to be generated to move the current media portrayal of a ‘tech-head’ type of person who analyses data wearing a hoodie in a dark corner. Or is he the baddie? The media’s misrepresentations perpetuate misunderstandings about cybersecurity in society. From a simple web search, the imagery used to represent cybersecurity is predominantly set in black backgrounds with hidden faces. This generates an air of secrecy, unfriendliness, and something alienating, or at least socially undesirable. For the average digital user, cybersecurity is socially alienating and complex to understand, even unobtainable. More engaging media representation should positively showcase a more comprehensive array of personalities.

8.6.4 Need for sociocultural approach to impact

Another part of the ‘awareness’ solution lies in addressing adults’ or teachers’ comprehension of the value of cybersecurity in our everyday lives (Johnson et al., 2023). We can borrow from our established understanding of privileged countries’ occupational health and safety standards and protocols and align with existing societal-level learning curves. Hence, a media push generating a more positive portrayal that engages the humanity embedded within security is required to meet national goals worldwide. Highlighting diverse individuals with varied skills and backgrounds in different industry roles is crucial for societal change. A vital shift is needed in how the public and media portray the human-led cybersecurity skills essential to the workforce.

8.6.5 Shifting the mindset of a generation through a national conversation

Often shaped by media and governments, national conversations are crucial in enhancing online children’s safety and security (Martin & Rice, 2012). Drawing parallels with historical workplace safety campaigns and other national safety efforts is sensible, given the similar need to protect children’s interactions with digital platforms like websites, apps, text messaging, social media, and games. An example of such regulation in traditional media is the imposition of watershed broadcasting times on television, which limits content based on the viewer’s age to ensure it is appropriate. However, digital content does not currently face similar stringent restrictions or guidelines. Online content regulation is primarily overlooked by parents, teachers, schools, and governments. For instance, there are no specific restrictions on advertising apps aimed at children, and while websites may ask for age confirmation, there is often no real verification of this information (O’Neill, 2013).

It is worth mentioning that some parents do make use of parental controls available through advanced antivirus software or streaming subscriptions to manage their children’s screen time and content exposure. However, this is not widespread and does not address all potential risks. The lack of comprehensive oversight for digital content is partly attributed to the internet’s inherent nature as a free and largely uncensored space.

The dialogue surrounding online child safety is essential in cultivating a societal mindset that values cyber safety and cybersecurity for children and holds content providers to the same standards of accountability expected in more traditional forms of media like television and radio. By elevating the public importance of cyberspace security, there is potential for a positive shift in how digital spaces are perceived and regulated. Notably, the strategy should recognise that preventative and restrictive measures might not be as effective as empowering children to make informed decisions using digital devices.

The human factor in cybersecurity education has its roots in the national psyche. Continuing with the ‘roots’ metaphor is the cybersecure tree of life, where tree branches are our attitude to online privacy and online ethics, and children’s learning takes place at the leaves, able to grow and explore freely from a solid, secure foundational culture of positive cybersecurity practices and behaviours. This allows the tree – a nation, a world – to support and flourish, whilst children and those curious about cyberspace remain the first line of defence against the bad weather of malintent and unregulated soil conditions.

8.7 Conclusion

Cybersecurity is crucial to our existence in the digital world and integral to modern-day life. While the variety and sophistication of cyberthreats continue to rise, and children start using digital devices and services at a younger age than ever before, cybersecure practices and core cybersecurity countermeasures ought to be taught throughout K-12. Educational approaches in this discipline should consider both the technical aspects and the human competencies that can lead to positive behavioural changes in cyberspace. This is important not only to individuals but also to entire societies. Major educational approaches identify and develop skills and competencies via contemporary techniques, whether by injecting them into existing curricula (covering concepts in other subjects) or providing extracurricular activities, such as training courses and educational campaigns.

Ybarra, M. L., Diener-West, M., & Leaf, P. J. (2007). Examining the overlap in internet harassment and school bullying: Implications for school intervention. Journal of Adolescent Health, 41(6), S42– S50. https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jadohealth.2007.09.004
Zheng, R. Z. (2012). Evolving psychological and educational perspectives on cyber behaviour. IGI Global. https://doi.org/10.4018/978-1-4666-1858-9

Chapter 9 Incident response drills on cyber ranges Enhancing cyber-defence education

Alexander E. Grojek, Leslie F. Sikos, David J. Holmes, and Oliver Guidetti

DOI: 10.1201/9781032664859-9

9.1 Introduction

The emergence of a new wave of generative AI-powered and constantly growing plethora of cyberthreats necessitates efficient teaching and training of cybersecurity analysts at an increasing pace. The cybersecurity world faces an ever-growing number of complex and dynamic threats. As a result, it has become essential to train future cybersecurity professionals on comprehensive technology environments rather than relying on a limited set of tools, practices, methods, and approaches.

The traditional approach to cybersecurity education often struggles to provide students with a practical, hands-on experience due to difficulties in securing industry placements, and the unavailability of authentic datasets hinders realistic training scenarios. Developing case studies authentically replicating the complexity of a real-world incident is daunting. In response, cyber ranges emerge as a transformative solution. These controlled and interactive environments allow participants to engage in simulated cyber incidents, providing a hands-on learning experience that bridges the gap between theoretical knowledge and practical application.

Cyber ranges create a dynamic space for experimental learning by simulating real-world cyber incidents in a controlled environment. Participants can actively respond to scenarios such as malware outbreaks, data breaches, and network intrusions, allowing them to develop and refine their incident response skills. Notably, the controlled nature of these simulations enables educators and trainers to monitor participants’ performance and decision-making processes. This monitoring aspect facilitates continuous improvement, allowing for targeted feedback and personalised guidance to enhance the participants’ skills over time.

Proactive incident response requires a solid technical background in communication networks and computer security and hands-on skills that rely on experience that could only be obtained via work placements that often cannot be arranged or working on authentic case studies (Sikos, 2023a). Such case studies cannot rely solely on static datasets because these would not provide authentic experience during training; the pressure, and in the case of threat hunters, the thrill, typically associated with working on cyber-incidents promptly, is lost, and there is no live network traffic. In addition, security analysts need to be trained in teamwork as well, such as in the form of a green team (infrastructure operators), a white team (organisers), a red team (attackers), and a blue team (defenders).

These issues face many challenges, from obtaining resources to ensuring that production sites and live services are unaffected on the network. Moreover, educators might find it challenging to repeat successful ad hoc training events or classes (Vykopal et al., 2017). One way to overcome these challenges is to use a live but controlled network environment on a cyber range (named after police and military shooting ranges used for hands-on practice), such as the CyberBit Range (CyberBit, 2020; Sikos, 2023b). Some cyber ranges may be available as a Cyber Range as a Service (CRaaS) (Angelogianni et al., 2021).

9.2 Cyber-Defence Education and the Need for Practical Training

Cyber ranges are pivotal as sophisticated technical platforms that serve a dual purpose: enabling cutting-edge research and development initiatives while providing a dynamic training and exercise environment within the cyber domain (Karjalainen & Kokkonen, 2020). At their core, cyber ranges emulate essential networks and systems, establishing a versatile foundation to support these activities, consisting of a closed and tightly controlled environment equipped with the necessary systems, tools, and network infrastructure. A distinguishing feature of cyber ranges is their ability to replicate realistic internet simulations, encompassing the generation of background traffic and user emulation. This comprehensive simulation extends beyond the mere imitation of cyber activities; it strives to mirror the intricacies of the real-world cyber landscape. This emulation includes but is not limited to scenarios involving sophisticated attacks, mimicking cyber adversaries’ tactics, techniques, and procedures. By doing so, cyber ranges provide a nuanced and authentic setting for researchers, professionals, and trainees to engage with genuine cyberthreats.

The cyberthreat landscape constantly evolves, with cybercriminals becoming more sophisticated and innovative in their attacks. These threats include malware, phishing attacks, ransomware, and DDoS attacks. Threat actors range from individual hackers to well-funded organised crime groups and nation-states. With the rise of IoT devices, cloud computing, and interconnected systems, the attack surface has expanded, making it more challenging to defend against cyberthreats. The escalating complexity of network systems has intensified the occurrence of unexpected behaviours and dependencies (Karjalainen & Kokkonen, 2020). This complexity underscores the importance of understanding the events in the cyber domain, assessing the statuses of valuable assets, and comprehending the impacts of various dependencies on these assets. In this intricate threat landscape, achieving situational awareness becomes crucial, as it plays a pivotal role in the cyber domain, allowing for a comprehensive understanding of the dynamic cyber environment.

Cyber-defence education is crucial in producing competent professionals to combat these evolving threats. With proper education and training, individuals can develop network security, cryptography, ethical hacking, incident response, and secure software development skills. Cybersecurity professionals are essential for protecting sensitive data and critical infrastructure and ensuring the overall stability of the digital economy. One of the challenges in cybersecurity education is the gap between theoretical knowledge and practical skills and the dynamic nature of information infrastructure settings (Arshad et al., 2021). Traditional academic programmes often focus on theoretical concepts, while real-world cyberthreats require hands-on skills and practical experience. Bridging this gap is essential to producing professionals who can effectively mitigate cyberthreats. Hands-on training, simulated environments, and practical exercises are crucial to developing the skills to defend against real cyberattacks.

Cyber ranges are specialised training platforms designed to simulate real-world cybersecurity scenarios. These platforms provide a controlled environment where individuals can practice and improve their cybersecurity skills without the risk of damaging live systems and may also be provisioned as a part of a smart campus (Tian et al., 2018). Cyber ranges allow users to engage in realistic exercises, including defending against simulated cyberattacks, conducting penetration testing, and responding to security incidents. Cyber ranges allow individuals to gain hands-on experience and develop practical skills in a safe and controlled environment. These platforms enable learners to apply their theoretical knowledge, enhance problem-solving abilities, and understand the complexities of managing and securing various systems and networks. Cyber ranges are valuable tools for educational institutions, cybersecurity professionals, and organisations seeking to train and assess their workforce’s capabilities in the ever-changing cyberthreat landscape.

The closed nature of cyber ranges is a crucial attribute that sets them apart from other educational aids. This closed environment offers a controlled and secure space, isolating the simulated cyber activities from external networks. This isolation is critical, as it ensures the confidentiality of often sensitive research and development efforts and creates a risk-free environmental setting for conducting training exercises. In this controlled environment, professionals can deploy and test realistic cybersecurity threat scenarios without fear of unintended consequences or actual harm to operational systems and networks. Cyber ranges are not confined to theoretical simulations; they can actively incorporate genuine attacks and intrusions into their scenarios. This hands-on approach supports cybersecurity professionals and researchers in addressing real-world challenges in a controlled setting, honing their skills and developing effective strategies for countering evolving threats. By offering a risk-free yet authentic environment, cyber ranges become invaluable tools for enhancing theoretical understanding and practical skills to navigate the complex and ever-evolving cyber landscape.

The Defence Advanced Research Projects Agency (DARPA) played a pioneering role in establishing one of the earliest cyber ranges, acknowledging the advancing frontiers of cybersecurity and the critical need for research and development fostered through rigorous testing and experimentation (DARPA, 2024; Karjalainen & Kokkonen, 2020). The National Cyber Range (NCR) initial iteration was a foundational prototype DARPA developed. Over time, researchers have adopted NCR as a paradigm for implementing cyber ranges. This pivotal contribution has spurred the global development of diverse cyber ranges by entities, including government, industry, and universities to research centres and national security organisations. These cyber ranges display a broad spectrum of capabilities, from modest laboratory-based single-server testbed infrastructures to elaborate, virtualised internet-like frameworks. Beyond their training utility, cyber ranges have proven highly effective for research and development endeavours.

Moreover, the Cyber Defence Pooling & Sharing project of the European Defence Agency (EDA) has recognised the necessity for cooperation between national cyber ranges at the European level (European Defence Agency, 2024; Karjalainen & Kokkonen, 2020). This initiative acknowledges the different capabilities of various cyber ranges and the growing demand for simulating the complexity of the cyber domain. Cyber ranges are designed to cater to specific needs and audiences, making their capabilities diverse. They are used in different scenarios, such as training individuals or groups, including employees of companies, organisations, and governments – the heterogeneous nature of cyber ranges results from their purposeful construction (Päijänen et al., 2021). Cyber ranges also serve additional applications from cybersecurity research and development, certifying products or services, conducting competence assessment, and even recruitment activities. The multifaceted role of cyber ranges underscores the necessity to simulate a comprehensive and intricate cyber environment, accounting for unforeseen dependencies and their potential consequences.

9.3 The Role of Cyber Ranges in Cybersecurity Education

A cyber range is a controlled, virtual environment specifically designed for simulating real-world cybersecurity scenarios and exercises, allowing the highest level of cyber-training experience. It serves as a training platform where individuals, including students, cybersecurity professionals, and organisations, can practice and enhance their skills in a secure setting. Cyber ranges mimic various systems, including computer networks, servers, routers, firewalls, and operating systems (such as Windows, Linux, and macOS). These virtual environments can replicate diverse network architectures, from small local area networks (LANs) to complex enterprise-level infrastructures.

In terms of hardware and software components, a cyber range typically consists of powerful servers and network equipment to host the virtualised systems and create realistic network topologies. Virtualisation software, such as VMware or Hyper-V, creates and manages virtual machines representing different devices and systems. Security tools and applications, including firewalls, intrusion detection systems, and vulnerability scanners, are integrated to replicate the functions of real-world cybersecurity solutions (Cooley et al., 2022; VMWare, 2024). Additionally, cyber ranges often incorporate simulation software that generates lifelike cyberthreats and attack scenarios, allowing participants to respond to incidents, conduct penetration testing, and practice defensive strategies. These components provide an immersive and practical learning experience within a controlled digital environment, enabling individuals to develop and refine their cybersecurity skills effectively. Cyber range platforms are highly customisable and scalable and offer a multitude of capabilities that can be utilised in teaching and learning:

Support for and replication of enterprise-grade ICT infrastructures covering various platforms (Windows, Linux), as well as industry control system (ICT)/operational technology (OT) environments, is enhanced by automatic scenario emulators. These emulators can generate benign/legitimate/background network traffic – this enables scenario emulation repeatability, which in turn can be measured and allows for the reliable comparison of teams across various days’ sessions. Additionally, the emulators can execute a preconfigured attack, making red teams optional in the range setup or conducting live injection during simulation sessions.
Off-the-shelf content: simulation technology plus content with increasing levels of difficulty.
Complex cyber-incidents with technical details, in-depth scenario documentation, and orchestration for event management.
Scenario composer for repurposing existing and creating new cyber-incident cases via leveraging a library of commercial systems and applications and collaboratively replicating complex IT/OT infrastructures.
Wearable sensors worn by individuals can provide valuable insight into the pressure of the participants throughout the training sessions.
Instructor feedback: real-time comments, session debriefing with videos of entire simulation sessions, individual and team performance, and decision scoring.

One of the requirements for preparing efficient incident response plans is staff training (Karie & Sikos, 2022). While cyber ranges can be used for conducting complex incident detection and response exercises, the dynamic nature of real-world systems and the adaptability for changing requirements would require task automation in the cybersecurity exercise life cycle (Yamin & Katt, 2022), including automating training content generation (Beuran et al., 2018). This needs to be complemented by the monitoring of training participants. For this reason, Andreolini et al. (2020) proposed a distributed monitoring architecture that gathers relevant information about trainees’ activities during cyber range exercises. This architecture aims to (1) measure the speed at which a user is progressing towards an objective and (2) assess the correctness of the actions taken.

The learners’ roles and target knowledge, skills, and abilities must be covered when using cyber ranges for training. A prime example of this is the Cyber Range Design Framework, proposed by Katsantonis et al. (2023), which adopts both modern and traditional learning theories, facilitates element reuse and allows dynamic assessments. It adapts to the learners’ needs and capabilities and provides real-time feedback.

The cognitive complexity (C) of cyber range event operating environments (O) can be modelled as the weighted arithmetic mean of the quantified properties p1 … nn ∈ P (e.g., variety of traffic types and paths of traffic flow, variety of user behaviours, etc.) as given by Equation 9.1.
