where w1 … n ∈ W represent weights that describe the impact of each property p on the final cognitive complexity measure, whether low, medium, or high (Roque et al., 2019). Note that task performance cannot be used directly to measure this complexity.

Cyber ranges serve various use cases, encompassing security research, testing, development, and certification efforts within the cybersecurity domain (Päijänen et al., 2021). The concept of a development testbed predates the currently known concept of a cyber range, with development teams creating these environments to assess the functionality of their updates in a setting resembling the production environment. Concurrently, research environments strive for authenticity, often employing isolated ICT environments to investigate malware without internet connectivity. In the domain of security education, there is a correlated relationship between competence building and assessment, where certification bodies provide cyber ranges as practical environments for students seeking validation of their skills. This approach positions the cyber range environment as a viable option for assessing students’ evolving competence. The evolution of an individual’s expertise is integral to the broader development of cyber capabilities and resilience, as viewed from an organisational perspective.

Cyber ranges are vital in bridging the gap between theoretical knowledge and practical skills and holistically teaching how to see cyberattacks and defence mechanisms. Traditional education often imparts a theoretical understanding of cybersecurity concepts, but practical application is essential in this rapidly evolving field. In addition, authentic case studies rely on complex networks, potentially with hundreds of participants and LANs (Liljenstam et al., 2005). Cyber ranges provide a platform where individuals can translate theoretical knowledge into hands-on experience, reinforcing their understanding of concepts and techniques. By confronting real-time threats and challenges, learners gain a deeper appreciation for the complexities of cybersecurity, allowing them to bridge the divide between theory and practice effectively. This experiential learning approach ensures that cybersecurity professionals are well-equipped to tackle the dynamic and diverse landscape of cyberthreats in the modern digital age. The main types of cyber ranges used in education include the following:

Simulation ranges: Recreating a synthetic network environment mimicking the behaviour of real network components
Emulation ranges: Running on dedicated network infrastructure, encompassing the mapping of network, server, and storage infrastructures onto physical infrastructure
Overlay ranges: Running on top of real networks, servers, and storage infrastructure
Hybrid ranges: A custom combination of any of the above

Purpose-designed multi-level training platforms have also been proposed, which cover hands-on and theoretical challenges, scenario templates, and capabilities for testing and evaluation, monitoring, recording, and scoring (Subaşu et al., 2017). Educators can utilise cyber ranges within both pre-packaged and ad hoc curricula in the implementation of core as well as advanced incident detection material developed by either professional or educational communities like K-SEC (Yonemura et al., 2022) and individual training of incident detection and response tasks mimicking the functions of a security operation centre. Operations related to ad hoc situations can be used to teach vulnerability assessment and ethical hacking. Tutors can use cyber ranges in alignment with education frameworks, such as the National Initiative for Cybersecurity Education (NICE) (National Institute of Standards and Technology, 2023).

Thrilling live jeopardy-style capture-the-flag (CTF) competitions are suitable not only for extracurricular activities but also in a class environment with students alternating between offensive and defensive roles (Katsantonis et al., 2017). CTFs also allow the creation of virtual machines to be used for formative assessments (Chothia & Novakovic, 2015).

9.4 Enhancing Psychological Readiness and Resilience with Cyber Ranges

A fundamental difference between training and education must be recognised in cybersecurity education (Lunn et al., 2021). Technical skills are paramount in the education of cybersecurity analysts; however, the psychological preparedness of individuals to respond to cyber emergencies is often overlooked (Lunn et al., 2021). Education concerns underlying principles, theories and technical skills required to defend a network. In contrast, training extends education and equips learners with the operational preparedness to respond to cyber emergencies (Lunn et al., 2021). By analogy, commercial pilots must complete in vivo training as a matter of safety, not just classroom education in operating an aircraft (Todd & Thomas, 2013). This competency-based approach could benefit cybersecurity professionals (Somarakis et al., 2019). For example, cybersecurity professionals typically receive a foundational level of education through undergraduate or master’s degrees. However, to develop specialised skills in defending critical infrastructure from types of cyberattacks, they can benefit from training in the simulated environments of cyber ranges. These environments replicate real-world scenarios, such as a port or telecommunications grid, allowing operators to become operationally prepared to tackle specific types of attacks (Somarakis et al., 2019).

Cyber range training also formalises continued professional development (CPD) requirements for cybersecurity operators. Formalised CPD requirements uplift the professional attributes of a profession, ensure operators’ skills are kept up to date, and expand beyond the curriculum of university degrees (Psychology Board of The Australian Health Practitioner Regulation Agency, 2019). For example, clinical psychologists in Australia must fulfil CPD requirements annually to maintain their registration with the Australian Health Practitioner Regulation Agency (AHPRA, 2024). Engaging in regular simulated emergency incidents on cyber ranges would likewise enhance the professional capacity of cybersecurity operators by exposing them to the intensity and pressures of real-world cyberthreats in a safe and controlled environment (Knox et al., 2019).

This exposure is crucial for building the mental resilience of cybersecurity analysts, enabling them to maintain composure better and think critically during emergency incident response. By repeatedly facing simulated threats, participants develop a stress-resistant mindset, enabling them to handle real-world scenarios more effectively (Knox et al., 2019). Indeed, specialised sectors such as maritime cybersecurity have adopted this competency-based approach. For example, CyberMAR (2023) is a federated cyber range that enables security professionals to simulate complex attack–defence scenarios on port infrastructure (Amditis, 2017). The scenarios are immersive experiences reflecting real-world maritime infrastructure demands. These simulations ensure that the professionals using them for training are theoretically knowledgeable and practically adept at handling threats and situations unique to the maritime sector. By focusing on practical skills and real-time decision-making, cyber ranges such as Cyber-MAR can aid in developing a workforce that is well-prepared and resilient against the evolving landscape of highly specialised cyberthreats to maritime infrastructures.

CyberMAR (2023) is one example of a range cybersecurity operators can use to enhance their professional capabilities. However, as cybersecurity expands within the Australian context, there will be an increasing need for cyber ranges that specialise in different classes of the Security of Critical Infrastructure Act (SOCI, 2018). For example, the Register of Critical Assets, defined in the SOCI Act (2018), comprehensively covers various cyber infrastructure classes. This extensive list includes the following:

Communications Networks: Vital for maintaining nationwide and global connectivity
Energy Systems: Essential for power generation and distribution
Financial Systems: The backbone of economic transactions and stability
Healthcare Systems: Critical for patient care and medical data management
Transport Networks: Encompassing both passenger and freight movement
Water and Wastewater Systems: Fundamental for public health and sanitation
Government Databases: Containing sensitive and essential public records
Electoral Systems: Crucial for maintaining democratic processes 9. Public Service Delivery Systems: Key for government service provision
Industrial and Manufacturing Systems: Central to the production and supply chain
Emergency Services: Vital for public safety and crisis response

Additionally, the act specifies various assets within these categories:

Broadcasting Assets (Section 12E)
Domain Name Systems (Section 12KA)
Data Storage or Processing Assets (Section 12F)
Freight Services Assets (Section 12C)
Water and Wastewater Assets (Section 5)
Hospitals (Section 5)
Food and Grocery Assets (Section 12K)
Energy Market Operator Assets (Section 5)
Freight Infrastructure Assets (Section 12B)
Liquid Fuel Assets (Section 12A)
Public Transport Assets (Section 5)
Maritime Ports (Section 11)
Gas Assets (Section 12)
Financial Market Infrastructures and Payment System Assets (Section 12D)

The detailed classification of critical infrastructure under the SOCI Act (Australian Department of Home Affairs, 2018) underscores each category’s unique and complex nature, presenting distinct security challenges. This intricate landscape necessitates the development of an industry of specialised cyber ranges that serve cybersecurity professionals to train effectively in safeguarding these diverse and vital environments (Australian Department of Home Affairs, 2018).

The Australian Government’s 2023–2030 Cybersecurity Strategy (2023) calls for a whole-of-nation effort to uplift Australia into the most cybersecure nation by 2030. Achieving this objective necessitates a concerted, nationwide effort that formalises CPD requirements for cybersecurity professionals, particularly those working with SOCI-registered critical assets. This strategy emphasises the importance of formalising CPD requirements for these professionals, extending beyond their initial educational qualifications. This initiative will require the development of a dedicated training industry of cyber ranges that caters specifically to the needs of the cybersecurity sector. This approach ensures that cybersecurity professionals’ skills are updated regularly, keeps pace with the emerging threats in the virtual landscape, and ensures a baseline level of regulated skill that other professions, such as clinical psychology and aviation, have already adopted. Such continuous skill enhancement is critical for bolstering Australia’s capability to protect and defend its critical cyber infrastructure effectively and meeting the goal of becoming the most cybersecure nation on Earth by 2030.

9.5 Advantages of Incident Response Drills on Cyber Ranges

Cyber ranges offer immersive learning experiences by simulating real-time cyber incidents. Participants can engage with lifelike scenarios, such as malware infections, data breaches, and DDoS attacks, allowing them to respond to these incidents as they would in the real world. This immersive approach provides a hands-on understanding of the complexities of handling actual cyberthreats, enhancing participants’ skills and confidence in dealing with similar situations in their professional roles. The authenticity of the case studies investigated on a cyber range can motivate students, and the experience gained can provide value both in their learning journey during candidature and in their career pathways after graduation. Beyond hyper-realistic attack scenarios, such as ransomware attacks, DoS attacks, SCADA attacks (factory silent attack, overload, or shutdown), or website defacements, students get the opportunity to master the following:

Corporate network environments
Critical infrastructure/SCADA security
Advanced cyberthreats (e.g., advanced persistent threat (APT), privilege escalation, DDoS SYN flood, data exfiltration, man in the middle)
Contemporary cybersecurity toolkits covering next-generation firewalls, IPS, and complex Security Operations Centres (SOCs)/Security Event Information Management Systems (SIEMs)/Security Orchestration, Automation, and Response (SOAR) solutions
Complex skills: incident detection and response (including incident response playbooks), network forensics, reverse engineering
Efficient SOC teamwork, potentially as a part of a capstone unit/applied project

Having a proven record and demonstrable hands-on skills in responding to incidents involving industry systems simulated in cyber ranges may also increase employer demand for graduates. In a cyber range environment, participants must make decisions under pressure, mirroring the high-stakes situations in actual cyber incidents. This fosters the development of critical thinking skills as individuals must analyse rapidly evolving situations, assess risks, and choose effective response strategies. Practising critical decision-making in a controlled setting prepares cybersecurity professionals to handle stressful scenarios with composure and precision, ensuring a more effective response to real cyberthreats. Working in high-pressure environments requires making timely decisions and ensuring the accuracy of the actions taken to achieve the best possible outcome in a given situation. Delayed good decisions, as well as poor or incorrect decisions, can have severe consequences and side effects.

Cybersecurity incidents often require collaboration and effective communication among team members. Cyber ranges facilitate team-based response exercises where participants analyse, strategise, and execute incident response plans. By practising collaborative problem-solving and clear communication, individuals learn how to coordinate efforts, share vital information, and optimise their collective expertise. These teamwork skills are an invaluable asset in a real-world environment, where a meaningful response to cyberthreats often necessitates timely collaboration between different cyber specialists within an organisation. Note that making good decisions in detecting cyberthreats and mitigating cyberattacks does not simply rely on background knowledge and technical skills but also depends on the contextual information available to the response team at that moment, which influences team adaptation (Deline et al., 2021), and on trust, risk behaviour (Granåsen & Andersson, 2016), and cognitive bias (Pfleeger & Caputo, 2013).

Cyber ranges allow organisations to test and refine their incident response plans. By simulating diverse cyber incidents, teams can assess the effectiveness of their existing plans, identify weaknesses, and make necessary improvements. Regular testing in a cyber range environment ensures that incident response plans are up to date, relevant, and well-coordinated, enabling organisations to respond swiftly and efficiently when actual incidents occur.

The dynamic nature of cyberthreats requires cybersecurity professionals to be adaptable and agile. Cyber ranges expose participants to various scenarios, challenging them to adapt their strategies to different types of attacks. By encountering diverse challenges and learning to adjust their responses accordingly, individuals become more versatile and better equipped to handle evolving cyberthreats. Nurturing adaptable and agile cyber defenders through cyber range training ensures they can effectively counteract new and sophisticated cyberthreats as they emerge, safeguarding digital assets and systems with resilience and expertise.

9.6 Parallels to Aviation Training

This section explores the utilisation of flight simulators in training pilots and aviation crew to unveil the benefits of this technology in a highly regulated industry. The focus is on drawing parallels to flight simulator training technologies and techniques that may be adaptable for incorporation into the cyber range domain. Hence, it is proposed that the cybersecurity community consider a focus on the aviation domain to extract valuable insights and knowledge from an industry with both a more extended history and a more highly regulated operational framework yet sharing similarities of purpose. The rewards of such a strategy are manifold, encompassing cost-efficiency, scenario repeatability, domain-specific familiarisation, risk mitigation, and adaptive training, all contributing to a comprehensive and meaningful evaluation of personnel performance and capabilities.

The adoption of flight simulators for civil aviation training and performance evaluation has matured to such a degree that they have become an essential and mandatory device for the effective and efficient training of aircrews (Lofaro & Smith, 2023). The now pervasive practice of using flight simulators within the civil aviation domain results from the appreciation of the heightened aviation skill set derived from such simulators and a convergence of other influences. In its early developmental stage, and continuing into the early 1990s, civil aviation relied upon a pool of experienced ex-military personnel as a source of aviation trainers and advisors. This resulted in the ex-military personnel directly influencing the increased use of the flight simulator in training civil aircrew. This resulted from the familiarity of the ex-military personnel with the use of flight simulators in military aircrew training programmes.

The inception of flight simulators dates back to 1929 when the first rudimentary simulator replicated the cockpit and functionalities of a flight deck. Generic in nature and designed for general aviation only, it quickly developed into a military flight trainer during the Second World War. The military use of flight simulator technology was a primary driver of early flight simulator technology. As aviation technology developed, military aircraft rapidly became more complicated and technology driven. The flow-on effect was that civil air transport began to demand aircraft manufacturers that the aircraft’s flight simulator accompany each new aircraft delivery. As the fidelity of these formative simulators continued to improve, many airlines began to appreciate the benefits of flight simulation technology in terms of both safety and financial return (Allerton, 2000). This development was supported through performance and innovation in computer technology, with the simulator manufacturers able to meet the exacting standards necessary to comply with increasing legislative obligations and to simulate aircraft dynamics more accurately in reflection of that experienced in the cockpit (Allerton & Lammertse, 2023). These advances culminated in several significant trends.

Firstly, as domestic computer performance improved, so did the availability and adoption of commercial off-the-shelf (COTS) flight simulator systems. Previously, simulators were bespoke devices with a price point only available to large and financially well-resourced companies. Secondly, a marked divergence of fidelity between simulator manufacturer products was developed. The subsequent flow-on effect introduced an undesirable disparity in training methods and delivery used in aircrew training. To address this divergence between simulator products, strict regulations were introduced by authorities to mandate simulator quality consistency. There was a particular focus to ensure that simulators more accurately modelled the aircraft they were supposed to simulate and more accurately monitored the skills assessments conducted by airlines to confirm that the simulator training provided met rigorous and objective standards. As a result, by the 1990s, international consensus had been reached such that aviation stakeholders adopted aviation regulations in most countries. For example, today, we have civil aviation flight simulators for the B-757/767 that cost over $35 million and ongoing maintenance and operational costs of thousands of dollars per hour (Lofaro & Smith, 2023).

Through the confluence of contingent vectors, the period from the late 1970s until today has witnessed the evolution of the flight simulator as an indispensable tool for aviation training and certification.

9.6.1 Flight simulator: a comprehensive definition and functional purpose

The essence of a flight simulator can be comprehensively defined by its fundamental purpose, which serves as the cornerstone for its design and application. Firstly, a flight simulator aims to meticulously present simulated information, ensuring that it faithfully replicates the intricacies of a real-world operational system (Allerton & Lammertse, 2023). This involves the recreation of the aircraft’s dynamics, systems, and environmental conditions to an extent that mirrors the challenges and nuances encountered in actual flight. The simulator becomes a dynamic, virtual realm where pilots can interact with simulated aircraft and respond to various scenarios, fostering an environment rich in realism.

Secondly, the flight simulator is designed to accommodate, actively facilitate, and enhance the aircrew’s technical and cognitive skills (Allerton & Lammertse, 2023). This goes beyond mere replication; it involves creating scenarios challenging and refining the skills required for proficient piloting. The simulator is a dynamic training ground where pilots can hone their decision-making abilities, emergency response skills, and overall aeronautical expertise in a controlled yet realistic setting.

Crucially, the overarching goal of these simulator-driven skill enhancements is to catalyse ‘training transfer’. This term encapsulates the seamless transition of acquired skills and knowledge from the simulated environment to the real-world operational context. Through targeted and realistic training scenarios, the flight simulator contributes to developing aircrew with improved functional capability. The skills acquired and refined within the simulated space are intended to directly translate into enhanced performance and adaptability when operating in the complexities of the actual aviation environment.

The flight simulator is a nexus between theoretical knowledge and practical application, functioning as a replication tool and a dynamic training platform. By aligning its purpose with the facilitation of training transfer, the flight simulator becomes an invaluable asset in cultivating aircrew proficiency, ultimately contributing to broader aviation safety goals, efficiency, and pilot competence.

9.7 The Human Factors Challenge in Training Transfer

Understanding the human factors problem in training transfer is crucial to optimise the efficiency and effectiveness of training programmes across all industries, including cybersecurity. The extent of the transfer problem is subject to varying estimates, reflecting the complexity of ensuring that trained individuals can successfully apply their acquired skills in the real-world context. Georgenson (1982) estimated a conservative 10% success rate in achieving behavioural change post-training. In contrast, Saks’ (2002) survey data depicts a more concerning notion, indicating that approximately 40% of students fail to transfer immediately after training. Moreover, this data reveals a progressive decline in transfer success, with 70% faltering after one year and only 50% of training investments resulting in tangible organisational or individual improvements.

These statistics underscore a critical concern: the considerable discrepancy between training funding and its return on investment. This misalignment raises questions about the efficiency of current training methodologies and necessitates a revaluation of human resource training efforts. To address this, interventions must be focused on enhancing the individual, the team, and organisational human resource performance (Yamnill & McLean, 2001). Flight simulators must be crucial in these interventions as a pivotal tool in human factor training.

However, predicting the transfer of training in advance, especially within flight simulation, poses a considerable challenge. It is exceedingly difficult to anticipate how well skills acquired in a simulated environment translate to real-world scenarios. A proposed method to objectively assess the transfer of training involves conducting tests with two distinct groups of trainees. One group exclusively receives airborne training, while the other undergoes flight simulator training. By comparing the competency levels between these two cohorts, it becomes possible to gauge the effectiveness of each training method.

This testing hypothesis rests on the assumption that specific tasks can be measured objectively within each learning environment. Allerton and Ross (1991) emphasise the importance of developing clear and measurable criteria for evaluating competency in airborne and simulator-based training. This approach offers a pragmatic strategy for addressing the human factors problem in training transfer, fostering a more nuanced understanding of how skills acquired in a simulated setting align with real-world proficiency. Ultimately, overcoming the human factors challenges in training transfer requires thoughtful integration of research, assessment methods, and target interventions to enhance the overall impact of training efforts on individual and organisational performance.

9.7.1 Application of training transfer

Training transfer, in the context of skill acquisition and application in real-world settings, involves effectively integrating learned knowledge and abilities into the practical environment. This process necessitates the seamless transition of acquired skills from the training setting to the workplace, where they must be consistently applied and sustained over an extended period. As stated by Baldwin and Ford (1988), for successful training transfer to occur, ‘Learned behaviour must be transferred to the workplace environment and subsequently maintained there over an extended period’ (p. 63).

In the aviation industry, particularly in training pilots, the concept of training transfer is exemplified through flight simulators. These sophisticated tools allow for establishing clear objectives and measurable standards of competency. For instance, achieving proficiency in flying an instrument approach under conditions of low or no visibility is a critical skill for pilots, which traditionally may require 25 hours of actual flight training. This training is time-consuming and can be fraught with risk, especially under adverse weather conditions. Flight simulators offer a safe and controlled environment where pilots can repeatedly practice these approaches, honing their skills without the associated risks of actual flights. A significant portion of this specialised training can be effectively offloaded to a flight simulator to enhance cost-effectiveness and reduce the inherent dangers of authentic flight training. This approach ensures pilots are well-prepared for challenging flight conditions and optimises training resources and safety.

Using flight simulators in pilot aviation training offers many advantages, including skill acquisition, cost-effectiveness, and risk mitigation. By providing a synthetically realistic and controlled environment, flight simulators enable students to develop skills without exposure to the inherent risks associated with actual flight. The immersive nature of flight simulators contributes to enhanced skill retention, allowing pilots to review and then revisit to reinforce their skillsets periodically. This approach aids in mitigating the effects of skill decay over time.

In essence, training transfer facilitated through meaningful simulator training contributes to developing more competent, proficient, and confident pilots. The culmination of these benefits ultimately leads to heightened aviation safety. The ability to effectively transfer and maintain skills acquired in a simulated environment to real-world scenarios underscores the significance of integrating innovative training methodologies in industries where precision, competence, and safety are paramount.

9.8 Cybersecurity and Aviation: Training Commonalities

Educational principles within cybersecurity and aviation domains share commonalities, highlighting the necessity for a comprehensive teaching concept addressing diverse subjects across multiple disciplines (Langner et al., 2022). Like cybersecurity, aviation training aims to enable learners, in this case, aspiring pilots, to realise their full potential through a blend of practical and theoretical education. Both domains employ various teaching methods to effectively prepare learners for the unique challenges within their respective domains. Additionally, the adaptability of teaching resources and materials, as emphasised in the cybersecurity context, aligns seamlessly with the dynamic nature of aviation training.

Formulating a comprehensive teaching concept for the cybersecurity discipline necessitates considering various requisites and factors from educators and students, alongside specific references to individual subjects and disciplines (Langner et al., 2022). The goal is to empower students to attain their full potential by delivering a practical and seamless integrated theoretical education through diverse methods to address cybersecurity challenges effectively. This approach enables educators to integrate and adapt established strategies while expanding their education frameworks of teaching resources, materials, and assignments by acquiring skills and competencies unattainable through conventional methods.

The efficacy of the learning process depends on factors directly and indirectly influenced by both educators and students, shaping the applied teaching methods during the learning process. Critical factors for successful learning in both the cybersecurity and aviation domains were identified (Langner et al., 2022).

9.8.1 Cognitive skills

Cognitive skills form the foundation of teaching in both domains, facilitating knowledge acquisition, information processing, logical thinking, and problem-solving. In cybersecurity, these skills are crucial to learning, decision-making, and attentiveness. In aviation training, cognitive skills are likewise crucial for pilots to acquire and process aeronautical knowledge, make informed decisions, and demonstrate logical thinking during flight operations.

9.8.2 Non-cognitive skills

Soft skills such as communication, cooperation, commitment, self-efficacy, and conscientiousness are essential in both domains. Beyond interpersonal aspects, they contribute to effective leadership and stress management. These skills are crucial for effective crew resource management, communication with air traffic control, and maintaining situational awareness during flights.

9.8.3 Social and compromise skills

The ability to interact and compromise socially is vital in both domains. In cybersecurity, these skills encompass social intelligence, interpersonal characteristics, verbal and non-verbal elements, and social self-regulation. They are essential for understanding complex topics and finding solutions, with adaptable, collaborative spaces addressing the significant educational challenge. In aviation, effective communication between cockpit crew members and ground personnel and the ability to compromise and interact socially is essential for achieving objectives and ensuring safe flight operations.

9.8.4 Learning strategies

Action patterns regulating an individual’s learning categorised as cognitive strategies, repetitive learning, development, and organisational strategies are accommodated in both domains. This empowers individuals to optimise their educational objectives. Like cybersecurity professionals, pilots employ various learning strategies to regulate their learning, reflecting the need for continual development, review, and maintenance of their aeronautical knowledge.

9.8.5 Motivation and expectations

Acknowledging diverse learner motivations and expectations, teaching methods recognise that mismatches between methods and learner preferences can diminish motivation. Explicit course descriptions become pivotal to managing expectations and supporting motivated individuals through self-directed methodologies, fostering a conducive learning environment. These concepts align closely with those of aviation training in maintaining the motivation of aspiring pilots.

The critical significance of practical and theoretical education is evident in cybersecurity and pilot aviation training. In cybersecurity, precritical education involves hands-on experience with security tools, simulated attacks, and real-world scenarios, ensuring learners can effectively apply theoretical knowledge to practical situations. Similarly, in pilot aviation training, aspiring pilots undergo practical flight simulations and hands-on experience in aircraft, translating the theoretical aeronautical principles into real-world flying skills. Integrating theory and practical application enhances understanding and proficiency in both domains.

Furthermore, developing cognitive and non-cognitive skills stands out as a critical priority in both domains. In cybersecurity, cultivating cognitive skills such as critical thinking, problem-solving, and decision-making is imperative for addressing complex cyberthreats. Non-cognitive skills, including effective communication, collaboration, and stress management, are equally crucial. Similarly, in pilot aviation training, cognitive skills are essential for a pilot’s ability to process information rapidly, make split-second decisions, and ensure the aircraft’s and its passengers’ safety. Non-cognitive skills, such as effective communication within the cockpit and with air traffic control, contribute to the overall success and safety of flight operations.

The alignment between teaching concepts and training factors in cybersecurity and pilot aviation training goes beyond surface-level similarities. It reflects a shared recognition of the multidimensional nature of learning, emphasising not only the transfer of knowledge but also the practical application of skills, adaptability to evolving circumstances, and the holistic development of cognitive and non-cognitive abilities. This synergy contributes to the overall effectiveness of educational strategies in preparing individuals for success in the intricate and dynamic domains of cybersecurity and aviation.

9.8.6 Challenges and considerations

One of the challenges in implementing incident response drills on cyber ranges is the presence of resource constraints and specific infrastructure requirements. Setting up and maintaining a cyber range involves significant financial investment in terms of hardware, software, and skilled personnel. Educational institutions and organisations may face limitations in funding and resources, making it difficult to establish and sustain fully equipped cyber ranges. Additionally, ensuring scalability to accommodate a growing number of participants and evolving technologies adds complexity to resource management.

Cyberthreats and cyberattack techniques are constantly evolving, making it essential for incident response drills to stay current and relevant. Attackers continuously develop new methods to exploit vulnerabilities and bypass security measures. Cyber ranges need to adapt by incorporating the latest attack vectors, malware strains, and tactics used by threat actors. This requires regular updates to the simulation scenarios and training modules, ensuring that participants are exposed to realistic and up-to-date challenges. Staying ahead of emerging threats is crucial to preparing cybersecurity professionals for the ever-changing landscape of cyberattacks.

When designing realistic training scenarios on cyber ranges, it is imperative to address ethical considerations. Simulated incidents should not involve illegal or unethical activities, even within a controlled environment. Maintaining a balance between realism and ethical boundaries is crucial to avoid crossing legal or moral lines. Educators and trainers must carefully craft scenarios challenging participants’ skills without encouraging malicious behaviour or actions violating privacy, legal regulations, or ethical norms. Ensuring a robust ethical framework within training scenarios is essential for the credibility and integrity of cyber range-based education programmes.

Measuring the effectiveness of incident response drills conducted on cyber ranges is essential to assess the training’s learning outcomes and overall impact. Traditional indicators include response time, accuracy in identifying and mitigating threats, and adherence to established protocols. However, assessing the participants’ ability to apply learned skills in real-world situations is equally important. Surveys, participant feedback, and performance evaluations can provide qualitative insights into the effectiveness of the training programme. Additionally, tracking participants’ progress over time and correlating their performance in drills with actual incidents, when applicable, can offer a comprehensive understanding of the training’s effectiveness. Regular evaluation and feedback mechanisms are essential for refining training methodologies and ensuring that incident response drills on cyber ranges deliver meaningful educational outcomes.

9.8.7 Future implications and trends

Integrating artificial intelligence (AI) and machine learning (ML) technologies in cyber ranges revolutionises cybersecurity training. AI and ML algorithms can create dynamic and adaptive cyber scenarios based on participants’ actions and responses, offering personalised and challenging learning experiences. These technologies can simulate sophisticated cyberthreats, analyse participants’ behaviour, and provide real-time feedback and guidance. AI-driven cyber ranges enhance the training environment by mimicking evolving attack techniques, enabling learners to practice defending against AI-generated threats, and fostering a deeper understanding of AI and ML in cybersecurity applications.

Gamification techniques, such as point systems, leader boards, and rewards, can significantly enhance engagement and motivation in cyber ranges (Jelo & Helebrandt, 2022). By transforming training exercises into interactive games, participants are more likely to immerse themselves in learning. Moreover, incorporating diverse and creative scenarios, ranging from ransomware attacks to social engineering schemes, ensures learners are exposed to various challenges. Gamification and scenario diversity make training enjoyable and provide a realistic and engaging platform for honing practical skills, encouraging healthy competition, and promoting continuous learning.

Cyber ranges facilitate global collaboration in cyber-defence education, enabling students and professionals from diverse geographical locations to collaborate, share knowledge, and engage in joint training exercises. Virtual collaboration platforms within cyber ranges allow participants to work together in real time, fostering a sense of community and encouraging the exchange of best practices. Collaborative international cyber-range training initiatives enhance cultural understanding, promote information sharing, and create a unified front against global cyberthreats. Such collaborative efforts strengthen nations’ and organisations’ collective cybersecurity capabilities worldwide.

Anticipated advancements in cybersecurity technologies, including quantum computing, blockchain, and secure multi-party computation, are poised to transform the cyber-defence landscape. Quantum computing may break existing encryption methods, prompting the development of quantum-resistant algorithms and cryptographic techniques. Blockchain technology offers tamper-proof and transparent transaction records, revolutionising cybersecurity practices such as secure identity management and supply chain integrity. Secure multi-party computation enables sensitive data analysis without exposing the raw data, ensuring privacy in collaborative cybersecurity efforts.

These advancements, coupled with AI-driven threat detection, real-time response automation, and enhanced user authentication mechanisms, will reshape the way cyber defence is approached. However, they also bring new challenges, requiring cybersecurity professionals to continuously update their skills and adapt to emerging technologies. As the cyber-defence landscape evolves, staying ahead of these advancements is crucial to effectively protect digital assets and maintain the integrity and confidentiality of sensitive information. Education and training programmes, particularly those utilising advanced cyber ranges, will be pivotal in preparing the cybersecurity workforce to leverage these advancements for proactive and robust cyber-defence strategies.

9.9 Conclusion

The pivotal role of cyber ranges in cybersecurity education cannot be overstated, as they blend authenticity (complexity, dynamic nature) and practicality (as a platform providing options for teaching, monitoring/feedback, and assessment) to cultivate a comprehensive learning experience. The authenticity of cyber ranges, marked by their complexity and dynamic nature, mirrors the real-world challenges faced in cybersecurity. Simultaneously, their practicality emerges as a versatile platform offering educators and students a spectrum of teaching options, robust monitoring and feedback mechanisms, and practical assessment tools.

Crucially, the contribution of cyber ranges extends beyond technical proficiency, providing a bridge to the diverse and ever-evolving landscape of cyberthreats. As the idea of cyber-graduate employability requires training on an endless variety of systems not available to educators and students, cyber ranges provide a viable option for memorable hands-on experiences in SOC/SIEM-like environments. The lessons learned during cyber range sessions are invaluable and go far beyond technical skills, covering teamwork in high-pressure environments, insight into psychological traits of insider attacks such as data exfiltration, the diversity of tools typically used in incident detection and response, and more.
